The spectral model of (Real) K-theory
Anupam Datta

Abstract
We use homotopy theoretic ideas to study the K K -theory of (graded, Real) C ∗ C^{*} -algebras in detail. After laying the foundations, and deriving the formal properties, the comparison of the model with the Kasparov picture of K K -theory has been made, and Bott periodicity has been proven using a Dirac-dual Dirac method.



1
Introduction

This work is devoted to understanding the “spectral model of K-theory”, originally due to Nigel Higson and Erik Guentner (
[
13
]
). This model is quite general in its setup; it works with
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-graded
C^{*}
-algebras with a possible Real structure. The spectral K-groups are defined as the homotopy groups of a “spectral K-theory space”. As a consequence, we can use tools from homotopy theory to deduce results about these groups, which help to streamline a lot of arguments which were otherwise quite technical in the classical models of operator K-theory (see for example section 3.2). However, the fact that in the unital and ungraded case, spectral K-theory is naturally isomorphic to the well-known projective module picture of K-theory is not present in the literature with sufficient details. We establish this isomorphism with the necessary details. Also, the proofs presented in
[
13
]
don’t carry over to the Real case. One of the main results of this thesis is to show that the Real spectral model ”classifies” Real K-theory, and can be used to deduce fundamental results about the same.

Here’s a rough idea about the contents of the article: In section 2, we give the necessary background and examples of the objects we study. Then, we cast spectral K-theory as a topologically enriched functor, and deduce some corollaries to this formalism.
In section 3, we dicuss stability and long exact sequences in spectral K-theory. Sections 4,5 and 6 are framed to carefully develop sums and products in the spectral picture. We do most of the work at the space level, which has not been done systematically in the literature. After all this, we finally define the spectral K-theory groups in section 7, and deduce some statements about them from the work done previously. Then in section 8, we prove our main result, deducing that spectral K-theory is isomorphic to the projective module picture of K-theory for unital and ungraded
C^{*}
-algebras. In fact, we show that both these pictures are naturally isomorphic to a third picture, what we call the “Fredholm picture of K-theory”, and it stems primarily from ideas in KK-theory. Finally, in section 9, we develop Bott periodicity in the spectral model, and deduce the 2-fold and 8-fold periodicity of complex and Real K-theory from it.

2
The first definitions, examples, and properties

As mentioned in the introduction, we want to develop a
K
-theory for
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-graded and Real
C^{*}
-algebras. We define these terms in the first part of this section, and give enough examples and counterexamples to motivate them. We will assume familiarity with the theory of (complex, ungraded)
C^{*}
-algebras throughout this article.

2.1
Graded and Real C*-algberas

We begin our considerations by recalling the notion of a
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-grading on a
C^{*}
-algebra.

Definition 2.1
.

A
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-graded complex
C^{*}
-algebra
(A,\varepsilon)
is a complex
C^{*}
-algebra
A
equipped with a
*
-automorphism
\varepsilon
such that
\varepsilon^{2}=1
.
\varepsilon
is called the grading on
A
. The
+1
and
-1
eigenspaces of
A
are called the even and odd elements of
A
respectively.

Example 2.2
.

1.

Every
C^{*}
-algebra can be “trivally graded” by taking the grading operator to be the identity.

2.

Let
A
be a
C^{*}
-algebra, and
u
a self-adjoint unitary in
\mathcal{M}(A)
, the multiplier algebra of
A
. Then,
A
can be “inner graded” by setting
\varepsilon(a)=uau\penalty\ \forall\penalty\ a\in A
.

3.

The
C^{*}
-algebra
C_{0}(\mathbb{R})
can be equipped with a grading given by
f(x)\mapsto f(-x)
. The even and odd parts under this grading precisely correspond to the set of even and odd functions respectively. This is not an inner grading.

4.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-graded Hilbert space, that is, a Hilbert space equipped with a direct sum decomposition
\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{H}_{0}\oplus\mathcal{H}_{1}
. Then,
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})
and
\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H})
become graded
C^{*}
-algebras with the diagonal-off diagonal grading. These gradings are actually inner; they are implemented by the operator
\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&-1\end{pmatrix}:\mathcal{H}_{0}\oplus\mathcal{H}_{1}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}_{0}\oplus\mathcal{H}_{1}
.

5.

The maximal tensor product of two graded
C^{*}
-algebras (denoted by
-\hat{\otimes}-
) can be equipped with a grading; see the references below for the construction.

More examples and elaborations on gradings can be found in
[
13
]
,
[
2
]
, and
[
27
]
. At this point, the reader might have the question
1
1

1

just like the author had, too!
: Why should we care about graded
C^{*}
-algebras if we are interested in only the ungraded ones? One of the reasons is, we have a natural identification between
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})
and the set of odd, self-adjoint operators on
\mathcal{B}(\hat{\mathcal{H}})
, where
\mathcal{H}
is an arbitrary Hilbert space, and
\hat{\mathcal{H}}
denotes the graded Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}\oplus\mathcal{H}
(and similarly for the compact operators). This way, we can apply tools like the functional calculus on these self-adjoint operators, which would otherwise not be available to us in the ungraded world. See Section 8 to see some of this in action!

Next, we move onto Real
C^{*}
-algebras.

Definition 2.3
.

A Real
C^{*}
-algebra
(A,\tau)
is a complex
C^{*}
-algebra
A
equipped with a
\mathbb{C}
-anti-linear
*
-automorphism
\tau
such that
\tau^{2}=1
.
\tau
is called the Real structure on
A
.
2
2

2

Sometimes, the Real structure will simply be denoted by
a\mapsto\bar{a}
.
The real
C^{*}
-algebra associated to
\tau
is
A_{\tau}:=\{a\in A\mid a=\tau(a)\}
.

Example 2.4
.

1.

The complex
C^{*}
-algebra
\mathbb{C}
, equipped with the complex conjugation map is a Real
C^{*}
-algebra. We will denote this Real
C^{*}
-algebra by
\mathbf{C}
in this article. The real
C^{*}
-algebra associated to this is simply
\mathbb{R}
.

2.

Let
X
be a Real compact Hausdorff space, that is, equipped with a homeomorphism
\tau_{X}
such that
\tau_{X}^{2}=1
. Then,
C(X,\mathbb{C})
can be equipped with a Real structure given by
\tau(f)(x)=\overline{f(\tau_{X}(x))}
.

3.

More generally, if
(A,\tau_{A})
is a Real
C^{*}
-algebra and
(X,\tau_{X})
is a Real compact Hausdorff space, then
C(X,A)
can be equipped with a Real structure given by
\tau(f)(x)=\tau_{A}(f(\tau_{X}(x)))
. Note that
C(X,A)_{\tau}=\{f\in C(X,A)\mid\tau_{A}\circ f=f\circ\tau_{X}\}
.

4.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a Real Hilbert space, that is, a complete complex inner product space equipped with a
\mathbb{C}
-antilinear involution
\overline{(.)}
such that
\overline{\langle x,y\rangle}=\langle\overline{x},\overline{y}\rangle
. Then,
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})
and
\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H})
can be equipped with a Real structure given by
\tau(T)(h)=\overline{T(\overline{h})}
.

5.

More generally, if
\mathcal{H}
is a Real Hilbert module over a Real
C^{*}
-algebra
(A,\tau_{A})
, then
\mathcal{B}_{A}(\mathcal{H})
and
\mathcal{K}_{A}(\mathcal{H})
can be equipped with a Real structure given by
\tau(T)h=\overline{T(\overline{h})}
, where
\overline{(.)}
is the
A
-antilinear involution on
\mathcal{H}
satisfying
\tau(\langle x,y\rangle)=\langle\overline{x},\overline{y}\rangle
.

6.

The maximal tensor product of two Real
C^{*}
-algebras can be equipped with a Real structure given by the tensor product of the individual Real structures.

7.

Not every complex
C^{*}
-algebras can be equipped with a Real structure, see for example
[
5
]
.

More examples and elaborations on Real
C^{*}
-algebras can be found in
[
23
]
and
[
21
]
. One might wonder how do real and Real
C^{*}
-algebras compare? To that end, we have the following result:

Theorem 2.5
.

(See
[
10
]
) There is an equivalence between the category of Real
C^{*}
-algebras with Real structure preserving
*
-homomorphisms, and the category of real
C^{*}
-algebras and
*
-homomorphisms. The correspondence goes as follows
3
3

3

the process of assigning a Real
C^{*}
-algebra to a real one as given here is called complexification
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\{\text{Real C*-algebras}\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\{\text{real C*-algebras}\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(A,\tau)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A_{\tau}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(B\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{C},1\otimes\overline{(.)})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}

Thus, while working with Real
C^{*}
-algebras, one has the tools from the complex world available (such as spectral theory), and the results in the Real world can be transferred over to the real world. On top of that, working directly with real
C^{*}
-algebras is quite technical as the very definition has more conditions than in the complex world (see
[
23
]
).

Finally, we consider the situation when our
C^{*}
-algebras have a grading as well as a Real structure. We want these two structures to interact well, as the following definition shows:

Definition 2.6
.

A
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-graded Real
C^{*}
-algebra
(A,\varepsilon,\tau)
is a complex
C^{*}
-algebra
A
equipped with a grading
\varepsilon
and a Real structure
\tau
which commute, that is,
\varepsilon\circ\tau=\tau\circ\varepsilon
.

Example 2.7
.

1.

Every Real
C^{*}
-algebra equipped with the trivial grading becomes a Real,
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-graded
C^{*}
-algebra.

2.

If
(A,\tau_{A})
is a
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-graded Real
C^{*}
-algebra and
(X,\tau_{X})
is a Real compact Hausdorff space, then
C(X,A)
can be equipped with a Real structure given by
\tau(f)(x)=\tau_{A}(f(\tau_{X}(x)))
and a grading given pointwise, thereby making it into a
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-graded Real
C^{*}
-algebra.

3.

The graded maximal tensor product of two
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-graded Real
C^{*}
-algebras can be equipped with a Real structure given by the tensor product of the individual ones. The symbol
-\hat{\otimes}-
will stand for the graded maximal tensor product throughout the article.

4.

C_{0}(\mathbb{R})
becomes a
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-graded, Real
C^{*}
-algebra when equipped with the even-odd grading, and the real structure given by point-wise conjugation. We denote this Real
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-graded
C^{*}
-algebra by
\mathcal{S}
.

5.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-graded, Real Hilbert space. Then,
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})
and
\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H})
become
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-graded, Real
C^{*}
-algebras with the grading and Real structure as discussed in
Example

2.2
and
Example

2.4
.

Remark 1
.

The last two examples will be extremely crucial to us in this article.

Having given all the relevant definitions, we will now focus only in the generality of
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-graded, Real
C^{*}
-algebras unless otherwise mentioned. We
don’t
always mention the adjective “Real” (but sometimes we do when it’s a very important definition / relevant for the proof), it assumed to be there by default. If the reader is only interested in the complex case, they may ignore the Real structure, and the theory goes through. Furthermore, every grading we consider in this article are over
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
; so at times we drop it too.

2.2
Hom sets as spaces

We will be coming up with a homotopy theoretic model of operator
K
-theory, in the sense that the
K
-theory groups of a
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-graded (Real)
C^{*}
-algebra will be defined as the homotopy groups of its “spectral
K
-theory space”. We move towards building this space now, by first coming up with a topology on the space of grading preserving
*
-homomorphisms between two graded
C^{*}
-algebras
A
and
B
.

Definition 2.8
.

Let
A
and
B
be
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-graded (Real)
C^{*}
-algebras. We equip the set of graded (Real)
*
-homomorphisms from
A
to
B
, denoted
\operatorname{Hom}(A,B)
, with the point-norm topology. That is, a net
\varphi_{i}\rightarrow\varphi
in
\operatorname{Hom}(A,B)\iff\varphi_{i}(a)\rightarrow\varphi(a)
in the norm topology in
B
for all
a\in A
.
\operatorname{Hom}(A,B)
is a pointed space, with
\underline{0}
, the zero homomorphism being the basepoint.

Remark 2
.

The point-norm topology is the “correct” topology to put on the hom sets of
C^{*}
-algebras. Indeed, it can be shown quite easily that when
X
and
Y
are compact Hausdorff spaces, then the Gelfand-Naimark duality

\operatorname{Map}_{\penalty\ \text{Top}}(X,Y)\rightarrow\operatorname{Hom}(C(Y),C(X))

upgrades to a homeomorphism of topological spaces when
\operatorname{Map}_{\penalty\ \text{Top}}(X,Y)
is equipped with the compact open topology in this case, and
\operatorname{Hom}(C(Y),C(X))
is equipped with the point-norm topology.
4
4

4

More justifications to the “correctness” of the point-norm topology will be given in this subsection.

Our next goal would be to develop the notion of homotopy between graded
*
-homomorphisms. Before that, we need a few preparatory results concerning mappings into the
\operatorname{Hom}
spaces from an arbitrary compact Hausdorff space. We will be using some properties of the compact open topology in the upcoming results, all of them can be found in
[
12
]
,
[
6
]
.

Remark 3
.

We bring to the attention of the reader a technical point: At this juncture, we have
not
k-ified the point-norm topology on hom sets of
C^{*}
-algebras. As a result, the hom spaces need not be compactly generated. However, no issues arise here, since we are only mapping from a compact Hausdorff space into them.

Proposition 2.9
.

Let
X
be a compact Hausdorff space, and
A
,
B
be
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-graded
C^{*}
-algebras. Then, there is a natural homeomorphism of spaces

\operatorname{Map}(X,\operatorname{Hom}(A,B))\cong\operatorname{Hom}(A,C(X,B)),

where the mapping space is equipped with the compact-open topology.
Furthermore, if
(X,x_{0})
is a pointed space, then the aforementioned homeomorphism restricts to a homeomorphism

\operatorname{Map}_{*}(X,\operatorname{Hom}(A,B))\cong\operatorname{Hom}(A,C_{0}(X,B)),

where
C_{0}(X,B)
is the ideal in
C(X,B)
consisting of functions which vanish at
x_{0}
.

Proof.

We construct two sided natural maps:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Phi:\operatorname{Map}(X,\operatorname{Hom}(A,B))\rightarrow\operatorname{Hom}(A,C(X,B))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Psi:\operatorname{Hom}(A,C(X,B))\rightarrow\operatorname{Map}(X,\operatorname{Hom}(A,B))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f\longmapsto a\mapsto\{x\mapsto f(x)(a)\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\varphi\longmapsto x\mapsto\{a\mapsto\varphi(a)(x)\}}}

Clearly, they are well-defined set maps, and are mutual inverses. It remains to show that they are continuous.

For the continuity of
\Phi

it suffices to show that for each
a\in A
, the map
\operatorname{ev}_{a}\circ\Phi:\operatorname{Map}(X,\operatorname{Hom}(A,B))\rightarrow C(X,B)
is continuous. The rest follows from the commutative diagram below, and since the left map therein, being composition with a single function
\operatorname{Hom}(A,B)\rightarrow A
, is continuous with respect to the compact-open topology:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{Map}(X,\operatorname{Hom}(A,B))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{Hom}(A,C(X,B))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{Map}(X,B)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C(X,B)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{ev}_{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\circ\operatorname{ev}_{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}

For the continuity of
\Psi
, note that since
X
is compact, it suffices to show that the adjoint map
\Psi^{\operatorname{ad}}:X\times\operatorname{Hom}(A,C(X,B))\rightarrow\operatorname{Hom}(A,B)
, is continuous. It suffices to show that for each
a\in A
, the map
\operatorname{ev}_{a}\circ\Psi^{\operatorname{ad}}:X\times\operatorname{Hom}(A,C(X,B))\rightarrow B
is continuous. But this follows from the following commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X\times\operatorname{Hom}(A,C(X,B))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{Hom}(A,B)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X\times C(X,B)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\times\operatorname{ev}_{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(x,f)\mapsto f(x)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi^{\operatorname{ad}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{ev}_{a}}

It is direct to see that the maps restrict as asserted in the pointed case.
∎

Proposition 2.10
.

Let
A,B,C
be
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-graded
C^{*}
-algebras. Then, the composition map

M:\operatorname{Hom}(A,B)\times\operatorname{Hom}(B,C)\rightarrow\operatorname{Hom}(A,C)

is continuous.

Proof.

Let
\varphi_{i}\rightarrow\varphi
and
\psi_{i}\rightarrow\psi
be convergent nets in
\operatorname{Hom}(A,B)
and
\operatorname{Hom}(B,C)
respectively. We have to show that
\psi_{i}\circ\varphi_{i}\rightarrow\psi\circ\varphi
in
\operatorname{Hom}(A,C)
. By the definition of the point-norm topology, it suffices to show that
\psi_{i}\circ\varphi_{i}(a)\rightarrow\psi\circ\varphi(a)\penalty\ \forall\penalty\ a\in A
. Now, for a given
a\in A
, we have

\displaystyle\lVert\psi(\varphi(a))-\psi_{i}(\varphi_{i}(a))\rVert

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\lVert\psi(\varphi(a))-\psi_{i}(\varphi(a))\rVert+\lVert\psi_{i}(\varphi(a))-\psi_{i}(\varphi_{i}(a))\rVert

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\lVert\psi(\varphi(a))-\psi_{i}(\varphi(a))\rVert+\lVert\varphi(a)-\varphi_{i}(a)\rVert\penalty\ \text{(as $*$-homomorphisms are contractive.)}.

Both the summands can be arbitrarily small because of the convergences of
\psi_{i}
and
\varphi_{i}
and the conclusion follows.
∎

Now, we study the notion of homotopy between graded
*
-homomorphisms.

Definition 2.11
.

Let
A
and
B
be
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-graded
C^{*}
-algebras. Two graded
*
-homomorphisms
\varphi,\psi:A\rightarrow B
are said to be homotopic if there exists a path in
\operatorname{Hom}(A,B)
between them.

As an immediate consequence of
Proposition

2.9
and
Proposition

2.10
, we have the following two corollaries:

Corollary 2.12
.

Let
A
and
B
be
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-graded
C^{*}
-algebras. Two graded
*
-homomorphisms
\varphi_{0},\varphi_{1}:A\rightarrow B
are homotopic iff there exists a graded
*
-homomorphism
\Phi:A\rightarrow C([0,1],B)
such that
\operatorname{ev}_{i}\circ\Phi=\varphi_{i}
for
i=0,1
.

Remark 4
.

The maps
\operatorname{ev}_{t}\circ\Phi
(
t\in[0,1]
) as above will often be denoted as
\Phi_{t}
.

Corollary 2.13
.

Let
A,B,C
be graded
C^{*}
-algebras. Then, given any graded
*
-homomorphism
\varphi:A\rightarrow B
, the induced maps
\operatorname{Hom}(C,A)\rightarrow\operatorname{Hom}(C,B)
and
\operatorname{Hom}(B,C)\rightarrow\operatorname{Hom}(A,C)
are continuous. Moreover, homotopic graded
*
-homomorphisms yield based homotopic maps.

2.3
The Spectral K-theory space underlying a Hilbert space

We are now ready to give the definition of the spectral
K
-theory space.

Definition 2.14
.

Let
A
be
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-graded (Real)
C^{*}
-algebra, and
\mathcal{H}
be a graded (Real) Hilbert space. Define
the spectral
K
-theory space of
A
underlying
\mathcal{H}
to be the space
\mathbb{K}(A,\mathcal{H}):=\operatorname{Hom}(\mathcal{S},A\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}))
.

To get a feel of this space, we give examples of elements in it, in the case when
A=\mathbf{C}
and
\mathcal{H}=\hat{l^{2}}
. The example needs some familiarity with unbounded operator theory, and especially the spectral theorem for self-adjoint operators; see
[
22
]
for the relevant background. We denote the space
\mathbb{K}(\mathbf{C},\hat{l^{2}})
by
\mathbb{K}(\mathbf{C})
in what follows (see also
Definition

7.1
).

Lemma 2.15
.

Let
D
an odd, self-adjoint Real operator on
\hat{l^{2}}
with compact resolvents. Then, the functional calculus of
D
,
\Phi_{D}:\mathcal{S}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H})
is an element of
\mathbb{K}(\mathbf{C})
. Moreover, if
P_{0}
denotes the projection onto the kernel of
D
, then
\Phi_{D}
and the graded Real homomorphism
f\mapsto f(0)P_{0}
lie in the same path-component of
\mathbb{K}(\mathbf{C})
.

Proof.

That
\Phi_{D}
is an element of
\mathbb{K}(\mathbf{C})
is immediate since
D
has compact resolvents. We therefore focus only on the second assertion.

As
D
has compact resolvents,
\sigma(D)
is discrete, countable, and consists of eigenvalues of
D
. By the spectral theorem, we can write
D=\sum_{\lambda\in\sigma(D)}\lambda P_{\lambda}
, where
P_{\lambda}
denotes the projection onto the eigenspace of
\lambda
. We then have

f(D)=\sum_{\lambda\in\sigma(D)}f(\lambda)P_{\lambda}

for all bounded measurable functions
f
on
\mathbb{R}
. Consider the map

\displaystyle[0,1]

\displaystyle\rightarrow\mathbb{K}(\mathbf{C})

\displaystyle t

\displaystyle\longmapsto\begin{cases}f&\mapsto f(t^{-1}D)\penalty\ \text{if}\penalty\ t>0\\
f&\mapsto f(0)P_{0}\penalty\ \text{if}\penalty\ t=0\end{cases}

It suffices to show that it is continuous. The continuity at all non-zero points are trivial. To see the continuity at
0
, we calculate, for an arbitrary but fixed
f\in\mathcal{S}
,

\displaystyle\lVert f(s^{-1}D)-f(0)P_{0}\rVert

\displaystyle=\lVert f(s^{-1}D)-f(0)\chi_{0}(D)\rVert

\displaystyle=r(f(s^{-1}D)-f(0)\chi_{0}(D)),\penalty\ \text{where $r$ denotes the spectral radius}

\displaystyle\leq\operatorname{sup}\{\lvert f(s^{-1}\lambda)-f(0)\chi_{0}(\lambda)\rvert\mid\lambda\in\sigma(D)\}\penalty\ \text{as $\sigma(g(D))\subseteq\overline{g(\sigma(D))}$}

\displaystyle=\operatorname{sup}\{\lvert f(s^{-1}\lambda)\rvert\mid\lambda\in\sigma(D),\lambda\neq 0\},

5
5

5

or
0
, if
\sigma(D)=\{0\}
.

which can be made arbitrarily small as
s\rightarrow 0
. The rest follows from the definition of the point-norm topology.
∎

We want to think about spectral
K
-theory space in a categorical way. To that end, we set up some topologically enriched categories:

Definition 2.16
.

Let
\mathfrak{C}^{*}
denote the category of graded (Real)
C^{*}
-algebras with graded
*
-homomorphisms as morphisms. We equip the
\operatorname{Hom}
sets with the k-ification of the point-norm topology, thereby making it into a topologically enriched category.

Remark 5
.

That
\mathfrak{C}^{*}
is indeed topologically enriched follows from
Proposition

2.10
.

Definition 2.17
.

Let
\mathfrak{H}
denote the category of graded separable (Real) Hilbert spaces with even (Real) isometries as morphisms. We equip the
\operatorname{Hom}
sets with the k-ification of the strong-
*
topology, thereby making it into a topologically enriched category.

Remark 6
.

That
\mathfrak{H}
is indeed topologically enriched follows from the basic functional analysis fact that composition of isometries is continuous with respect to the strong-
*
topology.

Remark 7
.

All Hilbert spaces we consider in this article will be separable even if we don’t explicitly mention it.

Remark 8
.

Throughout this article, we will be using the well known fact that the strong, strong-* and
\sigma
-strong-* (also called strict) topologies on the spaces of even isometries (and unitaries) coincide. See
[
2
]
and
[
14
]
for the relevant definitions and properties.

Note that, we have a functor

\mathbb{K}(-,-):\mathfrak{C}^{*}\times\mathfrak{H}\rightarrow\mathfrak{Top}_{*},

given as the composition of the functors

\mathfrak{C}^{*}\times\mathfrak{H}\xrightarrow{1\times\mathcal{K}(-)}\mathfrak{C}^{*}\times\mathfrak{C}^{*}\xrightarrow{-\hat{\otimes}-}\mathfrak{C}^{*}\xrightarrow{\operatorname{Hom}(\mathcal{S},-)}\mathfrak{Top}_{*}.

Remark 9
.

We oftentimes pass only a single argument to
\mathbb{K}(-,-)
. In this case, the other parameter is taken to be the identity by default.

Our main result of this section is that
\mathbb{K}(-,-)
is a topologically enriched functor, after k-ification:

Theorem 2.18
.

The functor

k\mathbb{K}(-,-):\mathfrak{C}^{*}\times\mathfrak{H}\rightarrow\mathfrak{Top}^{CG}_{*}

is a topologically enriched functor.

We build towards the proof of this theorem by proving some lemmas, which are of independent interest, and will be needed later too.

We begin with a general lemma on Banach spaces:

Lemma 2.19
.

Let
X,Y
be Banach spaces, and
Z
a topological space. Then, a map

\displaystyle G:Z

\displaystyle\rightarrow B_{1}(\mathcal{B}(X,Y))

is continuous
\iff
the map

\displaystyle G^{\operatorname{ad}}:Z\times X

\displaystyle\rightarrow Y

\displaystyle(z,x)

\displaystyle\mapsto G(z)x

is continuous, where
B_{1}(\mathcal{B}(X,Y))
denotes the unit ball (in the norm topology) inside the space
\mathcal{B}(X,Y)
of bounded operators between
X
and
Y
equipped with the point-norm topology.

Proof.

\implies
We have to show that if
z_{i}\rightarrow z
in
Z
and
x_{i}\rightarrow x
in
X
, then
G(z_{i})x_{i}\rightarrow G(z)x
in
Y
. We have

\displaystyle\lVert G(z)x-G(z_{i})x_{i}\rVert

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\lVert G(z)x-G(z_{i})x\rVert+\lVert G(z_{i})x-G(z_{i})x_{i}\rVert

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\lVert G(z)x-G(z_{i})x\rVert+\lVert x-x_{i}\rVert

where for the last inequality, we used that
G
mapped into the space of contractive operators. By the continuity of
G
, the first summand can be made arbitrarily small, and this concludes the forward implication.

\impliedby
By definition of the point-norm topology, this immediately follows by the continuity of
G^{\operatorname{ad}}
.
∎

Lemma 2.20
.

Let
\mathcal{H}_{0}
,
\mathcal{H}_{1}
be graded Hilbert spaces. Then, the following map is continuous:

\operatorname{Ad}(-):\operatorname{Iso}^{\operatorname{ev}}(\mathcal{H}_{0},\mathcal{H}_{1})\rightarrow\operatorname{Hom}(\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}_{0}),\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}_{1}))

Proof.

In the light of
Lemma

2.19
, our claim will follow immediately once we show that the following map is continuous:

\displaystyle G:\operatorname{Iso}^{\operatorname{ev}}(\mathcal{H}_{0},\mathcal{H}_{1})\times\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}_{0})\rightarrow\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}_{1})

\displaystyle(\iota,T)\mapsto\operatorname{Ad}(\iota)(T)

Let
\iota_{i}\rightarrow\iota
in
\operatorname{Iso}^{\operatorname{ev}}(\mathcal{H}_{0},\mathcal{H}_{1})
, and
T_{i}\rightarrow T
in
\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H})
. Then, we have

\displaystyle\lVert\iota T\iota^{*}-\iota_{i}T_{i}{\iota_{i}}^{*}\rVert

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\lVert\iota T\iota^{*}-\iota_{i}T{\iota_{i}}^{*}\rVert+\lVert\iota_{i}T{\iota_{i}}^{*}-\iota_{i}T_{i}{\iota_{i}}^{*}\rVert

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\lVert\iota T\iota^{*}-\iota_{i}T{\iota_{i}}^{*}\rVert+\lVert T-T_{i}\rVert

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\lVert\iota T\iota^{*}-\iota_{i}T{\iota}^{*}\rVert+\lVert\iota_{i}T{\iota}^{*}-\iota_{i}T{\iota_{i}}^{*}\rVert+\lVert T-T_{i}\rVert

The rest follows as the strong-
*
and the
\sigma
-strong-
*
topologies on
\operatorname{Iso}^{\operatorname{ev}}(\mathcal{H}_{0},\mathcal{H}_{1})
coincide.
∎

Lemma 2.21
.

Let
A,B,C,D
be
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-graded
C^{*}
-algebras. Then, if
\varphi_{i}\rightarrow\varphi
in
\operatorname{Hom}(A,B)
, and
\psi_{i}\rightarrow\psi
in
\operatorname{Hom}(C,D)
, then
\varphi_{i}\hat{\otimes}\psi_{i}\rightarrow\varphi\hat{\otimes}\psi
in
\operatorname{Hom}(A\hat{\otimes}C,B\hat{\otimes}D)
.

Proof.

We first prove it for simple tensors: Given
a\hat{\otimes}b
with
a\in A,b\in B
, we have

\displaystyle\lVert\varphi(a)\hat{\otimes}\psi(b)-\varphi_{i}(a)\hat{\otimes}\psi_{i}(b)\rVert

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\lVert\varphi(a)\hat{\otimes}\psi(b)-\varphi_{i}(a)\hat{\otimes}\psi(b)\rVert+\lVert\varphi_{i}(a)\hat{\otimes}\psi(b)-\varphi_{i}(a)\hat{\otimes}\psi_{i}(b)\rVert

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lVert(\varphi(a)-\varphi_{i}(a))\hat{\otimes}\psi(b))\rVert+\lVert\varphi_{i}(a)\hat{\otimes}(\psi(b)-\psi_{i}(b))\rVert

which can be made arbitrarily small as
\lVert x\hat{\otimes}y\rVert\leq\lVert x\rVert\lVert y\rVert
and
*
-homomorphisms are contractive.

Now, for a general element
x\in A\hat{\odot}B\subseteq A\hat{\otimes}B
, write
x=\sum_{k=1}^{n}a_{k}\hat{\otimes}b_{k}
. Then, we have

(\varphi_{i}\hat{\otimes}\psi_{i})(\sum_{k=1}^{n}a_{k}\hat{\otimes}b_{k})=\sum_{k=1}^{n}(\varphi_{i}\hat{\otimes}\psi_{i})(a_{k}\hat{\otimes}b_{k})\rightarrow\sum_{k=1}^{n}(\varphi\hat{\otimes}\psi)(a_{k}\hat{\otimes}b_{k})=(\varphi\hat{\otimes}\psi)(\sum_{k=1}^{n}a_{k}\hat{\otimes}b_{k}),

by the analysis done on elementary tensors before.

Finally, for a general
x\in A\hat{\otimes}B
, we can approximate
x
by an element of
y\in A\hat{\odot}B
. Then,

\displaystyle\lVert(\varphi\hat{\otimes}\psi)(x)-(\varphi_{i}\hat{\otimes}\psi_{i})(x)\rVert

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\lVert(\varphi\hat{\otimes}\psi)(x)-(\varphi\hat{\otimes}\psi)(y)\rVert+\lVert(\varphi\hat{\otimes}\psi)(y)-(\varphi_{i}\hat{\otimes}\psi_{i})(y)\rVert+\lVert(\varphi_{i}\hat{\otimes}\psi_{i})(y)-(\varphi_{i}\hat{\otimes}\psi_{i})(x)\rVert

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\lVert x-y\rVert+\lVert(\varphi\hat{\otimes}\psi)(y)-(\varphi_{i}\hat{\otimes}\psi_{i})(y)\rVert+\lVert x-y\rVert,

and the rest follows by what’s shown previously.
∎

(Proof of
Theorem

2.18
).

The most non-trivial part of the assertion is that the map induced between the hom spaces is continuous, i.e., the following map is continuous:

k\operatorname{Hom}(A,B)\times_{\operatorname{CG}}k\operatorname{Iso}^{\operatorname{ev}}(\mathcal{H}_{0},\mathcal{H}_{1})\rightarrow\operatorname{Map}(k\mathbb{K}(A,\mathcal{H}_{0}),k\mathbb{K}(B,\mathcal{H}_{1})).

We only show this here; note that the map can be written as a composite

where the right vertical map is the one adjoint to the map

k\operatorname{Hom}(A\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}_{0}),B\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}_{1}))\times k\mathbb{K}(A,\mathcal{H}_{0})\rightarrow k\mathbb{K}(B,\mathcal{H}_{1}).

In light of the properties of the compact open topology and the k-ification funtor, we are now done: we have already seen that
(1\times\operatorname{Ad}(-))
,
-\hat{\otimes}-
, and compositions of graded
*
-homomorphisms are continuous in
Lemma

2.20
,
Lemma

2.21
and
Proposition

2.10
respectively.
∎

As a consequence of the results developed in this subsection, we have the following:

Corollary 2.22
.

Let
\mathcal{H}_{0},\mathcal{H}_{1}
be graded Hilbert space, and
A,B
be graded
C^{*}
-algebras. Then,

1.

Homotopic
*
-homomorphisms
A\rightarrow B
induce based homotopic maps
\mathbb{K}(A,\mathcal{H}_{0})\rightarrow\mathbb{K}(B,\mathcal{H}_{0})
.

2.

Strongly path-connected even isometries
\mathcal{H}_{0}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}_{1}
induce based homotopic maps
\mathbb{K}(A,\mathcal{H}_{0})\rightarrow\mathbb{K}(A,\mathcal{H}_{1})
.

3.

0:A\rightarrow B
induces the constant map
\mathbb{K}(A,\mathcal{H}_{0})\rightarrow\mathbb{K}(B,\mathcal{H}_{0})
.

3
Two fundamental properties of spectral K-theory

In this subsection, we prove a couple of well known properties of
K
-theory from the classical ungraded picture, namely stability and existence of long exact sequences, in the spectral picture. These will also be needed later when we develop products in K-theory. Some of the ideas here were motivated from
[
8
]
.

Throughout this section,
H
denotes an arbitrary (but fixed) graded (Real) Hilbert space. For the proofs, we need to sometimes work with a somewhat simpler model of the spectral
K
-theory space underlying
H
. This, we do in terms of Cayley transforms, as follows:

Lemma 3.1
.

Let
A
be a
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-graded
C^{*}
-algebra
(A,\varepsilon)
(with a possible Real structure given by
a\mapsto\bar{a}
). Then there is a natural homeomorphism between
\operatorname{Hom}(\mathcal{S},A)
and

\operatorname{Cay}(A):=\{u\in\mathcal{U}(A^{+})\mid u^{*}=\varepsilon(u)(=\bar{u}),u\mapsto 1\penalty\ \text{under the canonical map}\penalty\ A^{+}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}\}

Proof.

By the universal property of adjunction of units, we get
\operatorname{Hom}(\mathcal{S},A)\cong\operatorname{Hom^{\prime}}_{C^{*}}(\mathcal{S}^{+},A^{+})
, the space of all unital
*
-homomorphisms preserving the augmentation. Consider the map

\displaystyle\alpha:\operatorname{Hom^{\prime}}_{C^{*}}(\mathcal{S}^{+},A^{+})

\displaystyle\rightarrow\operatorname{Cay}(A)

\displaystyle\varphi

\displaystyle\mapsto\varphi(\frac{x-i}{x+i})

We claim that this is the required homeomorphism. Indeed, note that
\mathcal{S}^{+}\cong C(\mathbb{R}^{+})\cong C(\mathbb{S}^{1})
, with the later isomorphism being induced from the underlying homeomorphism of spaces
\mathbb{R}^{+}\rightarrow\mathbb{S}^{1}
, given by
x\mapsto\frac{x-i}{x+i}
. The proposition follows once we invoke the fact that
C(\mathbb{S}^{1})
is the universal
C^{*}
-algebra on a single unitary (see
[
25
]
), and take into consideration the induced grading and Real structure on
C(\mathbb{S}^{1})
.
∎

3.1
Stability

For this subsection, we assume that the even and odd parts of
H
are countably infinite dimensional.
Our goal is to show the following:

Theorem 3.2
.

Let
A
be a graded
C^{*}
-algebra, and
\mathcal{H}
a graded Hilbert space. Then, the map
\Phi:A\rightarrow A\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H})
, given by
\Phi(a)=a\hat{\otimes}e_{11}
, where
e_{11}
is projection onto an even one-dimensional subspace of
\mathcal{H}
, induces a homotopy equivalence
\mathbb{K}(\Phi):\mathbb{K}(A,H)\rightarrow\mathbb{K}(A\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}),H)
.

Proof.

Our goal is to show that the induced map on the spectral
K
-theory space
\mathbb{K}(\Phi):\mathbb{K}(A,H)\rightarrow\mathbb{K}(A\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}),H)
is a homotopy equivalence.
First, note that the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\hat{\otimes}\mathbb{C}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H})},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a\mapsto a\hat{\otimes}1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}\hat{\otimes}{\operatorname{Ad}(\iota)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}}

where
\iota:\mathbb{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}
is the even isometry onto the range of
e_{11}
.

Now, note that there is a canonical homeomorphism
\mathbb{K}(A\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}),H)\cong\mathbb{K}(A,\mathcal{H}\hat{\otimes}H)
, and under this identification,
\mathbb{K}(\Phi)
corresponds to the map
\mathbb{K}(\operatorname{Ad}j):\mathbb{K}(A,H)\rightarrow\mathbb{K}(A,\mathcal{H}\hat{\otimes}H)
, where
j:H=\mathbb{C}\hat{\otimes}H\rightarrow\mathcal{H}\hat{\otimes}H
is given by
\iota\hat{\otimes}1
. But,
H
and
\mathcal{H}\hat{\otimes}H
are both graded Hilbert spaces whose even and odd grading degree parts are countably infinite dimensional, and hence there exists an even unitary isomorphism
u
between them. Then,
\mathbb{K}(\operatorname{Ad}u):\mathbb{K}(A,H)\rightarrow\mathbb{K}(A,\mathcal{H}\hat{\otimes}H)
is a homeomorphism. As
\operatorname{Iso}^{\operatorname{ev}}(H,\mathcal{H}\hat{\otimes}H)
is path-connected in the strong-
*
topology, our result follows.
∎

3.2
Long exact sequence

Theorem 3.3
.

Let
A,B
be
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-graded (Real)
C^{*}
-algebras, and
\varphi:A\rightarrow B
be a surjective homomorphism. Let
J:=\ker\varphi
. Then,
\mathbb{K}(A,H)\rightarrow\mathbb{K}(B,H)
is a Serre fibration, with fiber
\mathbb{K}(J)
.

The following proposition contains the technical heart of the proof:

Proposition 3.4
.

Let
\sigma:A\rightarrow B
be a surjective homomorphism of
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-graded (possibly Real)
C^{*}
-algebras. Then the induced map
\sigma_{*}:\operatorname{Hom}(\mathcal{S},A)\rightarrow\operatorname{Hom}(\mathcal{S},B)
is a Serre fibration.

Proof.

We work in the general case when
(A,\varepsilon,\overline{(.)})
is
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-graded and Real. We have to show that for any compact CW complex
X
, the following diagram has a solution:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{Hom}(\mathcal{S},A)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X\times[0,1]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{Hom}(\mathcal{S},B)}}

Now, in light of the homeomorphisms discussed in
Proposition

2.9
, we conclude that a solution in the above diagram is equivalent to a solution in the following diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\{0\}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{Hom}(\mathcal{S},C(X,A))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[0,1]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{Hom}(\mathcal{S},C(X,B))}}

Furthermore, the induced map
C(X,A)\rightarrow C(X,B)
is also surjective as a consequence of the Bartle-Graves selection theorem
[
1
]
. So, without loss of generality, it suffices to show that when
\sigma:A\rightarrow B
is a surjective homomorphism of
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-graded Real
C^{*}
-algebras, then
\sigma_{*}:\operatorname{Hom}(\mathcal{S},A)\rightarrow\operatorname{Hom}(\mathcal{S},B)
has the path lifting property.

In light of
Lemma

3.1
, it suffices to show that the induced map
\operatorname{Cay}(A)\rightarrow\operatorname{Cay}(B)
has the path-lifting property.
For the remainder of the proof, we need some more notation. Set

A_{0}:=\{a\in A\mid a^{*}=\varepsilon(a)=\bar{a}\}.

Similarly, we define
B_{0}
. Note that
A_{0}
and
B_{0}
are real Banach spaces. A modification of the proof that a surjective homomorphism of
C^{*}
-algebras restricts to a surjective linear map between their self-adjoint elements yields that
\sigma:A_{0}\rightarrow B_{0}
is surjective
6
6

6

more precisely, the grading, Real structure and
*
-operations define an irreducible
\mathbb{Z}/2^{3}\mathbb{Z}
-action on the spaces involved, and taking its isotypical parts is an exact functor.
. Note that

\operatorname{Cay}(A)=\{u\in\mathcal{U}(A^{+})\mid u-1\in A_{0}\},\operatorname{Cay}(B)=\{u\in\mathcal{U}(B^{+})\mid u-1\in B_{0}\}.

Let
p:[0,1]\rightarrow\operatorname{Cay}(B)
be a path with a specified starting point
p(0)=\sigma(u_{0})
for some
u_{0}\in\operatorname{Cay}(A)
. By the Bartle-Graves selection theorem, there exists a continuous
s:B_{0}\rightarrow A_{0}
with
s(0)=0
. Thus, there exists
\delta>0
such that
||x||<\delta\implies||s(x)||<\frac{1}{2}
. By compactness of
[0,1]
,
\penalty\ \exists\penalty\ N\in\mathbb{N}
such that
|s-t|\leq\frac{1}{N}

\implies||p(s)-p(t)||<\delta
. We will build our lift
\tilde{p}:[0,1]\rightarrow\operatorname{Cay}(A)
inductively. The lift at
0
is given to be
u_{0}
, and suppose, by induction, that
\tilde{p}
has been constructed on
[0,\frac{i}{N}]
for some
0\leq i<N
. Set

p^{\prime}(t):=\tilde{p}(\frac{i}{N})+s(p(t)-p(\frac{i}{N}))\penalty\ \forall\penalty\ \frac{i}{N}\leq t\leq\frac{i+1}{N}.

From the well-known “
1+x
” lemma on invertibles in a Banach algebra, and since
p^{\prime}(\frac{i}{N})
is a unitary, we conclude that
p^{\prime}(t)\in\{a\in\operatorname{GL}(A^{+})\mid a-1\in A_{0}\},\penalty\ \forall\penalty\ \frac{i}{N}<t\leq\frac{i+1}{N}
.

We have a well known strong deformation retraction from
\operatorname{GL}(A^{+})
onto
\mathcal{U}(A^{+})
as follows:

\displaystyle H:

\displaystyle\operatorname{GL}(A^{+})\times[0,1]\rightarrow\operatorname{GL}(A^{+})

\displaystyle(a,t)\mapsto t.a\sqrt{a^{*}a}^{-1}+(1-t).a

We first show that
H
preserves the space
A_{00}:=\{a\in\operatorname{GL}(A^{+})\mid a-1\in A_{0}\}
. To that end, the following claim is made:

Claim:

a\in A_{00}\implies a\sqrt{a^{*}a}^{-1}\in A_{00}
.

Proof.

As
a-1\in A_{0}
, we have
a^{*}=\varepsilon(a)=\bar{a}
. A direct calculation (we need to use the fact that the adjoint, grading and Real structure commutes with the continuous functional calculus of the positive element
a^{*}a
) yields that

(a\sqrt{a^{*}a}^{-1})^{*}=\sqrt{a^{*}a}^{-1}a^{*},\penalty\ \varepsilon(a\sqrt{a^{*}a}^{-1})=\overline{a\sqrt{a^{*}a}^{-1}}=a^{*}\sqrt{aa^{*}}^{-1}.

We claim that for any invertible
b
in a unital
C^{*}
-algebra,
\sqrt{b^{*}b}^{-1}b^{*}=b^{*}\sqrt{bb^{*}}^{-1}
. To see this, first note that
\sigma(bb^{*})=\sigma(b^{*}b)
. This is because
\sigma(xy)\cup\{0\}=\sigma(yx)\cup\{0\}
in general, and
b
is given to be invertible. Thus, by the real Stone-Weierstrass theorem, there is a sequence
\{p_{n}\}
of polynomials with real coefficients which converge uniformly to
\frac{1}{\sqrt{x}}
on
\sigma(bb^{*})=\sigma(b^{*}b)
. Thus,
b^{*}p_{n}(bb^{*})
and
p_{n}(b^{*}b)b^{*}
converges in norm to
b^{*}\sqrt{bb^{*}}^{-1}
and
\sqrt{b^{*}b}^{-1}b^{*}
respectively. But,
b^{*}p_{n}(bb^{*})=p_{n}(b^{*}b)b^{*}\penalty\ \forall\penalty\ n\in\mathbb{N}
, as
b^{*}(bb^{*})^{m}
and
(b^{*}b)^{m}b^{*}\penalty\ \forall\penalty\ m\in\mathbb{N}
.

It also remains to note that
a\sqrt{a^{*}a}^{-1}\mapsto 1
under the
*
-homomorphism
A^{+}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
. But this is clear.
∎

Thus, as the space
\{a\in A^{+}\mid a-1\in A_{0}\}
is closed under convex combinations, we get a well-defined continuous map

\displaystyle H:

\displaystyle A_{00}\times[0,1]\rightarrow A_{00}

\displaystyle(a,t)\mapsto t.a\sqrt{a^{*}a}^{-1}+(1-t).a

Now, we can set

\tilde{p}(t)=H_{1}(p^{\prime}(t))\penalty\ \forall\penalty\ t\in[\frac{i}{N},\frac{i+1}{N}].

Thus,
\tilde{p}
is defined on
[0,\frac{i+1}{N}]
, and we now build our desired lift of
p
by induction.
∎

Proof (of
Theorem

3.3
).

Forgetting the grading for a bit, we note that the short exact sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}

yields the short exact

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{J\otimes\mathcal{K}(H)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\otimes\mathcal{K}(H)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B\otimes\mathcal{K}(H)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0,}

(1)

because of the exactness properties of the maximal tensor product in the ungraded world (see
[
3
]
, Proposition 3.7.1). Now, the underlying Banach spaces of graded and ungraded maximal tensor products are the same (Lemma E.2.18 of
[
27
]
), and so we have the exact sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{J\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{K}(H)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{K}(H)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{K}(H)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}

In particular, we get
A\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{K}(H)\rightarrow B\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{K}(H)
is a surjective homomorphism of graded
C^{*}
-algebras with kernel
\mathbb{K}(J,H)
. The proof follows.
∎

4
Two important graded Real *-homomorphisms

In this section, we discuss two graded
*
-homomorphisms involving
\mathcal{S}
. These will give birth to a plethora of sums and products between spectral
K
-theory spaces which we will see in the subsequent sections.

4.1
The diagonal map

This subsection will be rather small, as the construction is straight-forward. The homomorphism we are considering in this section is the diagonal
*
-homomorphism
\operatorname{dia}:\mathcal{S}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}\oplus\mathcal{S}
, given by
\operatorname{dia}(f)=(f,f)\penalty\ \forall\penalty\ f\in\mathcal{S}
. Recall that, the notation is a bit misleading since
\oplus
is not a categorical direct sum, but in fact a categorical direct product. However, this is the notation used in
C^{*}
-algebra texts (see
[
18
]
), and we stick to this notation.

We conclude this subsection by stating the following property of the diagonal
*
-homomorphism, the proof of which is obvious.

Lemma 4.1
.

The following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{S}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{S}\oplus\mathcal{S}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{S}\oplus\mathcal{S}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{S}\oplus\mathcal{S}\oplus\mathcal{S}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{dia}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{dia}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{dia}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\oplus\operatorname{dia}}

4.2
The codiagonal map

We now come up with a codiagonal/comultiplication map
\mathcal{S}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{S}
. The construction of the comultiplication map is much more intricate than the diagonal map, so we develop it slowly.

First, we need to understand the
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-graded Real
C^{*}
-algebra
\mathcal{S}\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{S}
better. We follow the treatment as in
[
28
]
.

Lemma 4.2
.

\mathcal{S}\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{S}
is isomorphic as a graded, Real
C^{*}
-algebra to the following:

Consider the reflection automorphisms on
C_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
given by
\alpha_{x},\alpha_{y}
as follows:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\alpha_{x}:C_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\rightarrow C_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\alpha_{y}:C_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\rightarrow C_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f\mapsto((x,y)\mapsto f(-x,y))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f\mapsto((x,y)\mapsto f(x,-y))}}

Define

C_{0}^{(i,j)}(\mathbb{R}^{2})=\{f\in C_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\mid\alpha_{x}(f)=(-1)^{i}f\penalty\ \text{and}\penalty\ \alpha_{y}(f)=(-1)^{j}f\}.

The Banach space
C_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
then becomes a
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-graded
C^{*}
-algebra, by taking the grading operator to be
\alpha_{x}\circ\alpha_{y}
, and product and
*
-operation by setting

f\widehat{\bullet}g:=(-1)^{jk}f.g,\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ f^{*}=(-1)^{ij}\overline{f}\penalty\ \text{for}\penalty\ f\in C_{0}^{(i,j)}(\mathbb{R}^{2}),g\in C_{0}^{(k,l)}(\mathbb{R}^{2})

and extended linearly. The Real structure is pointwise conjugation.

Proof.

That the prescribed operations indeed make
C_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
into a
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-graded Real
C^{*}
-algebra is routine; for example, to see that
\alpha_{x}\circ\alpha_{y}
is indeed a grading, one needs to note that
\alpha_{x}
and
\alpha_{y}
are commuting involutions on
C_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
.

Note that, from
[
27
]
Lemma E.2.18, we have that the map

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{S}

\displaystyle\rightarrow C_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{2})

\displaystyle f\hat{\otimes}g

\displaystyle\mapsto((x,y)\mapsto f(x)g(y))

is a Banach space isomorphism. We claim that this map becomes a Real, graded
*
-homomorphism with the prescribed operations on
C_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, which is again routine.
∎

Remark 10
.

We emphasize one aspect about the grading on (
C_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
,
\widehat{\bullet}
): since
\alpha_{x}
and
\alpha_{y}
are commuting involutions, there is a direct sum decomposition

C_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{2})=C_{0}^{(0,0)}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\oplus C_{0}^{(1,1)}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\oplus C_{0}^{(1,0)}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\oplus C_{0}^{(0,1)}(\mathbb{R}^{2}).

The grading
\alpha_{x}\circ\alpha_{y}
on
C_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
is given by considering the first two summands as the even part, and the last two summands as the odd part.

Proposition 4.3
.

There is a unique graded
*
-homomorphism from
\mathcal{S}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{S}
such that
u\mapsto u\hat{\otimes}u
,
v\mapsto u\hat{\otimes}v+v\hat{\otimes}u
, where
u:=e^{-x^{2}}
,
v=xe^{-x^{2}}
.

Proof.

The uniqueness of such a homomorphism, if it exists, is clear by the Stone-Weierstrass theorem, as the algebra generated by
u
and
v
is dense in
\mathcal{S}
. So, we just need to show existence.

For the purpose of existence, we take the description of
\mathcal{S}\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{S}
as in
Lemma

4.2
. Let
r:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
be the Euclidean norm, that is,
r(x,y)=\sqrt{x^{2}+y^{2}}
. Define the functions
\xi_{x}
and
\xi_{y}
given by
\xi_{x}(x,y)=\frac{x}{r(x,y)}
and
\xi_{y}=\frac{y}{r(x,y)}\penalty\ \forall\penalty\ x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\{(0,0)\}
, and
\xi_{x}(0,0)=\xi_{y}(0,0)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}
.
Note that
\xi_{x}
and
\xi_{y}
are globally bounded, and is continuous everywhere except at

(0,0)
. Furthermore, the pointwise products
\xi_{x}\cdot f,\xi_{y}\cdot f\in\mathcal{C}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
for every
f\in\mathcal{C}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
vanishing at
(0,0)
. We define
linear maps,

\displaystyle\widetilde{\triangle}^{(0)}:\mathcal{S}^{(0)}

\displaystyle\to\mathcal{C}_{0}^{(0,0)}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\subset\mathcal{C}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{2}),

\displaystyle f

\displaystyle\mapsto f\circ r,

(2)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\triangle}^{(1)}:\mathcal{S}^{(1)}

\displaystyle\to\mathcal{C}_{0}^{(1,0)}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\oplus\mathcal{C}_{0}^{(0,1)}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\subset\mathcal{C}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{2}),

\displaystyle f

\displaystyle\mapsto\xi_{x}\cdot(f\circ r)+\xi_{y}\cdot(f\circ r).

(3)

and let
\widetilde{\triangle}:=\widetilde{\triangle}^{(0)}\oplus\widetilde{\triangle}^{(1)}:\mathcal{S}\to\mathcal{C}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
.

Claim
.

\widetilde{\triangle}
is a graded
*
-homomorphism
\mathcal{S}\rightarrow(\mathcal{C}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{2}),\hat{\bullet})
, which satisfies

\widetilde{\triangle}(u)=e^{-x^{2}-y^{2}},\qquad\widetilde{\triangle}(v)=(x+y)e^{-x^{2}-y^{2}}.

(4)

Proof.

Additivity of
\widetilde{\triangle}
is clear from definitition, and so is the fact that it is graded. Multiplicativity of
\widetilde{\triangle}
can be checked only on even and odd functions in
\mathcal{S}
; here, we only show multiplicativity when
f,g\in\mathcal{S}^{(1)}
. Indeed, we have

\displaystyle\widetilde{\triangle}(f)\hat{\bullet}\widetilde{\triangle}(g)

\displaystyle=\widetilde{\triangle}^{(1)}(f)\hat{\bullet}\widetilde{\triangle}^{(1)}(g)

\displaystyle=(\xi_{x}\cdot(f\circ r)+\xi_{y}\cdot(f\circ r))\hat{\bullet}(\xi_{x}\cdot(g\circ r)+\xi_{y}\cdot(g\circ r))

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\xi_{x}^{2}.(fg\circ r)+\xi_{x}\xi_{y}.(fg\circ r)-\xi_{y}\xi_{x}.(fg\circ r)+\xi_{y}^{2}.(fg\circ r)&\text{on}\penalty\ \mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\{(0,0)\}\\
0&\text{at}\penalty\ (0,0).\end{cases}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\xi_{x}^{2}.(fg\circ r)+\xi_{y}^{2}.(fg\circ r)&\text{on}\penalty\ \mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\{(0,0)\}\\
0&\text{at}\penalty\ (0,0).\\
\end{cases}

\displaystyle=fg\circ r

\displaystyle=\widetilde{\triangle}(fg)

Similarly, it can be shown that
\widetilde{\triangle}
preserves adjoints, and so its a graded
*
-homomorphism.

The values of
\widetilde{\triangle}
at
u
and
v
are clear from the definitions.
∎

Thus, we conclude that
\triangle:=\Phi^{-1}\circ\widetilde{\triangle}
is a graded
*
-homomorphism
\mathcal{S}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{S}
such that
u\mapsto u\hat{\otimes}u
, and
v\mapsto u\hat{\otimes}v+v\hat{\otimes}u
, as is clear by the definition of
\Phi
.
∎

We also have a graded
*
-homomorphism
\eta:\mathcal{S}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
, given by
\eta(f)=f(0)
.

Corollary 4.4
.

The
*
-homomorphisms
\eta
and
\triangle
equip
\mathcal{S}
with a coalgebra structure; in the sense that the following diagrams commute

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{S}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{S}\widehat{\otimes}\mathcal{S}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{S}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{S}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{S}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{S}\widehat{\otimes}\mathcal{S}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{S}\widehat{\otimes}\mathcal{S}\widehat{\otimes}\mathcal{S}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{S}\widehat{\otimes}\mathcal{S}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta\widehat{\otimes}1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\widehat{\otimes}\Delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta\widehat{\otimes}1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\widehat{\otimes}\eta}

Proof.

This is immediate by considering the elements
u
and
v
in
\mathcal{S}
.
∎

5
Addition and H-space structures

In this section, we develop the notion of “sum” of two elements in a spectral
K
-theory space of a graded
C^{*}
-algebra underlying (potentially different) graded Hilbert spaces.

Throughout this section,
\mathcal{H},\mathcal{H}_{0},\mathcal{H}_{1},...
will denote graded Hilbert spaces, and
A,B,C,...
will denote
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-graded
C^{*}
-algebras.

5.1
Sums and naturality

Definition 5.1
.

We have a continuous map

s:\mathbb{K}(A,\mathcal{H}_{0})\times\mathbb{K}(A,\mathcal{H}_{1})\rightarrow\mathbb{K}(A,\mathcal{H}_{0}\oplus\mathcal{H}_{1}),

which is natural with respect to
*
-homomorphisms
A\rightarrow A^{\prime}
and even isometries
\mathcal{H}_{0}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}_{0}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{H}_{1}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\prime}
. On an element
(\varphi,\psi)\in\mathbb{K}(A,\mathcal{H}_{0})\times\mathbb{K}(A,\mathcal{H}_{1})
, it yields the composite

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{S}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{S}\oplus\mathcal{S}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}_{0})\oplus A\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}_{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\hat{\otimes}(\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}_{0})\oplus\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}_{1}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}_{0}\oplus\mathcal{H}_{1})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi\oplus\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{dia}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\hat{\otimes}\operatorname{inc}}

(5)

where the isomorphism in the middle is inverse to the natural iso
a\hat{\otimes}(b\oplus c)\mapsto a\hat{\otimes}b\oplus a\hat{\otimes}c
. We call the element
s(\varphi,\psi)
as the sum of
\varphi
and
\psi
.

Note that the continuity of
s
is immediate from the definition of the point-norm topology and
Corollary

2.13
. The naturality is immediate from
Eq.

5
.

Remark 11
.

For our convenience, we will also call the map
s
as “add”/“sum” at some junctures.

Remark 12
.

Note that the naturality assertion in
Definition

5.1
really means that the following diagram commutes strictly, where
\varphi:A\rightarrow A^{\prime}
is a graded
*
-homomorphism, and
\iota_{0}:\mathcal{H}_{0}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}_{0}^{\prime}
,
\iota_{1}:\mathcal{H}_{1}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\prime}
are even isometries.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{K}(A,\mathcal{H}_{0})\times\mathbb{K}(A,\mathcal{H}_{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{K}(A^{\prime},\mathcal{H}_{0}^{\prime})\times\mathbb{K}(A,\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{K}(A,\mathcal{H}_{0}\oplus\mathcal{H}_{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{K}(A^{\prime},\mathcal{H}_{0}^{\prime}\oplus\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\prime})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{add}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{add}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{K}(\varphi,\iota_{0})\times\mathbb{K}(\varphi,\iota_{1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{K}(\varphi,\iota_{0}\oplus\iota_{1})}

We draw this diagram here for future reference.

Proposition 5.2
.

Sums are associative and commutative, in the sense that the following diagrams commute strictly:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{K}(A,\mathcal{H}_{0})\times\mathbb{K}(A,\mathcal{H}_{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{K}(A,\mathcal{H}_{1})\times\mathbb{K}(A,\mathcal{H}_{0})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{K}(A,\mathcal{H}_{0}\oplus\mathcal{H}_{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{K}(A,\mathcal{H}_{1}\oplus\mathcal{H}_{0})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{K}(A,\mathcal{H}_{0})\times\mathbb{K}(A,\mathcal{H}_{1})\times\mathbb{K}(A,\mathcal{H}_{2})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{K}(A,\mathcal{H}_{0}\oplus\mathcal{H}_{1})\times\mathbb{K}(A,\mathcal{H}_{2})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{K}(A,\mathcal{H}_{0})\times\mathbb{K}(A,\mathcal{H}_{1}\oplus\mathcal{H}_{2})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{K}(A,\mathcal{H}_{0}\oplus\mathcal{H}_{1}\oplus\mathcal{H}_{2})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{flip}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{add}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\hat{\otimes}\operatorname{Ad}((x,y)\mapsto(y,x))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{add}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{add}\times 1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\times\operatorname{add}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{add}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{add}}

Proof.

We only give the argument for commutativity; the argument for associativity is more straight-forward.

Checking the strict commutativity of the space level diagram amounts to checking commutativity of the following diagram at the algebra level, where
\varphi\in\mathbb{K}(A,\mathcal{H}_{0}),\psi\in\mathbb{K}(A,\mathcal{H}_{1})
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{S}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{S}\oplus\mathcal{S}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{S}\oplus\mathcal{S}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}_{0})\oplus A\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}_{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}_{1})\oplus A\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}_{0})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\hat{\otimes}(\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}_{0})\oplus\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}_{1}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\hat{\otimes}(\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}_{1})\oplus\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}_{0}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}_{0}\oplus\mathcal{H}_{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}_{1}\oplus\mathcal{H}_{0})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi\oplus\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\hat{\otimes}\operatorname{inc}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f,g)\rightarrow(g,f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi\oplus\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\hat{\otimes}\operatorname{inc}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\hat{\otimes}\operatorname{Ad}(\operatorname{flip}\mathcal{H}_{0}\penalty\ \&\penalty\ \mathcal{H}_{1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{dia}}

It does commute, as can be checked by taking
(f,g)\in\mathcal{S}\oplus\mathcal{S}
and checking its fate along the two itineraries.
∎

For our purposes later, we need to discuss functoriality of sums not just with respect to the
*
-homomorphisms as discussed in
Definition

5.1
, but also for “amplified” versions of them. First, we look at the following definition:

Definition 5.3
.

1.

Let
\kappa:\mathcal{S}\hat{\otimes}A\rightarrow B\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H})
be a graded
*
-homomorphism, and
\iota:\mathcal{H}_{0}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}_{0}^{\prime}
an even isometry. Then, it induces a map
(\kappa,\iota)_{*}:\mathbb{K}(A,\mathcal{H}_{0})\rightarrow\mathbb{K}(B,\mathcal{H}_{0}^{\prime}\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{H})
by the following prescription:
Given
\varphi_{A}\in\mathbb{K}(A,\mathcal{H}_{0})
, the map
(\kappa,\iota)_{*}
is given by the composite:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{S}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{S}\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{S}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{S}\hat{\otimes}A\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}_{0})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H})\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}_{0}^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}_{0}^{\prime}\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{H})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}_{0}^{\prime})\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\triangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\hat{\otimes}\varphi_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa\hat{\otimes}\operatorname{Ad}(\iota)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{flip}\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H})\penalty\ \&\penalty\ \mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}_{0}^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\hat{\otimes}\Psi}

(6)

where
\Psi:\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}_{0}^{\prime})\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H})\rightarrow\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}_{0}^{\prime}\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{H})
is the natural isomorphism.
Note that
\varphi_{A}
is clearly continuous from
Corollary

2.13
and
Lemma

2.21
. Furthermore, if
\kappa\sim\kappa^{\prime}
and
\iota\sim\iota^{\prime}
, then
(\kappa,\iota)_{*}\sim(\kappa^{\prime},\iota^{\prime})_{*}
from the second part of
Corollary

2.13
.

2.

Let
\kappa:A\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{S}\hat{\otimes}B\rightarrow C\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H})
be a graded
*
-homomorphism, and
\iota:\mathcal{H}_{0}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}_{0}^{\prime}
an even isometry. Then, it induces a map
(\kappa,\iota)_{*}:\mathbb{K}(A\hat{\otimes}B,\mathcal{H}_{0})\rightarrow\mathbb{K}(C,\mathcal{H}_{0}^{\prime}\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{H})
by the following prescription: Given
\varphi\in\mathbb{K}(A\hat{\otimes}B,\mathcal{H}_{0})
, the map
(\kappa,\iota)_{*}
is given by the composite

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{S}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{S}\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{S}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{S}\hat{\otimes}A\hat{\otimes}B\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}_{0})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{S}\hat{\otimes}B\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}_{0})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}_{0}^{\prime}\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{H})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}_{0}^{\prime})\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H})\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}_{0}^{\prime})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\triangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\hat{\otimes}\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{flip}A\penalty\ \&\penalty\ \mathcal{S}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa\hat{\otimes}\operatorname{Ad}(\iota)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{flip}\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}_{0}^{\prime})\penalty\ \&\penalty\ \mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\hat{\otimes}\Psi}

where
\Psi:\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}_{0}^{\prime})\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H})\rightarrow\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}_{0}^{\prime}\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{H})
is the natural isomorphism.
Note that
\varphi_{A}
is clearly continuous from
Corollary

2.13
and
Lemma

2.21
. Furthermore, if
\kappa\sim\kappa^{\prime}
and
\iota\sim\iota^{\prime}
, then
(\kappa,\iota)_{*}\sim(\kappa^{\prime},\iota^{\prime})_{*}
from the second part of
Corollary

2.13
.
