\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

2.

Let
\kappa:\mathcal{S}\hat{\otimes}A\rightarrow B\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H})
be a graded
*
-homomorphism, and
\iota:\mathcal{H}_{0}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}_{1}
an even isometry. Then, the induced map
(\kappa,\iota)_{*}:\mathbb{K}(A,\mathcal{H}_{0})\rightarrow\mathbb{K}(B,\mathcal{H}_{1}\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{H})
as in
Definition

5.3
(i) preserves the H-space structure, in the sense that the following diagram commutes upto homotopy:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{K}(A,\mathcal{H}_{0})\times\mathbb{K}(A,\mathcal{H}_{0})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{K}(B,\mathcal{H}_{1}\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{H})\times\mathbb{K}(B,\mathcal{H}_{1}\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{H})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{K}(A,\mathcal{H}_{0})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{K}(B,\mathcal{H}_{1}\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{H})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\kappa,\iota)_{*}\times(\kappa,\iota)_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{u}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{v}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\kappa,\iota)_{*}}

3.

Let
\kappa:A\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{S}\hat{\otimes}B\rightarrow C\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H})
be a graded
*
-homomorphism, and
\iota:\mathcal{H}_{0}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}_{1}
an even isometry. Then, the induced map
(\kappa,\iota)_{*}:\mathbb{K}(A\hat{\otimes}B,\mathcal{H}_{0})\rightarrow\mathbb{K}(C,\mathcal{H}_{1}\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{H})
as in
Definition

5.3
(ii) preserves the H-space structure, in the sense that the following diagram commutes upto homotopy:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{K}(A\hat{\otimes}B,\mathcal{H}_{0})\times\mathbb{K}(A\hat{\otimes}B,\mathcal{H}_{0})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{K}(C,\mathcal{H}_{1}\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{H})\times\mathbb{K}(C,\mathcal{H}_{1}\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{H})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{K}(A\hat{\otimes}B,\mathcal{H}_{0})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{K}(C,\mathcal{H}_{1}\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{H})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{u}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\kappa,\iota)_{*}\times(\kappa,\iota)_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\kappa,\iota)_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{v}}

Proof.

We only discuss commutativity of
(iii)
;
(i)
and
(ii)
are similar (and easier).

We have the following diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{K}(A\hat{\otimes}B,\mathcal{H}_{0})\times\mathbb{K}(A\hat{\otimes}B,\mathcal{H}_{0})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{K}(C,\mathcal{H}_{1}\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{H})\times\mathbb{K}(C,\mathcal{H}_{1}\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{H})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{K}(A\hat{\otimes}B,\mathcal{H}_{0}\oplus\mathcal{H}_{0})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{K}(C,(\mathcal{H}_{1}\oplus\mathcal{H}_{1})\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{H}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{K}(C,\mathcal{H}_{1}\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{H}\oplus\mathcal{H}_{1}\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{H})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{K}(A\hat{\otimes}B,\mathcal{H}_{0})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{K}(C,\mathcal{H}_{1}\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{H})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\kappa,\iota)_{*}\times(\kappa,\iota)_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\kappa,\iota)_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\kappa,\iota_{0}\oplus\iota_{0})_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{add}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\hat{\otimes}\operatorname{Ad}(v)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{add}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\hat{\otimes}\operatorname{Ad}(u)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\hat{\otimes}\operatorname{Ad}(w\hat{\otimes}1)}

Our goal is to show that the outer diagram commutes up to homotopy. To that end, it suffices to show that for some (in fact any) isometry
w:\mathcal{H}_{1}\oplus\mathcal{H}_{1}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}_{1}
, each of the three smaller diagrams commute up to homotopy. The top rectangle commutes from
Proposition

5.5
(ii). The trapezoid at the bottom left commutes upto homotopy by
Lemma

5.4
(ii) (here, we use the fact that
\operatorname{Iso}^{ev}(\mathcal{H}_{0}\oplus\mathcal{H}_{0},\mathcal{H}_{1}\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{H})
is path-connected in the strong-
*
topology). The triangle at the bottom right commutes up to homotopy too (here, we use the fact that
\operatorname{Iso}^{ev}((\mathcal{H}_{1}\oplus\mathcal{H}_{1})\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{H},\mathcal{H}_{1}\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{H})
is path-connected in the strong-
*
topology).
∎

6
Products in the spectral model

One of the biggest advantages of working with the spectral model of
K
-theory is that it is well acquainted to handling sums and products. Having already studied sums, in this section we study products.

6.1
Products and naturality

We start off with the definition of products in the spectral picture:

Definition 6.1
.

Given
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-graded
C^{*}
-algebras
A
and
B
, we have a continuous map

p:\mathbb{K}(A,\mathcal{H}_{0})\times\mathbb{K}(B,\mathcal{H}_{1})\rightarrow\mathbb{K}(A\hat{\otimes}B,\mathcal{H}_{0}\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{H}_{1}),

which is natural with respect to
*
-homomorphisms
A\rightarrow A^{\prime}
and
B\rightarrow B^{\prime}
, and even isometries
\mathcal{H}_{0}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}_{0}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{H}_{1}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\prime}
. On an element
(\varphi,\psi)\in\mathbb{K}(A,\mathcal{H}_{0})\times\mathbb{K}(B,\mathcal{H}_{1})
, it yields the composite

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{S}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{S}\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{S}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}_{0})\hat{\otimes}B\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}_{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\hat{\otimes}B\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}_{0})\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}_{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\hat{\otimes}B\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}_{0}\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{H}_{1})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\triangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi\hat{\otimes}\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\hat{\otimes}\tau\hat{\otimes}1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\hat{\otimes}1\hat{\otimes}\Psi}

(7)

where
\tau:\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}_{0})\hat{\otimes}B\rightarrow B\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}_{0})
is the flip isomorphism, and
\Psi
is the canonical isomorphism between
\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}_{0})\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}_{1})
and
\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}_{0}\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{H}_{1})
. We call the element
p(\varphi,\psi)
as the product of
\varphi
and
\psi
.

Note that the continuity of the map is immediate in light of
Corollary

2.13
and
Lemma

2.21
. The naturality assertion is immediate from
Eq.

7
.

Remark 14
.

For our convenience, we will also call the map
p
as “prod” at some junctures.

Remark 15
.

Note that the naturality assertion in
Definition

6.1
really means that the following diagram commutes, where
\varphi_{A}:A\rightarrow A^{\prime},\varphi_{B}:B\rightarrow B^{\prime}
are a graded
*
-homomorphism, and
\iota_{0}:\mathcal{H}_{0}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}_{0}^{\prime}
,
\iota_{1}:\mathcal{H}_{1}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\prime}
are even isometries.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{K}(A,\mathcal{H}_{0})\times\mathbb{K}(B,\mathcal{H}_{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{K}(A^{\prime},\mathcal{H}_{0}^{\prime})\times\mathbb{K}(B^{\prime},\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{K}(A\hat{\otimes}B,\mathcal{H}_{0}\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{H}_{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{K}(A^{\prime}\hat{\otimes}B^{\prime},\mathcal{H}_{0}^{\prime}\oplus\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\prime})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{prod}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{prod}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{K}(\varphi_{A},\iota_{0})\times\mathbb{K}(\varphi_{B},\iota_{1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{K}(\varphi_{A}\hat{\otimes}\varphi_{B},\iota_{0}\hat{\otimes}\iota_{1})}

We draw this diagram here for future reference.

Proposition 6.2
.

Products are commutative and associative, in the sense that the following diagrams commute strictly:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{K}(A,\mathcal{H}_{0})\times\mathbb{K}(B,\mathcal{H}_{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{K}(B,\mathcal{H}_{1})\times\mathbb{K}(A,\mathcal{H}_{0})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{K}(A\hat{\otimes}B,\mathcal{H}_{0}\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{H}_{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{K}(B\hat{\otimes}A,\mathcal{H}_{1}\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{H}_{0})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{K}(A,\mathcal{H}_{0})\times\mathbb{K}(B,\mathcal{H}_{1})\times\mathbb{K}(C,\mathcal{H}_{2})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{K}(A,\mathcal{H}_{0})\times\mathbb{K}(B\hat{\otimes}C,\mathcal{H}_{1}\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{H}_{2})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{K}(A\hat{\otimes}B,\mathcal{H}_{0}\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{H}_{1})\times\mathbb{K}(C,\mathcal{H}_{2})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{K}(A\hat{\otimes}B\hat{\otimes}C,\mathcal{H}_{0}\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{H}_{1}\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{H}_{2})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{flip}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{prod}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{prod}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\tau_{A\hat{\otimes}B},\tau_{\mathcal{H}_{0}\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{H}_{1}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{prod}\times 1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\times\operatorname{prod}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{prod}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{prod}}

Proof.

We only discuss the commutativity case, the associativity case is more straight-forward.

Checking the strict commutativity of the space level diagram amounts to checking commutativity of the following diagram at the algebra level, where
\varphi\in\mathbb{K}(A,\mathcal{H}_{0}),\psi\in\mathbb{K}(B,\mathcal{H}_{1})
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{S}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{S}\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{S}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{S}\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{S}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}_{0})\hat{\otimes}B\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}_{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}_{1})\hat{\otimes}A\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}_{0})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\hat{\otimes}B\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}_{0})\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}_{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B\hat{\otimes}A\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}_{1})\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}_{0})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\hat{\otimes}B\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}_{0}\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{H}_{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B\hat{\otimes}A\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}_{1}\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{H}_{0})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\hat{\otimes}g\mapsto(-1)^{\partial f\partial g}g\hat{\otimes}f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\triangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi\hat{\otimes}\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{flip}B\penalty\ \&\penalty\ \mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}_{0})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi\hat{\otimes}\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{flip}A\penalty\ \&\penalty\ \mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}_{1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{A\hat{\otimes}B}\hat{\otimes}\operatorname{Ad}(\tau_{\mathcal{H}_{0}\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{H}_{1}})}

It does commute, as can be checked by tracing the fate of the homogeneous element
f\hat{\otimes}g\in\mathcal{S}\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{S}
.
∎

We now show that products “distribute” over sums. In fact, it suffices to show only one sided distributivity:

Lemma 6.3
.

Products distribute over sums from the left, in the sense that the following diagram commutes strictly:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{K}(A,\mathcal{H}_{0})\times\mathbb{K}(B,\mathcal{H}_{1})\times\mathbb{K}(B,\mathcal{H}_{2})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{K}(A,\mathcal{H}_{0})\times\mathbb{K}(A,\mathcal{H}_{0})\times\mathbb{K}(B,\mathcal{H}_{1})\times\mathbb{K}(B,\mathcal{H}_{2})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{K}(A,\mathcal{H}_{0})\times\mathbb{K}(B,\mathcal{H}_{1}\oplus\mathcal{H}_{2})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{K}(A,\mathcal{H}_{0})\times\mathbb{K}(B,\mathcal{H}_{1})\times\mathbb{K}(A,\mathcal{H}_{0})\times\mathbb{K}(B,\mathcal{H}_{2})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{K}(A\hat{\otimes}B,\mathcal{H}_{0}\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{H}_{1})\times\mathbb{K}(A\hat{\otimes}B,\mathcal{H}_{0}\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{H}_{2})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{K}(A\hat{\otimes}B,\mathcal{H}_{0}\hat{\otimes}(\mathcal{H}_{1}\oplus\mathcal{H}_{2}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{K}(A\hat{\otimes}B,\mathcal{H}_{0}\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{H}_{1}\oplus\mathcal{H}_{0}\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{H}_{2}))}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\times\operatorname{add}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.8392,0.3608,0.8392}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{flip}\mathbb{K}(A,\mathcal{H}_{0})\penalty\ \&\penalty\ \mathbb{K}(B,\mathcal{H}_{1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{dia}\times 1\times 1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{prod}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.8392,0.3608,0.8392}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{prod}\times\operatorname{prod}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.8392,0.3608,0.8392}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{add}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

where the bottom isomorphism is the one induced by the canonical unitary isomorphism
\mathcal{H}_{0}\hat{\otimes}(\mathcal{H}_{1}\oplus\mathcal{H}_{2})\rightarrow\mathcal{H}_{0}\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{H}_{1}\oplus\mathcal{H}_{0}\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{H}_{2}
.

Remark 16
.

Purple arrows have been used on the right-hand side of this diagram to use that portion in a later diagram; it is not really needed for the proof below.

Proof.

(We only discuss right distributivity of sum over products; left distributivity will be completely analogous.)
Unravelling the above space level diagram down to the
C^{*}
-algebra level, we have:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{S}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{S}\hat{\otimes}(\mathcal{S}\oplus\mathcal{S})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{S}\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{S}\oplus\mathcal{S}\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{S}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}_{0})\hat{\otimes}(B\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}_{1})\oplus B\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}_{2}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}_{0})\hat{\otimes}B\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}_{1})\oplus A\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}_{0})\hat{\otimes}B\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}_{2}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}_{0})\hat{\otimes}(B\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}_{1}\oplus\mathcal{H}_{2}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\hat{\otimes}B\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}_{0})\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}_{1})\oplus A\hat{\otimes}B\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}_{0})\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}_{2}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\hat{\otimes}B\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}_{0})\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}_{1}\oplus\mathcal{H}_{2})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\hat{\otimes}B\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}_{0}\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{H}_{1})\oplus A\hat{\otimes}B\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}_{0}\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{H}_{2}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\hat{\otimes}B\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}_{0}\hat{\otimes}(\mathcal{H}_{1}\oplus\mathcal{H}_{2}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\hat{\otimes}B\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}_{0}\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{H}_{1}\oplus\mathcal{H}_{0}\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{H}_{2})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha\hat{\otimes}\beta_{1}\oplus\alpha\hat{\otimes}\beta_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha\hat{\otimes}(\beta_{1}\oplus\beta_{2})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\hat{\otimes}\operatorname{add}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\operatorname{flip}B\penalty\ \&\penalty\ \mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}_{0}))\oplus(\operatorname{flip}B\penalty\ \&\penalty\ \mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}_{1}))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{flip}B\penalty\ \&\penalty\ \mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}_{0})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\hat{\otimes}1\hat{\otimes}\Psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\hat{\otimes}1\hat{\otimes}\Psi\oplus 1\hat{\otimes}1\hat{\otimes}\Psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{add}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\triangle,\triangle)}

This diagram does commute, as can be seen by considering homogeneous elementary tensors. Note that the map
\mathcal{S}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}\hat{\otimes}(\mathcal{S}\oplus\mathcal{S})
is the one making the the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{S}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{S}\hat{\otimes}(\mathcal{S}\oplus\mathcal{S})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{S}\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{S}\oplus\mathcal{S}\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{S}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\triangle,\triangle)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

commutative, where the bottom isomorphism is inverse to the natural map
f\hat{\otimes}(g\oplus h)\mapsto f\hat{\otimes}g\oplus f\hat{\otimes}h
.
∎

Proposition 6.4
.

Products distribute over sums.

Proof.

Having already shown left distributivity, right distributivity now follows from the commutativity of products. (It can directly be shown just like right distributivity too, as well).
∎

6.2
More naturality

We now see how the maps in
Definition

5.3
go with products. To that end, we have the following:

Proposition 6.5
.

The
K
-theory product is natural with respect the maps described in
Definition

5.3
in the following sense: if
\alpha:\mathcal{S}\hat{\otimes}B\rightarrow B^{\prime}\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H})
is a
*
-homomorphism, and
\varphi:A\rightarrow A^{\prime}
is a
*
-homomorphism, then the following diagram commutes strictly:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{K}(A,\mathcal{H}_{0})\times\mathbb{K}(B,\mathcal{H}_{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{K}(A\hat{\otimes}B,\mathcal{H}_{0}\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{H}_{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{K}(A^{\prime},\mathcal{H}_{0})\times\mathbb{K}(B^{\prime},\mathcal{H}_{1}\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{H})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{K}(A^{\prime}\hat{\otimes}B^{\prime},\mathcal{H}_{0}\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{H}_{1}\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{H})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K-\text{theory product}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K-\text{theory product}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{*}\times\alpha_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\varphi\hat{\otimes}\alpha)_{*}}

Remark 17
.

Note that
\alpha
is as in
Definition

5.3
(i), whereas
\varphi\hat{\otimes}\alpha
is as in
Definition

5.3
(ii).

Proof.

A direct diagram chase, let
\Phi_{A}\in\mathbb{K}(A,\mathcal{H}_{0})
,
\Phi_{B}\in\mathbb{K}(B,\mathcal{H}_{1})
. Then, at the algebra level, we have to show that the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{S}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}_{0})\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{S}\hat{\otimes}B\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}_{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{S}\hat{\otimes}A\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}_{0})\hat{\otimes}B\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}_{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{S}\hat{\otimes}A\hat{\otimes}B\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}_{0}\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{H}_{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A^{\prime}\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}_{0})\hat{\otimes}B^{\prime}\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H})\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}_{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{S}\hat{\otimes}B\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}_{0}\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{H}_{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A^{\prime}\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}_{0})\hat{\otimes}B^{\prime}\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}_{1})\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A^{\prime}\hat{\otimes}B^{\prime}\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}_{0}\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{H}_{1})\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A^{\prime}\hat{\otimes}B^{\prime}\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H})\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}_{0}\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{H}_{1})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi_{A}\hat{\otimes}1\hat{\otimes}\Phi_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\hat{\otimes}\Phi_{A}\hat{\otimes}\Phi_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{flip}\penalty\ B\penalty\ \&\penalty\ \mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}_{0})\penalty\ \text{(step of product)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{flip}\penalty\ A\penalty\ \&\penalty\ \mathcal{S}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi\hat{\otimes}1\hat{\otimes}\alpha\hat{\otimes}1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{flip}\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H})\penalty\ \&\penalty\ \mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}_{1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{flip}B^{\prime}\penalty\ \&\penalty\ \mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}_{0})(\penalty\ \text{step of product})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi\hat{\otimes}\alpha\hat{\otimes}1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{flip}\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H})\penalty\ \&\penalty\ \mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}_{0}\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{H}_{1})}

It does commute, as can be directly check with elementary homogeneous tensors.
∎

6.3
H-space structures and products

We now see how products interact with the H-space structures on the spectral K-theory spaces.

In order to systematically do the treatment, we make the following definition:

Definition 6.6
.

Let
(X,\mu_{x}),(Y,\mu_{Y}),(Z,\mu_{Z})
be
H
-spaces, and
\varphi:X\times Y\rightarrow Z
a pointed continuous map. We say that
\varphi
is a
bi H-map
if the following diagram commutes upto pointed homotopy:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X\times Y\times Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X\times X\times Y\times Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X\times X\times Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X\times X\times Y\times Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X\times Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X\times Y\times X\times Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X\times Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X\times Y\times X\times Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Z\times Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Z\times Z}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\times\mu_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{flip}X\penalty\ \&\penalty\ Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{dia}\times 1\times 1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi\times\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{Z}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\times 1\times\operatorname{dia}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{flip}X\penalty\ \&\penalty\ Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi\times\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{X}\times 1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{Z}}

Lemma 6.7
.

Let
(X,\mu_{x}),(Y,\mu_{Y}),(Z,\mu_{Z})
be commutative
H
-monoids, and
\varphi:X\times Y\rightarrow Z
be a bi-H map. Then,
\varphi
induces a bilinear monoid map

\pi_{0}(X)\times\pi_{0}(Y)\rightarrow\pi_{0}(Z)

Proof.

By the functoriality of
\pi_{0}
, it is clear that there is a set map
\pi_{0}(X)\times\pi_{0}(Y)\rightarrow\pi_{0}(Z)
; the point is to check its bilinearity.

We first fix
[x]\in\pi_{0}(X)
, and show that the map

\displaystyle\varphi_{x_{*}}:\pi_{0}(Y)\rightarrow\pi_{0}(Z)

\displaystyle[y]\mapsto[\varphi(x,y)]

is linear. Indeed, on the one hand, we have

\displaystyle[y_{0}]+[y_{1}]=[\mu_{Y}(y_{0},y_{1})]

\displaystyle\implies

\displaystyle\varphi_{x_{*}}([y_{0}]+[y_{1}])=[\varphi(x,\mu_{Y}(y_{0},y_{1}))].

On the other hand,

\displaystyle\varphi_{x_{*}}([y_{0}])+\varphi_{x_{*}}([y_{1}])=[\varphi(x,y_{0})]+[\varphi(x,y_{1})]=[\mu_{Z}(\varphi(x,y_{0}),\varphi(x,y_{1}))]

The rest follows from the homotopy commutativity of the left diagram in
Definition

6.6
.

Similarly, linearity upon fixing an element of
\pi_{0}(Y)
can be shown by considering the right diagram in
Definition

6.6
.
∎

Remark 18
.

We caution the reader that the term “bi H-map” is not standard terminology. The author was unable to find a better word to describe the required properties.

Proposition 6.8
.

Let
\mathcal{H}_{0}
,
\mathcal{H}_{1}
be graded Hilbert spaces whose and even and odd parts are countably infinite dimensional. Fix H-space structures
\mu_{u_{0}},\mu_{u_{1}},\mu_{u}
as prescribed in
Definition

5.7
on
\mathbb{K}(A,\mathcal{H}_{0}),\mathbb{K}(B,\mathcal{H}_{1})
and
\mathbb{K}(A\hat{\otimes}B,\mathcal{H}_{0}\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{H}_{1})
.
8
8

8

equivalently, fix even unitaries
u_{0}:\mathcal{H}_{0}\oplus\mathcal{H}_{0}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}_{0}
,
u_{1}:\mathcal{H}_{1}\oplus\mathcal{H}_{1}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}_{1}
,
u:\mathcal{H}_{0}\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{H}_{1}\oplus\mathcal{H}_{0}\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{H}_{1}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}_{0}\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{H}_{1}
.

Then, the map described in
Definition

6.1
is a bi H-map.

Proof.

Consider the following diagram, where the purple arrow is the composite of the purple arrows in
Lemma

6.3
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{K}(A,\mathcal{H}_{0})\times\mathbb{K}(B,\mathcal{H}_{1})\times\mathbb{K}(B,\mathcal{H}_{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{K}(A,\mathcal{H}_{0})^{2}\times\mathbb{K}(B,\mathcal{H}_{1})^{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{K}(A,\mathcal{H}_{0})\times\mathbb{K}(B,\mathcal{H}_{1}\oplus\mathcal{H}_{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{K}(A\hat{\otimes}B,\mathcal{H}_{0}\hat{\otimes}(\mathcal{H}_{1}\oplus\mathcal{H}_{1}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{K}(A,\mathcal{H}_{0})\times\mathbb{K}(B,\mathcal{H}_{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{K}(A\hat{\otimes}B,\mathcal{H}_{0}\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{H}_{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{K}(A\hat{\otimes}B,\mathcal{H}_{0}\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{H}_{1}\oplus\mathcal{H}_{0}\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{H}_{1}))}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\times\operatorname{add}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{prod}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.8392,0.3608,0.3608}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\times(\operatorname{Ad}u_{1})_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.8392,0.3608,0.3608}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Ad}(1\hat{\otimes}u_{1})_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.8392,0.3608,0.3608}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{prod}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.3608,0.3608,0.8392}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\hat{\otimes}(\operatorname{Ad}u)_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{dia}\times 1\times 1}

Our aim is to show that the outer diagram commutes upto homotopy.

Note that the diagram spanned by the black arrows and the purple arrow commutes strictly by
Lemma

6.3
. The lower left rectangle commutes strictly because of the naturality of products as mentioned in
Remark

15
. The blue-black-orangle triangle commutes upto homotopy as
\mathcal{U}^{\operatorname{ev}}(\mathcal{H}_{0}\hat{\otimes}(\mathcal{H}_{1}\oplus\mathcal{H}_{1}),\mathcal{H}_{0}\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{H}_{1}\oplus\mathcal{H}_{0}\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{H}_{1})
is path connected in the strong-
*
topology.
So overall, the outer diagram commutes upto homotopy.
∎

7
Spectral K-theory groups

We now give the definition of
K
-groups in the spectral model. Having developed most of the machinery at the space level, most of the properties of these
K
-groups will be mere corollaries.

Definition 7.1
.

Given a
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-graded (Real)
C^{*}
-algebra
A
, we define
the spectral K-theory space
(without mention of any given underlying Hilbert space) of
A
to be
\mathbb{K}(A):=\mathbb{K}(A,\hat{l^{2}})
.

Definition 7.2
.

Given a
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-graded (Real)
C^{*}
-algebra
A
, we define its spectral K-theory groups to be the homotopy groups of
\mathbb{K}(A)
at the basepoint
0
. That is, we define
{K_{i}}^{sp}(A):=\pi_{i}(\mathbb{K}(A),\underline{0})
.

Before proceeding further, we note that for any graded Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
whose even and odd parts are countably infinite dimensional, there exists a preferred homotopy class of maps
\mathbb{K}(A,\mathcal{H})\rightarrow\mathbb{K}(A)
for each
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-graded
C^{*}
-algebra
A
, given by conjugation with any even unitary from
\mathcal{H}
to
\hat{l^{2}}
. Any unlabelled map
\mathbb{K}(A,\mathcal{H})\rightarrow\mathbb{K}(A)
would be assumed to be (the homotopy class of) this.

As an immediate corollary to our section on H-spaces, we have the following definition:

Definition 7.3
.

By
the
H-space structure on
\mathbb{K}(A)
, we mean the homotopy class of the following composite:

\mathbb{K}(A)\times\mathbb{K}(A)\xrightarrow{s}\mathbb{K}(A,\hat{l^{2}}\oplus\hat{l^{2}})\rightarrow\mathbb{K}(A)

The following is an immediate corollary to
Corollary

5.13
.

Corollary 7.4
.

K_{0}^{\operatorname{sp}}(A)
is an abelian monoid.
K_{1}^{\operatorname{sp}}(A)
is an abelian group, and so are the higher spectral
K
-groups by homotopy theory.

Remark 19
.

Note that as of now, we don’t know yet that the spectral
K_{0}
-group is an abelian group. However, we phrase the subsequent results of this section treating them as abelian groups; it doesn’t create any problem as abelian group homomorphisms are homomorphisms of the underlying abelian monoid, and constructions like tensor products are done on the underlying abelian monoid. We prove that the spectral
K_{0}
groups are abelian groups in
Theorem

8.41
.

Basic homotopy theory immediately yields the following corollary to
Corollary

2.22
.

Corollary 7.5
.

For each
i\geq 0
,
K_{i}^{\operatorname{sp}}(-)
is a functor from the category of
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-graded
C^{*}
-algebras to the category of abelian groups which satisfies the following properties:

1.

Homotopic
*
-homomorphisms
\varphi,\psi:A\rightarrow B
induce the same map
K_{i}^{\operatorname{sp}}(\varphi)=K_{i}^{\operatorname{sp}}(\psi):K_{i}^{\operatorname{sp}}(A)\rightarrow K_{i}^{\operatorname{sp}}(B)
.

2.

Any isometry
\iota\in\hat{l^{2}}
induces an isomorphism
K_{i}^{\operatorname{sp}}(\iota):K_{i}^{\operatorname{sp}}(A)\rightarrow K_{i}^{\operatorname{sp}}(A)
.

3.

0:A\rightarrow B
induces the zero homomorphism
K_{i}^{\operatorname{sp}}(A)\rightarrow K_{i}^{\operatorname{sp}}(B)
.

We have the following two corollaries to the results established in section 3. The proofs follow from the homotopy invariance of homotopy groups, and from the long exact sequence of homotopy groups associated to a Serre fibration (see
[
12
]
Theorem 4.41).

Corollary 7.6
.

1.

Let
A
be a graded
C^{*}
-algebra, and
\mathcal{H}
a graded Hilbert space. Then, the map
\Phi:A\rightarrow A\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H})
, given by
\Phi(a)=a\hat{\otimes}e_{11}
, where
e_{11}
is projection onto an even one-dimensional subspace of
\mathcal{H}
, induces an isomorphism
K_{i}^{\operatorname{sp}}(\Phi):K_{i}^{\operatorname{sp}}(A)\rightarrow K_{i}^{\operatorname{sp}}(A\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}))\penalty\ \forall\penalty\ i\geq 0
.

2.

Let
A,B
be graded
C^{*}
-algebras, and
\varphi:A\rightarrow B
be a surjective graded
*
-homomorphism. Let
J:=\ker\varphi
. Then, we have a long exact sequence as follows:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K_{n+1}^{sp}(A)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K_{n+1}^{sp}(B)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K_{n}^{sp}(J)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K_{n}^{sp}(B)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{inc}_{*}}

The sequence terminates at
K_{0}^{\operatorname{sp}}(B)
.

Now, we cast
Definition

5.3
at the level of K-groups:

Definition 7.7
.

1.

Let
\kappa:\mathcal{S}\hat{\otimes}A\rightarrow B\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H})
be a graded
*
-homomorphism. Then, it induces a well-defined group homomorphism
\kappa_{*}:K_{0}^{\operatorname{sp}}(A)\rightarrow K_{0}^{\operatorname{sp}}(B)
, given by applying the
\pi_{0}
functor to the following composite:

\mathbb{K}(A)\xrightarrow{(\kappa,1)_{*}}\mathbb{K}(B,\hat{l^{2}}\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{H})\rightarrow\mathbb{K}(B).

Note that
\kappa_{*}
is indeed a monoid homomorphism in light of
Proposition

5.14
(ii) and (i). Moreover, homotopic
*
-homomorphisms
\kappa,\kappa^{\prime}:\mathcal{S}\hat{\otimes}A\rightarrow B\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H})
yield the same map
\kappa_{*}=\kappa_{*}^{\prime}:K_{0}^{\operatorname{sp}}(A)\rightarrow K_{0}^{\operatorname{sp}}(B)
.

2.

Let
\kappa:A\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{S}\hat{\otimes}B\rightarrow C\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H})
be a graded
*
-homomorphism. Then, it induces a map
\kappa_{*}:K_{0}^{\operatorname{sp}}(A\hat{\otimes}B)\rightarrow K_{0}^{\operatorname{sp}}(C)
, given by applying the
\pi_{0}
functor to the following composite:

\mathbb{K}(A\hat{\otimes}B)\xrightarrow{(\kappa,1)_{*}}\mathbb{K}(C,\hat{l^{2}}\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{H})\rightarrow\mathbb{K}(C).

Note that
\kappa_{*}
is indeed a monoid homomorphism in light of
Proposition

5.14
(iii) and (i). Moreover, homotopic
*
-homomorphisms
\kappa,\kappa^{\prime}:A\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{S}\hat{\otimes}B\rightarrow C\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H})
yield the same map
\kappa_{*}=\kappa_{*}^{\prime}:K_{0}^{\operatorname{sp}}(A)\rightarrow K_{0}^{\operatorname{sp}}(B)
.

7.1
Products at the group level

We can now form products at the “group level”. We will be brisk from now on, as all the hard work has been developed at the space level before.

Definition 7.8
.

Let
A
and
B
be
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-graded Real
C^{*}
-algebras. We have a well-defined homotopy class given by the composition

\mathbb{K}(A)\times\mathbb{K}(B)\xrightarrow{p}\mathbb{K}(A\hat{\otimes}B,\hat{l^{2}}\hat{\otimes}\hat{l^{2}})\rightarrow\mathbb{K}(A\hat{\otimes}B),

which is natural with respect to
*
-homomorphisms
A\rightarrow A^{\prime}
and
B\rightarrow B^{\prime}
. It descends to a well-defined bilinear pairing (the bilinearity follows from
Proposition

6.8
,
Lemma

6.7
and
Proposition

5.14
(i)):

K_{0}^{\operatorname{sp}}(A)\times K_{0}^{\operatorname{sp}}(B)\rightarrow K_{0}^{\operatorname{sp}}(A\hat{\otimes}B),

which we call as the K-theory product (at the group level).

Remark 20
.

Note that the naturality assertion in
Definition

7.8
is saying that the following diagram commutes, where
\varphi:A\rightarrow A^{\prime}
and
\psi:B\rightarrow B^{\prime}
are
*
-homomorphisms:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K_{0}^{\operatorname{sp}}(A)\otimes K_{0}^{\operatorname{sp}}(B)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K_{0}^{\operatorname{sp}}(A\hat{\otimes}B)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K_{0}^{\operatorname{sp}}(A^{\prime})\otimes K_{0}^{\operatorname{sp}}(B^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K_{0}^{\operatorname{sp}}(A^{\prime}\hat{\otimes}B^{\prime})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{*}\hat{\otimes}\psi_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K-\text{theory product}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K-\text{theory product}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\varphi\hat{\otimes}\psi)_{*}}

That the diagram commutes is a consequence of
Remark

15
and
Definition

7.8
. Moreover, all the maps in the diagram are group homomorphisms: K-theory product has already been seen to be one, and the justfication for the maps
\varphi_{*},\psi_{*}
and
(\varphi\hat{\otimes}\psi)_{*}
comes from
Proposition

5.14
(i).

We also have the following naturality statement:

Corollary 7.9
.

The
K
-theory product is natural with respect the maps described in
Definition

7.7
in the following sense: if
\alpha:\mathcal{S}\hat{\otimes}B\rightarrow B^{\prime}\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H})
is a
*
-homomorphism, and
\varphi:A\rightarrow A^{\prime}
is a
*
-homomorphism, then the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K_{0}^{\operatorname{sp}}(A)\otimes K_{0}^{\operatorname{sp}}(B)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K_{0}^{\operatorname{sp}}(A\hat{\otimes}B)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K_{0}^{\operatorname{sp}}(A^{\prime})\otimes K_{0}^{\operatorname{sp}}(B^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K_{0}^{\operatorname{sp}}(A^{\prime}\hat{\otimes}B^{\prime})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K-\text{theory product}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K-\text{theory product}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{*}\hat{\otimes}\alpha_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\varphi\hat{\otimes}\alpha)_{*}}

Proof.

The diagram commutes as a consequence of
Proposition

6.5
and
Definition

7.8
, and since their a well defined homotopy class of isomorphisms between the Hilbert spaces appearing in
Proposition

6.5
and
\hat{l^{2}}
. Moreover, all the maps in the diagram are group homomorphisms: K-theory product has already seen to be one, and the justfication for the maps
\varphi_{*},\alpha_{*}
and
(\varphi\hat{\otimes}\alpha)_{*}
comes from
Proposition

5.14
(i), (ii) and (iii).
∎

We end this subsection by compiling some properties of K-theory product, which are a reflection of the properties of products already discussed at the space level:

Proposition 7.10
.

The
K
-theory product has the following properties:

1.

It is associative, in the sense that if
x\in K_{0}^{\operatorname{sp}}(A)
,
y\in K_{0}^{\operatorname{sp}}(B)
,
z\in K_{0}^{\operatorname{sp}}(C)
, then
x\times(y\times z)=(x\times y)\times z
.

2.

It is commutative, in the sense that if
x\in K_{0}^{\operatorname{sp}}(A)
and
y\in K_{0}^{\operatorname{sp}}(B)
, and if
\tau:A\hat{\otimes}B\to B\hat{\otimes}A
is the transposition isomorphism, then

\tau_{*}(x\times y)=y\times x
.

3.

It is functorial, in the sense that if
\varphi:A\to A^{\prime}

and
\psi:B\to B^{\prime}
are graded
*
-homomorphisms then

(\varphi\hat{\otimes}\psi)_{*}(x\times y)=\varphi_{*}(x)\times\psi_{*}(y)
.

7.2
Asymptotic morphisms and more products at the group level

In this section, we discuss the notion of an asymptotic morphism, and show that they too, just like
*
-homomorphisms, induce maps on spectral
K
-theory groups. However, unlike
*
-homomorphisms, there is no natural space level constructions inducing them.

Definition 7.11
.

Let
A
and
B
be graded
C^{*}
-algebras. An asymptotic morphism
from
A
to
B
is a family of functions
\varphi_{t}:A\to B
,
t\in[1,\infty)
satisfying the continuity condition that for all
a\in A

t\longmapsto\varphi_{t}(a):[1,\infty)\to B\text{ is bounded and
continuous}

and the asymptotic conditions that for all
a,a_{1},a_{2}\in A
and

\lambda\in\mathbb{C}

\begin{cases}\varphi_{t}(a_{1}a_{2})-\varphi_{t}(a_{1})\varphi_{t}(a_{2})\\
\varphi_{t}(a_{1}+a_{2})-\varphi_{t}(a_{1})-\varphi_{t}(a_{2})\\
\varphi_{t}(\lambda a)-\lambda\varphi_{t}(a)\\
\varphi_{t}(a^{*})-\varphi_{t}(a)^{*}\end{cases}\to 0,\qquad\text{as }t\to\infty

Since
A
and
B
are graded, we shall require that in addition

\alpha(\varphi_{t}(a))-\varphi_{t}(\alpha(a))\to 0\qquad\text{as }t\to\infty,

where
\alpha
denotes the grading automorphism. We shall denote an
asymptotic morphism with a dashed arrow, thus
\varphi:A\dashrightarrow B
.

Although the definition of an asymptotic morphism didn’t necessarily “involve” any
*
-homomorphisms, associated to every
C^{*}
-algebra
B
, there is a
C^{*}
-algebra
\mathfrak{A}B
such that the following result holds:

Proposition 7.12
.

For
C^{*}
-algebras
A
and
B
, there is a natural bijection between the set of equivalence classes of asymtotic morphisms
A\dashrightarrow B
and
\operatorname{Hom}(A,\mathfrak{A}B)
. Furthermore, homotopy classes of asymtototic morphisms from
A
to
B
correspond to homotopy classes of
*
-homomorphisms from
A
to
\mathfrak{A}B
.

Definition 7.13
.

Let
A
be a
C^{*}
-algebra and let
T=[1,\infty).
Then define
the asymptotic algebra of
A
,
\mathfrak{A}A
, by:

\mathfrak{A}A:=C_{b}(T,A)/C_{0}(T,A),

where
C_{b}(T,A)
denotes bounded continuous functions from
T
to
A

and
C_{0}(T,A)
denotes those functions of
C_{b}(T,A)
which vanish in
norm at infinity. Alternatively,

\mathfrak{A}A=\{[f]\mid f:[1,\infty)\to A\mid f\text{
is bounded and continuous}\},

and
[f]
denotes the equivalence class of functions, where
f\sim g

if

\lim_{t\to\infty}\lVert f(t)-g(t)\rVert=0.

The algebra structure here is defined pointwise as expected.

We don’t prove
Proposition

7.12
here; a detailed proof can be found in Theorem 1.2.5 of
[
4
]
. We just note what the natural bijection is going to be: given
\varphi:A\dashrightarrow B
, we map it to the composite

A\xrightarrow{\Phi}C_{b}[1,\infty)\xrightarrow{\operatorname{q}}\mathfrak{A}B,

where
\Phi(a)(t):=\varphi_{t}(a)
, and
q
is the quotient map.

We now show that asymptotic morphisms induce maps on the spectral
K
-theory groups.

Proposition 7.14
.

An asymptotic morphism
\varphi_{t}:A\to B
determines a
K
-theory
map
\varphi_{*}:K_{0}^{\operatorname{sp}}(A)\to K_{0}^{\operatorname{sp}}(B)
, with the following properties:

1.

The correspondence
\varphi\mapsto\varphi_{*}
is
functorial with respect to composition with
*
-homomorphism

A_{1}\to A
and
B\to B_{1}
.

2.

The map
\varphi_{*}
depends only on the homotopy class of

\varphi
.

3.

If each
\varphi_{t}
is actually a
*
-homomorphism, then

\varphi_{*}:K_{0}^{\operatorname{sp}}(A)\to K_{0}^{\operatorname{sp}}(B)
is the map induced by
\varphi_{1}
.

Proof.

1.

We have a natural short exact sequence of graded
C^{*}
-algebras

0\rightarrow C_{0}(T,B)\rightarrow C_{b}(T,B)\xrightarrow{q}\mathfrak{A}B\rightarrow 0,

(8)

and
C_{0}(T,B)
is contractible. By
Corollary

7.6
(ii), we have natural isomorphisms

K_{0}^{\operatorname{sp}}(C_{b}(T,B))\xrightarrow[\cong]{q_{*}}K_{0}^{\operatorname{sp}}(\mathfrak{A}B)

By
Proposition

7.12
, we have a map
K_{0}^{\operatorname{sp}}(A)\xrightarrow{\tilde{\varphi}_{*}}K_{0}^{\operatorname{sp}}(\mathfrak{A}B)
. Our sought after natural map
K_{0}^{\operatorname{sp}}(A)\rightarrow K_{0}^{\operatorname{sp}}(B)
is the one which makes the following diagram commute:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K_{0}^{\operatorname{sp}}(A)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K_{0}^{\operatorname{sp}}(B)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K_{0}^{\operatorname{sp}}(\mathfrak{A}B)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K_{0}^{\operatorname{sp}}(C_{b}(T,B))}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\varphi}_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{q_{*}}^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{ev}_{1}}_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{*}}

2.

This follows from the second part of
Proposition

7.12
and the homotopy invariance mentioned in
Definition

7.7
(i).

3.

As all
\varphi_{t}
are
*
-homomorphisms, the map
\tilde{\varphi_{*}}
factors through

K_{0}^{\operatorname{sp}}(A)\rightarrow K_{0}^{\operatorname{sp}}(C_{b}(T,B))\xrightarrow{q_{*}}K_{0}^{\operatorname{sp}}(\mathfrak{A}B),

and the rest follows from the definition of the map.

∎

Corollary 7.15
.

The
K
-theory product is natural with respect the maps described in
Proposition

7.14
in the following sense: if
\psi_{t}:B\dashrightarrow B^{\prime}
is an asymptotic morphism, and
\varphi:A\rightarrow A^{\prime}
is a
*
-homomorphism, then the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K_{0}^{\operatorname{sp}}(A)\otimes K_{0}^{\operatorname{sp}}(B)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K_{0}^{\operatorname{sp}}(A\hat{\otimes}B)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K_{0}^{\operatorname{sp}}(A^{\prime})\otimes K_{0}^{\operatorname{sp}}(B^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K_{0}^{\operatorname{sp}}(A^{\prime}\hat{\otimes}B^{\prime})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{*}\hat{\otimes}\psi_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K-\text{theory product}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K-\text{theory product}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\varphi\hat{\otimes}\psi)_{*}}

Proof.

The above diagram can be factored as:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K_{0}^{\operatorname{sp}}(A)\otimes K_{0}^{\operatorname{sp}}(B)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K_{0}^{\operatorname{sp}}(A\hat{\otimes}B)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K_{0}^{\operatorname{sp}}(A^{\prime})\otimes K_{0}^{\operatorname{sp}}(\mathfrak{A}B^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K_{0}^{\operatorname{sp}}(A^{\prime}\hat{\otimes}\mathfrak{A}B^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K_{0}^{\operatorname{sp}}(A^{\prime})\otimes K_{0}^{\operatorname{sp}}(C_{b}(T,B^{\prime}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K_{0}^{\operatorname{sp}}(A^{\prime}\hat{\otimes}C_{b}(T,B^{\prime}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K_{0}^{\operatorname{sp}}(A^{\prime})\otimes K_{0}^{\operatorname{sp}}(B^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K_{0}^{\operatorname{sp}}(A^{\prime}\hat{\otimes}B^{\prime})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{*}\hat{\otimes}\tilde{\psi}_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K-\text{theory product}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K-\text{theory product}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\varphi\hat{\otimes}\tilde{\psi})_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K-\text{theory product}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\otimes(q_{*})^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K-\text{theory product}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1\hat{\otimes}q)_{*}^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\otimes\operatorname{ev}_{1_{*}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1\hat{\otimes}\operatorname{ev_{1})}_{*}}

and each of the rectangles commute because of
Remark

20
; for the middle rectangle we also need the naturality of
Eq.

8
.
∎

Proposition 7.16
.

A graded asymptotic morphism
\varphi_{t}:\mathcal{S}\hat{\otimes}A\dashrightarrow B\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H})
determines a
K
-theory map
\varphi_{*}:K_{0}^{\operatorname{sp}}(A)\to K_{0}^{\operatorname{sp}}(B)
, with the following properties:

1.

The correspondence
\varphi\mapsto\varphi_{*}
is
functorial with respect to composition with
*
-homomorphisms

A_{1}\to A
and
B\to B_{1}
.

2.

The map
\varphi_{*}
depends only on the homotopy class of

\varphi
.

3.

If each
\varphi_{t}
is actually a
*
-homomorphism, then

\varphi_{*}:K_{0}^{\operatorname{sp}}(A)\to K_{0}^{\operatorname{sp}}(B)
is the map induced by

\varphi_{1}
.

Proof.

1.

Note that
\varphi:\mathcal{S}\hat{\otimes}A\dashrightarrow B\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H})
induces a
*
-homomorphism
\mathcal{S}\hat{\otimes}A\rightarrow\mathfrak{A}(B\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}))\cong\mathfrak{A}B\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H})
. By
Definition

7.7
, this induces a map
K_{0}^{\operatorname{sp}}(A)\rightarrow K_{0}^{\operatorname{sp}}(\mathfrak{A}B)
, which can be pushed forward by
q_{*}^{-1}
to obtain a map
K_{0}^{\operatorname{sp}}(A)\rightarrow K_{0}^{\operatorname{sp}}(B)
; the map which we denote by
\varphi_{*}
.

2.

This follows from the second part of
Proposition

7.12
and the homotopy invariance mentioned in
Definition

7.7
(ii).

3.

Similar to the proof of
(iii)
of
Proposition

7.14
.

∎

Corollary 7.17
.

The
K
-theory product is natural with respect the maps described in
Proposition

7.16
in the following sense: if
\alpha:\mathcal{S}\hat{\otimes}B\dashrightarrow B^{\prime}\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H})
is an asymptotic morphism, and
\varphi:A\rightarrow A^{\prime}
is a
*
-homomorphism, then the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K_{0}^{\operatorname{sp}}(A)\otimes K_{0}^{\operatorname{sp}}(B)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K_{0}^{\operatorname{sp}}(A\hat{\otimes}B)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K_{0}^{\operatorname{sp}}(A^{\prime})\otimes K_{0}^{\operatorname{sp}}(B^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K_{0}^{\operatorname{sp}}(A^{\prime}\hat{\otimes}B^{\prime})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K-\text{theory product}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K-\text{theory product}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{*}\hat{\otimes}\alpha_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\varphi\hat{\otimes}\alpha)_{*}}

Proof.

The diagram factors as follows:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K_{0}^{\operatorname{sp}}(A)\otimes K_{0}^{\operatorname{sp}}(B)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K_{0}^{\operatorname{sp}}(A\hat{\otimes}B)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K_{0}^{\operatorname{sp}}(A^{\prime})\otimes K_{0}^{\operatorname{sp}}(\mathfrak{A}B^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K_{0}^{\operatorname{sp}}(A^{\prime}\hat{\otimes}\mathfrak{A}B^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K_{0}^{\operatorname{sp}}(A^{\prime})\otimes K_{0}^{\operatorname{sp}}(C_{b}(T,B^{\prime}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K_{0}^{\operatorname{sp}}(A^{\prime}\hat{\otimes}C_{b}(T,B^{\prime}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K_{0}^{\operatorname{sp}}(A^{\prime})\otimes K_{0}^{\operatorname{sp}}(B^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K_{0}^{\operatorname{sp}}(A^{\prime}\hat{\otimes}B^{\prime})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K-\text{theory product}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K-\text{theory product}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{*}\otimes\tilde{\alpha}_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\varphi\hat{\otimes}\tilde{\alpha})_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K-\text{theory product}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K-\text{theory product}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\otimes(q_{*})^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1\hat{\otimes}q)_{*}^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\otimes\operatorname{ev}_{1_{*}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1\hat{\otimes}\operatorname{ev_{1}})_{*}}

where the top rectangle commutes because of
Corollary

7.9
, and the other rectangles commute because of
Remark

20
; for the middle rectangle, we also need the naturality of
Eq.

8
.
∎

7.3
A note on higher K-groups

As the reader would have noticed, we developed products only at the
K_{0}
level in the preceding subsection. For all our applications, this suffices. We still give a brief sketch of generalizing products to a pairing between the higher K-groups, as a formal consequence of results in homotopy theory.

First, note that for any
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-graded
C^{*}
-algebra
A
, its higher spectral
K
-groups are naturally isomorphic to the
K_{0}^{\operatorname{sp}}
-group of a function algebra on it. More precisely:

Proposition 7.18
.

For any
n\geq 1
, we have a natural isomorphism of abelian monoids:

K_{n}^{\operatorname{sp}}(A)\rightarrow K_{0}^{\operatorname{sp}}(C_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{n},A))

Proof.

Our sought after isomorphism comes from the following series of isomorphisms:

\displaystyle K_{n}^{\operatorname{sp}}(A)

\displaystyle\cong\pi_{0}(\Omega^{n}\mathbb{K}(A))

\displaystyle=\pi_{0}(\operatorname{Map}_{*}(\mathbb{S}^{n},\mathbb{K}(A)))

\displaystyle\cong\pi_{0}(\mathbb{K}(C_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{n},A)))

\displaystyle=K_{0}^{\operatorname{sp}}(C_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{n},A)),

where the middle isomorphism stems from the isomorphism in
Proposition

2.9
. The fact that the isomorphism therein preserves the H-space structure (whence we have an isomorphism of monoids) is routine upon observing that
\mathbb{K}(A)
is a H-space, whence the group structure on
\operatorname{Map}_{*}(\mathbb{S}^{n},\mathbb{K}(A))
is induced from the addition on
\mathbb{K}(A)
.
∎

Thus, every higher spectral
K
-group is a spectral
K_{0}
group in disguise.

To discuss the pairings between higher
K
-groups, we first note the following:

Lemma 7.19
.

The product map in
Definition

7.8
descends to a map

p:\mathbb{K}(A)\wedge\mathbb{K}(B)\rightarrow\mathbb{K}(A\hat{\otimes}B).

Proof.

This is obvious from observing that if one of product factors in
Definition

6.1
is the zero homomorphism, then so is the resultant.
∎

Corollary 7.20
.

Given any two graded
C^{*}
-algebras, there is a bilinear pairing
K_{i}^{\operatorname{sp}}(A)\otimes K_{j}^{\operatorname{sp}}(B)\rightarrow K_{i+j}^{\operatorname{sp}}(A\hat{\otimes}B)\penalty\ \forall\penalty\ i,j\geq 0
which is natural for
*
-homomorphisms
A\rightarrow A^{\prime}
and
B\rightarrow B^{\prime}
.

Proof Sketch.

It is a result in homotopy theory that if
X,Y,Z
are based spaces, then a continuous map
f:X\wedge Y\rightarrow Z
induces a natural bilinear pairing
\pi_{i}(X)\otimes\pi_{j}(Y)\rightarrow\pi_{i+j}(Z)\penalty\ \forall\penalty\ i,j\geq 1
. Note that when
n=1
, by “bilinear”, we really mean being a group homomorphism separately.

This result immediately yields the desired pairing when
i,j\geq 1
. When
i=0
or
j=0
, we use
Proposition

7.18
to reduce to a pairing between spectral
K_{0}
-groups, for which we have already established products.
∎

8
Relating Spectral K-theory with usual K-theory

In this section, we come up with a natural isomorphism between
K_{0}^{\operatorname{sp}}(A)
and
K_{0}(A)
(the strandard projective module picture) for any trivially graded, unital (Real)
C^{*}
-algebra
A
. To that end, we will compare both the the pictures with a third picture, which we call the Fredholm picture of
K
-theory. We begin by recalling the notions of bounded and compact operators on Hilbert modules.

8.1
Preliminaries on Hilbert modules and technical results

We assume the reader is familiar with the notion of a Hilbert module over a
C^{*}
-algebra (in the complex and ungraded case,
[
26
]
is a good reference), and about operators on them. We have analogous notions for
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-graded (Real) Hilbert modules over a
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-graded (Real)
C^{*}
-algebra, see for example
[
14
]
and
[
2
]
.

Throughout this section,
A
denotes a
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-graded (Real)
C^{*}
-algebra (unless otherwise mentioned), and
\mathcal{E}
and
\mathcal{F}
graded (Real) Hilbert modules over it.

For notational purposes, we recall the definitions of bounded, finite-rank and compact operators.

Definition 8.1
.

A map
T:\mathcal{E}\rightarrow\mathcal{F}
(apriori neither linear nor bounded) is said to be adjointable if there exists a map
T^{*}:\mathcal{F}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
satisfying
\langle x,Ty\rangle=\langle T^{*}x,y\rangle\penalty\ \forall\penalty\ x,y\in\mathcal{E}
. Such a map
T^{*}
is then called the adjoint for
T
. By
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{F})
, we denote the set of all adjointable maps between
\mathcal{E}
and
\mathcal{F}
, and when
\mathcal{E}=\mathcal{F}
, we simply denote it by
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{E})
.

It is well known (see
[
26
]
) that the elements of
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{F})
are automatically
A
-linear and bounded, and
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{E})
forms a
C^{*}
-algebra with respect to the operator norm. Furthermore, the grading on
\mathcal{E}
equips
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{E})
with the structure of a
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-graded
C^{*}
-algebra.

Definition 8.2
.

If
e\in\mathcal{E},f\in\mathcal{F}
, define
\lvert f\rangle\langle e\rvert:\mathcal{E}\rightarrow\mathcal{F}
, which maps
x
to
f\langle e,x\rangle
. They are called rank one operators between
\mathcal{E}
and
\mathcal{F}
. They satisfy the following easy to verify facts:

•

The operators
\lvert f\rangle\langle e\rvert
are adjointable, with
\lvert f\rangle\langle e\rvert^{*}=\lvert e\rangle\langle f\rvert
.

•

If
T\in\operatorname{Hom}_{A}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G})
(and in particular, if
T\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G})
) , then
T\lvert f\rangle\langle e\rvert=\lvert Tf\rangle\langle e\rvert
. Similarly, if
T\in\mathbb{B}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{F})
, then
\lvert g\rangle\langle e\rvert T=\lvert g\rangle\langle T^{*}f\rvert
.

•

The subspace spanned by the rank one elements of
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{E})
form an ideal, called the ideal of
A
-finite rank operators on
\mathcal{E}
, and denoted by
\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{E})
.

•

The closure of
\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{E})
in the norm topology is called the ideal of
A
-compact operators, and denoted by
\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{E})
.

Remark 21
.

In the Real case (when we are considering Real operators between Real Hilbert modules), there are potentially two definitions of the finite rank operators that can be taken:

•

A Real finite rank operator is one which is finite rank as prescribed above, and which is also a Real operator

•

A Real finite rank operator is one which can be written in the form
\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lvert f_{i}\rangle\langle e_{i}\rvert
, where
f_{i},e_{i}
are Real elements of the
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{E}
respectively.

The two definitions are equivalent, as can be checked easily: that the latter definition implies the former is clear, and in the other direction, first note that
\overline{\lvert f\rangle\langle e\rvert}=\lvert\overline{f}\rangle\langle\overline{e}\rvert
, and that

\frac{\lvert f\rangle\langle e\rvert+\lvert\overline{f}\rangle\langle\overline{e}\rvert}{2}=\lvert\frac{f+\overline{f}}{2}\rangle\langle\frac{e+\overline{e}}{2}\rvert+\lvert\frac{f-\overline{f}}{2i}\rangle\langle\frac{e-\overline{e}}{2i}\rvert.

However, it is not true that every rank-one Real operator is of the form
\lvert f\rangle\langle e\rvert
for some Real
f\in\mathcal{F},e\in\mathcal{E}
.

Next, we recall some facts about the (internal and external) tensor products of Hilbert modules; see
[
16
]
for a thorough treatment.

First, recall that when
A
is a
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-graded unital
C^{*}
-algebra, and
H
a graded Hilbert module on it, then for every compact Hausdorff space
X
, the graded Hilbert
C(X)\hat{\otimes}A
-module
C(X)\hat{\otimes}H
can be naturally identified with the graded Hilbert
C(X,A)
-module
C(X,H)
. We present a lemma characterizing bounded and compact operators on it:

Proposition 8.3
.

There are natural isomorphisms of graded
C^{*}
-algebras:

\mathcal{B}_{C(X,A)}(C(X,H))\cong\{F:X\rightarrow\mathcal{B}_{A}(H)\mid F\penalty\ \text{is strictly continuous}\},

and

\mathcal{K}_{C(X,A)}(C(X,H))\cong\{F:X\rightarrow\mathcal{K}_{A}(H)\mid F\penalty\ \text{is norm continuous}\},

where both the function algebras are equipped with the supremum norm.

Proof.

That the function algebras described above are
C^{*}
-algebras is routine. We first deal with bounded operators: there are natural maps in both directions:

\displaystyle\Phi_{A}:\mathcal{B}_{C(X,A)}(C(X,H))

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\{F:X\rightarrow\mathcal{B}_{A}(H)\mid F\penalty\ \text{is strictly continuous}\}

\displaystyle T

\displaystyle\longmapsto x\mapsto T_{x},\penalty\ \text{where}\penalty\ T_{x}(h)=(T1_{h})x.

9
9

9

1_{h}
stands for the constant function taking the value
h

and

\displaystyle\Psi_{A}:\{F:X\rightarrow\mathcal{B}_{A}(H)\mid F\penalty\ \text{is strictly continuous}\}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathcal{B}_{C(X,A)}(C(X,H))

\displaystyle x\mapsto T_{x}

\displaystyle\longmapsto f\mapsto\{x\mapsto T_{x}(f(x))\}

We only check now that both
\Phi_{A}
and
\Psi_{A}
are well-defined set maps; that they are mutually inverse
*
-homomorphisms is again routine.

For the well-definedness of
\Phi_{A}
, we first note that the operators
T_{x}
are indeed adjointable operators on
H
. Indeed,
(T_{x})^{*}=(T^{*})_{x}
, as can be easily seen by observing that
\langle T1_{h_{1}},1_{h_{2}}\rangle=\langle 1_{h_{1}},T^{*}(1_{h_{2}})\rangle\penalty\ \forall\penalty\ h_{1},h_{2}\in H
. Next, we need to show that
\Phi_{A}(T)
is strictly continuous for all
T\in\mathcal{B}_{C(X,A)}(C(X,H))
. Since
X
is compact, in light of Lemma 2.3.6 of
[
26
]
, it suffices to show that
\Phi_{A}(T)
is continuous in the point-norm-
*
topology. But this is clear: for all
h\in H
, we have

x_{i}\rightarrow x\implies(T1_{h})(x_{i})\xrightarrow{\lVert.\rVert}(T1_{h})(x)\implies T_{x_{i}}(h)\xrightarrow{\lVert.\rVert}T_{x}(h).

For the well-definedness of
\Psi_{A}
, we first check that given
f\in C(X,H)
, the map
x\mapsto T_{x}(f(x))
is also an element of
C(X,H)
. This can be seen easily as follows: given
x_{i}\rightarrow x
, we have

\displaystyle\lVert T_{x}(f(x))-T_{x_{i}}(f(x_{i}))\rVert

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\lVert T_{x}(f(x))-T_{x_{i}}(f(x))\rVert+\lVert T_{x_{i}}(f(x))-T_{x_{i}}(f(x_{i}))\rVert

and both the summands can be made small enough for large enough
i
; the first one as
x\mapsto T_{x}
is given to be strictly continuous, and the second one because
x\mapsto T_{x}
is norm bounded courtesy the compactness of
X
. We can now conclude that
\Psi_{A}(x\mapsto T_{x})
is an adjointable operator on
C(X,H)
, since
\Psi_{A}(x\mapsto(T_{x})^{*})
can be readily seen to be its adjoint.

It remains now to show the statements involving compact operators. We first show that

\Phi_{A}(\mathcal{K}_{C(X,A)}(C(X,H)))\subseteq\{F:X\rightarrow\mathcal{K}_{A}(H)\mid F\penalty\ \text{is norm continuous}\}.

Indeed, the rank-one operator
\lvert f\rangle\langle g\rvert
maps to the norm continuous map
10
10

10

this requires a check, but is easy!

x\mapsto\lvert f(x)\rangle\langle g(x)\rvert
under
\Phi_{A}
, and as
\mathcal{K}_{C(X,A)}(C(X,H))
is the closed linear span of rank-one operators are the compact operators, the containment follows. To see that the containment is infact an equality, we first note that given
f\in C(X,\mathbb{C})
and
h,k\in H
,
\Phi_{A}(\lvert f_{h}\rangle\langle 1_{k}\rvert)=x\mapsto f(x)\lvert h\rangle\langle k\rvert
, where
f_{h}(x)=f(x)h
. As the collection
\{x\mapsto f(x)\lvert h\rangle\langle k\rvert\mid f\in C(X,\mathbb{C}),h,k\in H\}
is dense in
\{F:X\rightarrow\mathcal{K}_{A}(H)\mid F\penalty\ \text{is norm continuous}\}
, and
\mathcal{K}_{A}(\mathcal{H})
is a Banach space with respect to the norm topology, we are done by Lemma 6.4.16 of
[
18
]
.
∎

In the remainder of this subsection, we include a series of technical results that will come very handy later. For the remainder of this subsection,
H
denotes a graded Hilbert
A
-module, and
E\subseteq H
is a graded Hilbert
A
-submodule of
H
. Let
\mathfrak{K}:={\{T\in\mathcal{K}(H)\mid\operatorname{Im}T\subseteq E,\operatorname{Im}T^{*}\subseteq E\}}
.

Lemma 8.4
.

Let
T\in\mathfrak{K}
. Then,
T\mid_{E}:E\rightarrow E
is an element of
\mathcal{K}(E)
.

Proof.

Note that
\mathfrak{K}
is a
C^{*}
-algebra. Let
\{S_{i}\}_{i\in I}
be an approximate unit of it. Let
\varepsilon>0
be given.
Choose a finite rank operator
F
in
\mathcal{K}(H)
such that
\lVert T-F\rVert<\frac{\varepsilon}{2}
. Also, there exists some
i\in I
such that
\lVert S_{i}FS_{i}-F\rVert<\frac{\varepsilon}{2}
. Thus,
\lVert T-S_{i}FS_{i}\rVert<\varepsilon
. But, it is direct from the elementary properties of finite rank operators as mentioned in
Definition

8.2
that
S_{i}FS_{i}
is of the form
\sum_{k=1}^{n}\rvert r_{k}\rangle\langle s_{k}\rvert
, with
r_{k},s_{k}\in E
. Thus, as
\lVert T\mid_{E}-S_{i}FS_{i}\mid_{E}\rVert\leq\lVert T-S_{i}FS_{i}\rVert<\varepsilon
, and
\varepsilon>0
is arbitrary, we conclude that
T\mid_{E}:E\rightarrow E
is an element of
\mathcal{K}(E)
.
∎

Remark 22
.

From the proof of
Lemma

8.4
, it is clear that the
C^{*}
-algebra
\mathfrak{K}
is the norm closure of the span of the set
\{\lvert x\rangle\langle y\rvert\mid x,y\in E\}
inside
\mathcal{K}(H)
.

Lemma 8.5
.

Associated to the inclusion
j:E\hookrightarrow H
, there is a graded
*
-homomorphism
j_{*}:\mathcal{K}(E)\rightarrow\mathcal{K}(H)
. Moreover,
j_{*}=\operatorname{Ad}(j)
when
E
is complemented.

Proof.

We claim that the map
j_{*}:\mathcal{K}(E)\rightarrow\mathcal{K}(H)
, given by
j_{*}(T)=j(jT^{*})^{*}
is the one we are after. We first show:

Claim:

For any graded Hilbert
A
-module
F
, if
T\in\mathcal{K}(F,E)
, then
jT\in\mathcal{K}(F,H)
.

Proof.

First, we deal the case when
T
is of finite rank. Write
T=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\rvert r_{k}\rangle\langle s_{k}\lvert
, with
r_{k}\in E,s_{k}\in F
. Then, it is direct from the definition that
jT=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\rvert j(r_{k})\rangle\langle s_{k}\lvert
. In the general case when
T
is compact, write
T
as a norm limit of finite rank operators, and the fact that
j
, being an isometry, preserves norms.
∎

Using the above claim twice, together with the fact that the adjoint of a compact operator is compact, yields that
j(jT^{*})^{*}\in\mathcal{K}(H)
. It remains to show that it is a
*
-homomorphism.

•

\varphi
is linear:
j(j(S+T)^{*})^{*}=j(jS^{*}+jT^{*})^{*}=j((jS^{*})^{*}+(jT^{*})^{*})=j(jS^{*})^{*}+j(jT^{*})^{*}
;
j(j(\lambda T)^{*})^{*}=\lambda j(jT^{*})^{*}
.

•

\varphi
preserves
*
: We have to show that
(j(jS^{*})^{*})^{*}=j(jS)^{*}
, or,
j(jS^{*})^{*}=(j(jS)^{*})^{*}
. But this is direct to verify when
S
is finite rank, and the general case follows by writing
S
as a norm limit of finite ranks, and using that
j
is an isometry.

•

\varphi
preserves multiplication: We have to show that
j(j(ST)^{*})^{*}=j(jS^{*})^{*}j(jT^{*})^{*}
. The left hand side directly simplifies to
jS(jT^{*})^{*}
. It thus suffices to show that
S=(jS^{*})^{*}j
. But this is again clear when
S
is finite rank, and the general case follows by writing
S
as a norm limit of finite rank operators, and using that
j
is an isometry.

•

\varphi
commutes with the grading: This is clear by considering the definition of the grading on the compact operators induced by the grading on the underlying Hilbert module.

∎

Theorem 8.6
.

There exists a unique
*
-isomorphism

\Phi:\mathcal{K}(E)\rightarrow\mathfrak{K}

such that
\Phi(\lvert x\rangle\langle y\rvert)=\lvert jx\rangle\langle jy\rvert
. The inverse to
\Phi
is given by

-\mid_{E}:\mathfrak{K}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}(E).

Proof.

The uniqueness of the isomorphism
\Phi
, if it exists, is clear since
\mathcal{K}(E)
is the norm closure of the span of rank-one operators. We therefore focus on existence.

We claim that the map
j_{*}
as in
Lemma

8.5
is the required isomorphism. Clearly,
j_{*}(\lvert x\rangle\langle y\rvert)=\lvert jx\rangle\langle jy\rvert
. From this, it follows that the image of
j_{*}
does indeed lie in
\mathfrak{K}
.
Both the composites
j_{*}\circ-\mid_{E}
and
-\mid_{E}\circ j_{*}
maps
\lvert x\rangle\langle y\rvert
to
\lvert x\rangle\langle y\rvert
(where
x,y\in E
), whence both are identity on a dense subset, and we are done.
∎

We end this subsection with a couple of more lemmas using the notation developed in the above theorem.

Lemma 8.7
.

Let
j_{0},j_{1}:E\rightarrow H
be two (not necessarily adjointable) even isometries, which are homotopic (in the norm topology). Then,
{j_{0}}_{*}\sim{j_{1}}_{*}:\mathcal{K}(E)\rightarrow\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H})
.

Proof.

We claim that
t\mapsto j_{t_{*}}:\mathcal{K}(E)\rightarrow\mathcal{K}(H)
is a homotopy of
*
-homomorphisms, where
t\mapsto j_{t}
is the norm continuous path of isometries. We have to show that
t\mapsto j_{t_{*}}(T)
is a continuous map
[0,1]\rightarrow\mathcal{K}(H)
for each
T\in\mathcal{K}(E)
. We calculate:

\displaystyle\lVert j_{t}(j_{t}T^{*})^{*}-j_{s}j_{s}T^{*})^{*}\rVert

\displaystyle=\lVert j_{t}(j_{t}T^{*})^{*}-j_{t}(j_{s}T^{*})^{*}+j_{t}(j_{s}T^{*})^{*}-j_{s}(j_{s}T^{*})^{*}\rVert

\displaystyle\leq\lVert j_{t}(j_{t}T^{*})^{*}-j_{t}(j_{s}T^{*})^{*}\rVert+\lVert j_{t}(j_{s}T^{*})^{*}-j_{s}(j_{s}T^{*})^{*}\rVert

\displaystyle=\lVert(j_{t}T^{*})^{*}-(j_{s}T^{*})^{*}\rVert+\lVert(j_{t}-j_{s})(j_{s}T^{*})^{*}\rVert

\displaystyle\leq\lVert j_{t}-j_{s}\rVert\lVert T^{*}\rVert+\lVert j_{t}-j_{s}\rVert\lVert(j_{s}T^{*})^{*}\rVert,

and the continuity follows.
∎

Recall that
\hat{\mathcal{H}_{A}}
denotes the standard graded Hilbert
A
-module. It is going to be extremely crucial for us in the forthcoming subsections. Note that there’s a natural identification between
A\hat{\otimes}\hat{l^{2}}
and
\hat{\mathcal{H}_{A}}
. Details about it can be found in
[
2
]
,
[
14
]
.

Lemma 8.8
.

Let
E
be a graded Hilbert submodule of
\hat{\mathcal{H}_{A}}
. Let
j:E\rightarrow\hat{\mathcal{H}_{A}}
be the inclusion. Then the
*
-homomorphisms
j_{*}:\mathcal{K}(E)\rightarrow\mathcal{K}(\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{A})
and
Ad(i):\mathcal{K}(E)\rightarrow\mathcal{K}(\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{A})
obtained from an adjointable even isometry are homotopic.

Proof.

Let
\iota_{0},\iota_{1}:\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{A}\rightarrow\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{A}\oplus\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{A}
be the inclusions. Then,
\operatorname{Ad}(\iota_{0})\sim\operatorname{Ad}(\iota_{1}):\mathcal{K}(\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{A})\rightarrow\mathcal{K}(\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{A}\oplus\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{A})
are homotopic homotopy equivalences, since both are strong-
*
connected to an unitary isomorphism
\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{A}\rightarrow\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{A}\oplus\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{A}
. So, it suffices to show that
\operatorname{Ad}(\iota_{1})\circ j_{*}\sim\operatorname{Ad}(\iota_{0})\circ\operatorname{Ad}(i)
. But,
\operatorname{Ad}(\iota_{0})\circ\operatorname{Ad}(i)\sim(\iota_{0}\circ i)_{*}
, and
\operatorname{Ad}(\iota_{1})\circ j_{*}=(\iota_{1}\circ j)_{*}
. Finally, note that
\iota_{1}\circ j
,
\iota_{0}\circ i
are homotopic (in the norm topology!) through the path
t\mapsto(\cos(t)i,\sin(t)j)
. The rest follows from
Lemma

8.7
.
∎

8.2
Fredholm operators and their index

We now discuss the most important class of operators for K-theory; namely the Fredholm operators. Keeping Atkinson’s theorem from classical Hilbert space theory in mind, we have the following definition: (see also
[
26
]
,
[
11
]
):

Definition 8.9
.

Let
F\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{F})
. We say that
F
is an
A
-Fredholm operator if
1-FG\in\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{F})
and
1-GF\in\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{E})
for some
G\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{E})
. An equivalent definition when
\mathcal{E}=\mathcal{F}
is to ask for invertibility in
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{E})/\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{E})
. We write
F\in\penalty\ \text{Fred}_{A}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{F})
, or simply
F\in\penalty\ \text{Fred}_{A}(\mathcal{E})
when
\mathcal{E}=\mathcal{F}
.
G
as in this definition is called a parametrix for
F
. If
1-FG
and
1-GF
are
A
-finite rank, we call
G
a finite rank parametrix for
F
.

Remark 23
.

In the Real case, a Real Fredholm operator always has a Real parametrix. Indeed, if
G
is any parametrix to
F
,
\frac{G+\bar{G}}{2}
is a Real parametrix to
F
. Similarly, an odd, self-adjoint Fredholm operator has an odd, self-adjoint parametrix.

Remark 24
.

If
F\in\penalty\ \text{Fred}_{A}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{F})
, then
F^{\ast}
is also Fredholm. Indeed, take
G
to be a parametrix for
F
. Then, as adjoint of an
A
-compact operator is
A
-compact, we get
1-G^{\ast}F^{\ast}\in\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{F})
and
1-F^{\ast}G^{\ast}\in\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{E})
, as desired. Similarly, it can be shown that the composition of two
A
-Fredholm operators is
A
-Fredholm.

Note that we, in some sense, take Atkinson’s theorem from classical Fredholm theory as a definition here. The next lemma shows that some equivalent formulations of Atkinson’s theorem in the classical world also hold good here:

Lemma 8.10
.

The following are equivalent for
F\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{F})
:

(i)

F is
A
-Fredholm.

(ii)

\exists\penalty\ G_{1},G_{2}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{E})
such that
1-G_{1}F\in\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{E})
,
1-FG_{2}\in\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{F})
.

(iii)

\exists\penalty\ H\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{E})
such that
1-HF
and
1-FH
are
A
-finite rank operators.

(iv)

\exists\penalty\ H_{1},H_{2}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{E})
such that
1-H_{1}F
, and
1-FH_{2}
are
A
-finite rank operators.

Proof.

•

[
(i)\implies(ii)
] Obvious!

•

[
(ii)\implies(i)
] Let
1-G_{1}F=K_{1}\in\mathbb{K}(\mathcal{E})
,
1-FG_{2}=K_{2}\in\mathbb{K}(\mathcal{F})
. Note that
G_{2}-K_{1}G_{2}=(1-K_{1})G_{2}=(G_{1}F)G_{2}=G_{1}(FG_{2})=G_{1}(1-K_{2})=G_{1}-G_{1}K_{2}
. Thus,
G_{2}-G_{1}=K_{1}G_{2}-G_{1}K_{2}=:K_{3}
is compact, which implies that
G_{2}F=G_{1}F+K_{3}F=1-K_{1}+K_{3}F
, whence
1-G_{2}F
is compact. So we can take
G=G_{2}
(or similarly,

G=G_{1}
).

•

[
(iii)\iff(iv)
] Similar to the above equivalence; note that all we needed above was that compact operators on a Hilbert
C^{\ast}
-module forms an ideal. But so does the set of finite rank operators.

•

[
(iii)\implies(i)
] Obvious, as finite rank operators are compact.

•

[
(i)\implies(iv)
] We have
1-GF\in\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{E})
implies that
GF
is invertible in
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{E})/\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{E})
. The rest follows from the easy result about Banach algebras (see
[
11
]
for a proof): If
A
is a unital Banach algebra, and
J
is an ideal in it, then
a\in A
is invertible modulo
\overline{J}
if and only if it is invertible modulo
J
.

∎

We also note what
Proposition

8.3
says about Fredholm operators on function algebras:

Corollary 8.11
.

Fredholm operators on
C(X,H)
correspond to precisely those strictly continuous functions
F:X\rightarrow\mathcal{B}_{A}(H)
which takes values in Fredholm operators on
H
, and for which a strictly continuous family of pointwise-parametrices
G:X\rightarrow\mathcal{B}_{A}(H)
exist such that
1-FG,1-GF:X\rightarrow\mathcal{K}_{A}(\mathcal{H})
is norm continuous. Moreover, if
F:X\rightarrow\mathcal{B}_{A}(H)
is Fredholm, and if a parametrix
g
to
F(x_{0})
for a fixed
x_{0}\in X
is given, then a parametrix
G:X\rightarrow\mathcal{B}_{A}(\mathcal{H})
to
F
exsits such that
G(x_{0})=g
.

Proof.

The first part of the lemma is immediate form
Proposition

8.3
. We therefore only focus on the second part: from the proof of (ii)
\implies
(i) in
Lemma

8.10
, we have that any two parametrices to
F(x_{0})
differ by a compact operator. So, if
G^{\prime}
is any parametrix to
F
, then
G^{\prime}(x_{0})-g=:k\in\mathcal{K}_{A}(H)
. The operator
G:=G^{\prime}-k:X\rightarrow\mathcal{B}_{A}(\mathcal{H})
is the desired parametrix to
F
.
∎

Corollary 8.12
.

Let
F:X\rightarrow\mathcal{B}_{A}(H)
be a norm continuous family of (odd, self-adjoint, Real) Fredholm operators on
H
. Then,
\Psi_{A}(F)
is a (odd, self-adjoint, Real) Fredholm operator on
\mathcal{B}_{C(X,A)}(C(X,H))
.

Proof.

In light of
Corollary

8.11
, it suffices to show that there exists a norm continuous family
G:X\rightarrow\mathcal{B}_{A}(H)
of pointwise-parametrices to
F
. Let
\mathcal{Q}_{A}(H):=\mathcal{B}_{A}(H)/\mathcal{K}_{A}(H)
. Choose a continuous cross-section
p:\mathcal{Q}_{A}(H)\rightarrow\mathcal{B}_{A}(H)
of the quotient map
q
, which exists by the Bartle-Graves theorem. Then, our sought after
G
can be seen as the following composite:

\hbox to219.04pt{\vbox to53.27pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 109.52081pt\lower-26.31947pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}

Indeed, for any
x\in X
,

\displaystyle q(F(x)G(x))

\displaystyle=q(F(x))q(G(x))

\displaystyle=q(F(x))q(F(x))^{-1}\penalty\ \text{since $s\circ q=\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{Q}_{A}(H)}$}

\displaystyle=1.

implying
G(x)
is a parametrix to
F(x)
. The odd, self-adjoint, Real case follows as in
Footnote

6
.
∎

We now restrict our attention only to
unital, trivially graded

C^{*}
-algebras
A
. Some of the ideas below are adapted from
[
9
]
.

The classical theory of Fredholm operators on a Hilbert space worked very smoothly because the image of such an operator was always closed, and its kernel and cokernel were finite dimensional. However, it is not the case in general over Hilbert
C^{*}
-modules; see
[
11
]
. However, if the image of an
A
- Fredholm operator is closed, then some well known results from Fredholm theory over Hilbert spaces do continue to hold. In what follows
\mathcal{E},\mathcal{F}
are Hilbert modules over
A
.

Lemma 8.13
.

Let
F:\mathcal{E}\rightarrow\mathcal{F}
be an
A
-Fredholm operator with closed range. Then,
\ker F
is algebraically finitely generated and projective.

Proof.

In light of Theorem 15.4.2 of
[
26
]
, it suffices to show that the identity operator on
\ker F
is compact. To that end, choose a
G\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{E})
such that
1-GF=:R
is an
A
-compact operator on
\mathcal{E}
. Then, note that

x\in\ker F\implies Rx=x.

Also, as
\operatorname{im}F
is given to be closed, Theorem 3.2 of
[
16
]
shows that
\ker F
is complemented in
\mathcal{E}
, whence there exists a projection operator
P:\mathcal{E}\rightarrow\ker F
. Therefore, the identity on
\mathcal{E}
factors as
