and the conclusion follows since
R
is compact.
∎

Lemma 8.14
.

Given any surjective
A
-Fredholm operator
F
, there exists a
\delta>0
such that if
G
is a surjective Fredholm operator with
\lVert F-G\rVert<\delta
, then
\ker F\cong\ker G
.

Proof.

As
F
is surjective,
FF^{*}
is invertible (see the proof of Theorem 3.2 in
[
16
]
), and we can write
\ker F=\ker F_{1}
, where
F_{1}=(FF^{*})^{-\frac{1}{2}}F
. Similarly,
\ker G=\ker G_{1}
, where
G_{1}=(GG^{*})^{-\frac{1}{2}}G
. Then
F_{1}{F_{1}}^{*}={G_{1}}{G_{1}}^{*}=1
. So,
{G_{1}}^{*}{G_{1}}
and
{F_{1}}^{*}{F_{1}}
are the projections onto the kernels of
G
and
F
respectively. We can also choose a
\delta>0
such that
\lVert F_{1}{F_{1}}^{*}-G_{1}{G_{1}}^{*}\rVert<1
. It now follows from basic facts about projections in a unital
C^{*}
-algebra (see
[
20
]
Section 2.2) that
F_{1}{F_{1}}^{*}
and
G_{1}{G_{1}}^{*}
are unitarily equivalent, whence have isomorphic kernels.
∎

In light of the above discussion, we define index of a
A
-Fredholm operator in the following way:

Definition 8.15
.

Let
F:\mathcal{E}\rightarrow\mathcal{F}
be an
A
-Fredholm operator. Choose
n\in\mathbb{N}
, and an
A
-linear map
g:A^{n}\rightarrow\mathcal{F}
such that
F+g:\mathcal{E}\oplus A^{n}\rightarrow\mathcal{F}
is surjective. We call the pair
(n,g)
a taming of
F
, and define the index of
F
, denoted
\operatorname{ind}F
, as
\operatorname{ind}F=[\ker(F+g)]-[A^{n}]
.

Lemma 8.16
.

The above definition is well-defined, that is, a taming always exist, and the index is independent of the chosen taming.

Proof.

First, we note that
F+g:\mathcal{E}\oplus A^{n}\rightarrow\mathcal{F}
is Fredholm for any
A
-linear map
g:A^{n}\rightarrow\mathcal{F}
, since
g
is
A
-finite rank. Thus, in light of
Lemma

8.13
,
\ker(F+g)
is finitely-generated and projective (and hence a valid class in
K_{0}(A)
) as soon as
F+g
is surjective.

As in
Lemma

8.10
, choose an
A
-Fredholm operator
G:\mathcal{F}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
such that
R:=1-FG\in\mathbb{F}(\mathcal{F})
. Write
R=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lvert r_{i}\rangle\langle s_{i}\rvert
. Define
L_{r}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lvert r_{i}\rangle\langle e_{i}\rvert:A^{n}\rightarrow\mathcal{F}
. Then, by construction,
F+g
is surjective, and we conclude that
(n,g)
is a taming of
F
.

It remains to show that the index is independent of the taming chosen. To that end, suppose
(n,g)
and
(m,h)
are two tamings. Then, consider the family of
A
-Fredholm operator parametrized over
[0,1]^{2}
given by

\displaystyle F(s,t):\mathcal{E}\oplus A^{n}\oplus A^{m}\rightarrow\mathcal{F}

\displaystyle(e,x,y)\mapsto e+sg(x)+th(y)

By the definition of taming,
F(s,t)
is surjective whenever
(s,t)\neq(0,0)
. Now, the path
t\mapsto F(t,1-t)
is clearly a norm continuous path of surjective Fredholm operators.
Lemma

8.14
now shows (by covering the path with small enough balls and using compactness of
[0,1]
) that
\ker F(0,1)\cong\ker F(1,0)
. But,
\ker F(0,1)\cong\ker(F+h)\oplus A^{n}
, and
\ker F(1,0)\cong\ker(F+g)\oplus A^{m}
, and the rest follows by the group structure on
K_{0}(A)
.
∎

8.3
The Fredholm picture of K-theory

In this subsection, we restrict our attention only to unital
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-graded
C^{*}
-algebras
A
. Although this isn’t a complete necessity, and perhaps something weaker like
\sigma
-unitality could suffice, the applications we have in mind later need unitality assumptions anyway.

Definition 8.17
.

A Fredholm family/cycle over
A
is a triple
(\mathcal{E},\iota,F)
, where
(\mathcal{E},\iota)
is a countably generated
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-graded Hilbert
A
module
11
11

11

often, the grading
\iota
on
\mathcal{E}
will be suppresed in the notation
, and
F
is an odd, self-adjoint Fredholm operator on it.

Definition 8.18
.

The following are some definitions circling Fredholm families:

•

A Fredholm family
F
is called acyclic if
F
is invertible.

•

If
(\mathcal{E}_{0},\iota_{0},F_{0})
and
(\mathcal{E}_{1},\iota_{1},F_{1})
are Fredholm families, then
(\mathcal{E}_{0},\iota_{0},F_{0})\oplus(\mathcal{E}_{1},\iota_{1},F_{1}):=(\mathcal{E}_{0}\oplus E_{1},\iota_{0}\oplus\iota_{1},F_{0}\oplus F_{1})
.

•

Two Fredholm cycles
(\mathcal{E}_{0},\iota_{0},F_{0})
and
(\mathcal{E}_{1},\iota_{1},F_{1})
are said to be isomorphic if there exists an even unitary
U:\mathcal{E}_{0}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}_{1}
such that
UF_{0}U^{*}=F_{1}
. We write
(\mathcal{E}_{0},\iota_{0},F_{0})\cong_{U}(\mathcal{E}_{1},\iota_{1},F_{1})
in this case. Although the unitary
U
is a crucial part of the data, we will often suppress it in the notation.

•

Two Fredholm cycles
(\mathcal{E}_{0},\iota_{0},F_{0})
and
(\mathcal{E}_{1},\iota_{1},F_{1})
are said to be concordant (denoted
(\mathcal{E}_{0},\iota_{0},F_{0})\sim(\mathcal{E}_{1},\iota_{1},F_{1})
) if there exists
(\mathcal{E},\iota,F)
, a Fredholm family over
A\hat{\otimes}C[0,1]
such that
(\mathcal{E}\hat{\otimes}_{\operatorname{ev}_{i}}A,\operatorname{ev}_{i}(\iota)\operatorname{ev}_{i}(F))\cong(\mathcal{E}_{i},\iota_{i},F_{i})
for
i=0,1
, where
\operatorname{ev}_{t}:A[0,1]\rightarrow A
is the graded homomorphism given by
\operatorname{ev}_{t}(f)=f(t)
.

The notion of concordance is meant to be a substitute for “paths of Fredholm operators”, but is much more flexible. Indeed, we have the following result:

Lemma 8.19
.

Let
\mathcal{E}
be a countably generated Hilbert module, and
F,G:\mathcal{E}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
odd, self-adjoint Fredholm operators on it. If
F
and
G
are connected by a norm continuous path of odd, self-adjoint Fredholm operators, then
(\mathcal{E},F)\sim(\mathcal{E},G)
.

Proof.

This follows immediately from
Proposition

8.3
and
Corollary

8.12
; the path between
F
and
G
works as a concordance.
∎

The next lemma shows the flexibility of the concordance relation.

Lemma 8.20
.

Let
(\mathcal{E},F)
be a Fredholm cycle over
A
. If
F
is invertible, then
(\mathcal{E},F)\sim(0,0)
.

Proof.

Consider the following concordance
(\mathcal{E}\hat{\otimes}C_{0}[0,1),F\hat{\otimes}1)
, where
C_{0}[0,1)
is the ideal of functions on
C[0,1]
which vanishes at
1
. This is a concordance between
(\mathcal{E},F)
and
(0,0)
, since
F\hat{\otimes}1
is Fredholm courtesy being invertible.
∎

We need another lemma in a similar spirit, whose proof is a little more technical. However, both the lemma and its proof will be used at critical junctures later. Before proceeding, we remind the reader of the famous Kasparov stabilization theorem, which asserts that every countably generated graded (Real) Hilbert
A
-module is isomorphic to a complemented submodule of
\hat{\mathcal{H}_{A}}
. Moreover, the isomorphism can be constructed so that the complement of the image is again isomorphic is
\hat{\mathcal{H}_{A}}
. This extremely important result is going to be our backbone for many of the results. See the seminal paper by G.G. Kasparov
[
15
]
for proofs.

Lemma 8.21
.

Let
(\mathcal{E},F)
be a Fredholm cycle over
A
. Then,
(\mathcal{E}\oplus\hat{\mathcal{H}_{A}},F\oplus U)\sim(\mathcal{E},F)
, where
U:\hat{\mathcal{H}_{A}}\rightarrow\hat{\mathcal{H}_{A}}
is an invertible operator.

Proof.

By Kasparov stabilization, there exists an unitary isomorphism
u:\mathcal{E}\oplus\hat{\mathcal{H}_{A}}\rightarrow\hat{\mathcal{H}_{A}}
. Let
G:\hat{\mathcal{H}_{A}}\rightarrow\hat{\mathcal{H}_{A}}
be such that the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}\oplus\hat{\mathcal{H}_{A}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}\oplus\hat{\mathcal{H}_{A}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\hat{\mathcal{H}_{A}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\hat{\mathcal{H}_{A}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\oplus\mathcal{I}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}

On
C([0,1],\hat{\mathcal{H}_{A}})
, let
\tilde{G}
denote the operator on it given by
\tilde{G}(f)(t):=G(f(t))\penalty\ \forall\penalty\ t\in[0,1]
. Note that under isomorphisms described in
Proposition

8.3
,
\tilde{G}
corresponds to the constant function
G
on
X
.

Now, note that the following restricted diagram commutes too:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{u(\mathcal{E})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{u(\mathcal{E})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\mid_{u(\mathcal{E})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}

Setting
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}:=u(\mathcal{E})
, it suffices to exhibit a concordance between
(\hat{\mathcal{H}_{A}},G)
and
(\mathcal{E}^{\prime},G\mid_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}})
. This can be built as follows: Let

\mathcal{H}=\{e:[0,1]\rightarrow\mathcal{H}_{A}\penalty\ \text{continuous}\penalty\ \mid e(0)\in\mathcal{E}^{\prime}\},

and define consider
\tilde{G}\mid_{\mathcal{H}}:\mathcal{H}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}
.

Then, as
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
is complementable in
\hat{\mathcal{H}_{A}}
, we conclude that
\tilde{G}\mid_{\mathcal{H}}
is adjointable with
\tilde{G}\mid_{\mathcal{H}}^{*}=\tilde{G}^{*}\mid_{\mathcal{H}}
. Furthermore, considering a parametrix
F_{0}^{\prime}
to
F_{0}
, we get
G^{\prime}:=(uF_{0}^{\prime}u^{*})\oplus U^{-1}
is a parametrix to
G
, whence the operator
\tilde{G^{\prime}}(f)(t):=G^{\prime}(f(t))
is a parametrix to
\tilde{G}
. It can also be seen that
\tilde{G}^{\prime}\mid_{\mathcal{H}}:\mathcal{H}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}
is an adjointable operator. We finally observe that
1\mid_{\mathcal{H}}-\tilde{G}^{\prime}\mid_{\mathcal{H}}\tilde{G}\mid_{\mathcal{H}}
and
1\mid_{\mathcal{H}}-\tilde{G}\mid_{\mathcal{H}}\tilde{G}^{\prime}\mid_{\mathcal{H}}
are elements of
\mathcal{K}_{A[0,1]}(\mathcal{H})
. But note that
\operatorname{im}(1-GG^{\prime})\subseteq\mathcal{E}
(here’s where we crucially use the invertibility of
U
), and the rest follows from
Lemma

8.4
.

Thus, we conclude that
(\mathcal{H},\tilde{G}\mid_{\mathcal{H}})
is a Fredholm
A[0,1]
cycle. It is also clear that
(\mathcal{H}\hat{\otimes}_{\operatorname{ev}_{0}}A,\operatorname{ev}_{0}(\tilde{G}\mid_{\mathcal{H}}))\cong(\mathcal{E}^{\prime},G\mid_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}})
, and
(\mathcal{H}\hat{\otimes}_{\operatorname{ev}_{1}}A,\operatorname{ev}_{1}(\tilde{G}\mid_{\mathcal{H}}))\cong(\hat{\mathcal{H}_{A}},G)
.
∎

Lemma 8.22
.

Concordance is an equivalence relation.

Proof.

Throughout this proof, we use the notation of
Proposition

8.3
.

•

Reflexivity: To see that
(\mathcal{E},F)\sim(\mathcal{E},F)
, just consider the concordance
(\mathcal{E}\hat{\otimes}C[0,1],F\hat{\otimes}1)
.

•

Symmetry: Suppose
(\mathcal{E}_{0},F_{0})\sim(\mathcal{E}_{1},F_{1})
, with
(\mathcal{E},F)
being a concordance. Then,
(\mathcal{E}\hat{\otimes}_{\varphi}A[0,1],F\hat{\otimes}_{\varphi}\operatorname{id})
is a concordance between
(\mathcal{E}_{1},F_{1})
and
({\mathcal{E}_{0}},F_{0})
, where
\varphi:A[0,1]\rightarrow A[0,1]
is the order reversing isomorphism.

•

Transitivity: Suppose
(\mathcal{E}_{0},F_{0})\sim(\mathcal{E}_{1},F_{1})
, and
(\mathcal{E}_{1},F_{1})\sim(\mathcal{E}_{2},F_{2})
. We first consider the case where the concordances are of the form
(\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{A[0,1]},F^{\prime})
and
(\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{A[1,2]},F^{\prime\prime})
respectively. This will contain the bulk of the proof; the general case will use some of the ideas used here, and won’t be spelled out in detail.

From
Proposition

8.3
, we can view
F^{\prime}
and
F^{\prime\prime}
as strictly continuous maps
p:[0,1]\rightarrow\mathcal{B}_{A}(\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{A})
and
q:[1,2]\rightarrow\mathcal{B}_{A}(\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{A})
respectively. Now, from the definition of concordance, there exists even unitaries
u,v
making the following diagrams commute:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\hat{\mathcal{H}_{A}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\hat{\mathcal{H}_{A}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\hat{\mathcal{H}_{A}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\hat{\mathcal{H}_{A}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p(1)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q(1)}

Combining the two diagrams, we obtain

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\hat{\mathcal{H}_{A}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\hat{\mathcal{H}_{A}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\hat{\mathcal{H}_{A}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\hat{\mathcal{H}_{A}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p(1)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces vu^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q(1)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces vu^{*}}

Now, we consider the map

\displaystyle\alpha:[0,2]

\displaystyle\rightarrow\mathcal{B}_{A}(\hat{\mathcal{H}_{A}})

\displaystyle t

\displaystyle\mapsto\begin{cases}vu^{*}p(t)uv^{*}\penalty\ \text{if}\penalty\ 0\leq t\leq 1\\
q(t)\penalty\ \text{if}\penalty\ 1\leq t\leq 2\end{cases}

The well definedness of
\alpha
as a function is clear.
We claim that
t
corresponds to a Fredholm operator on
C([0,2],\hat{\mathcal{H}_{A}})
under the isomorphisms of
Proposition

8.3
. The strict continuity of
\alpha
follows from the strict continuity of
p
and
q
. As
p
and
q
corresponded to Fredholm operators, there exists parametrices
p^{\prime}:[0,1]\rightarrow\mathcal{B}_{A}(\hat{\mathcal{H}_{A}})
and
q^{\prime}:[1,2]\rightarrow\mathcal{B}_{A}(\hat{\mathcal{H}_{A}})
respectively. Moreover, we can demand that
q^{\prime}(1)=vu^{*}p^{\prime}(1)uv^{*}
from the second part of
Corollary

8.11
. It follows that the following map is a parametrix to
\alpha

\displaystyle\beta:[0,2]

\displaystyle\rightarrow\mathcal{B}_{A}(\hat{\mathcal{H}_{A}})

\displaystyle t

\displaystyle\mapsto\begin{cases}vu^{*}p^{\prime}(t)uv^{*}\penalty\ \text{if}\penalty\ 0\leq t\leq 1\\
q^{\prime}(t)\penalty\ \text{if}\penalty\ 1\leq t\leq 2\end{cases}

Thus,
(\hat{\mathcal{H}_{A[0,2]}},\Psi_{A}(\alpha))
is a concordance between
(\mathcal{E}_{0},F_{0})
and
(\mathcal{E}_{2},F_{2})
.

Now, we come back to the general case. Let
(\mathcal{E}^{\prime},F^{\prime})
and
(\mathcal{E}^{\prime\prime},F^{\prime\prime})
be concordances between
(\mathcal{E}_{0},F_{0})
and
(\mathcal{E}_{1},F_{1})
, and between
(\mathcal{E}_{1},F_{1})
and
(\mathcal{E}_{2},F_{2})
respectively.
Then,
(\mathcal{E}^{\prime}\oplus\hat{\mathcal{H}_{A[0,1]}},F^{\prime}\oplus 1)
is a concordance between
(\mathcal{E}_{0}\oplus\hat{\mathcal{H}_{A}},F_{0}\oplus 1)
and
(\mathcal{E}_{1}\oplus\hat{\mathcal{H}_{A}},F_{1}\oplus 1)
, and
(\mathcal{E}^{\prime\prime}\oplus\hat{\mathcal{H}_{A[2,3]}},F^{\prime\prime}\oplus 1)
is a concordance between
(\mathcal{E}_{1}\oplus\hat{\mathcal{H}_{A}},F_{1}\oplus 1)
and
(\mathcal{E}_{2}\oplus\hat{\mathcal{H}_{A}},F_{2}\oplus 1)
.

We now invoke Kasparov stabilization to conclude that there exist Fredholm
A[0,1]
- cycles
(\hat{\mathcal{H}_{A[0,1]}},G_{1})
and
(\hat{\mathcal{H}_{A[1,2]}},G_{2})
such that the former is a concordance between
(\mathcal{E}_{0}\oplus\hat{\mathcal{H}_{A}},F_{0}\oplus 1)
and
(\mathcal{E}_{1}\oplus\hat{\mathcal{H}_{A}},F_{1}\oplus 1)
, and the latter is a concordance between
(\mathcal{E}_{1}\oplus\hat{\mathcal{H}_{A}},F_{1}\oplus 1)
and
(\mathcal{E}_{2}\oplus\hat{\mathcal{H}_{A}},F_{2}\oplus 1)
. The first part of the proof now shows that
(\mathcal{E}_{0}\oplus\hat{\mathcal{H}_{A}},F_{0}\oplus 1)
and
(\mathcal{E}_{2}\oplus\hat{\mathcal{H}_{A}},F_{2}\oplus 1)
are concordant.

We still have to come up with a concordance between
(\mathcal{E}_{0},F_{0})
and
(\mathcal{E}_{1},F_{1})
. We first note the concordance between
(\mathcal{E}_{0},F_{0})
and
(\mathcal{E}_{0}\oplus\hat{\mathcal{H}_{A}},F_{0}\oplus 1)
given by
Lemma

8.21
. Similarly, we can also cook up a concordance between
(\mathcal{E}_{1}\oplus\hat{\mathcal{H}_{A}},F_{1}\oplus 1)
and
(\mathcal{E}_{1},F_{1})
. Just like the first part of the proof, we can concatenate the paths corresponding to the concordances so as to build a concordance between
(\mathcal{E}_{0},F_{0})
and
(\mathcal{E}_{2},F_{2})
, with the module being of the form
\{e:[0,3]\rightarrow\hat{\mathcal{H}_{A}}\penalty\ \text{continuous}\penalty\ \mid e(0)\in\mathcal{E}_{0}^{\prime},e(3)\in\mathcal{E}_{2}^{\prime}\}
, where
\mathcal{E}_{0}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{E}_{2}^{\prime}
are complementable copies of
\mathcal{E}_{0}
and
\mathcal{E}_{2}
respectively inside
\hat{\mathcal{H}_{A}}
, as was considered in the proof of
Lemma

8.21
.

∎

Definition 8.23
.

Denote by
K_{0}^{\operatorname{Fr}}(A)
the abelian monoid of concordance classes of Fredholm families over
A
.

Lemma 8.24
.

K_{0}^{\operatorname{Fr}}(A)
is a group.

Proof.

The only thing we need to show is the existence of inverses. We claim that
(\mathcal{E},-\iota,-F)
is the inverse to
(\mathcal{E},\iota,F)
. To that end, we have to show that
(\mathcal{E}\oplus\mathcal{E},\iota\oplus-\iota,F\oplus-F)\sim(0,0)
. We define a norm continuous function
p:[0,1]\rightarrow\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{E}\oplus\mathcal{E})
as follows:

\displaystyle t\mapsto\begin{pmatrix}\cos(\frac{\pi}{2}t)F&\sin(\frac{\pi}{2}t)\\
\sin(\frac{\pi}{2}t)&-\cos(\frac{\pi}{2}t)F\end{pmatrix}

It is direct to see that
p(t)
is an odd, self-adjoint operator on
\mathcal{E}\oplus\mathcal{E}
. We claim that
p(t)
is invertible for
t>0
. Indeed, we have

p(t)^{*}p(t)=\begin{pmatrix}\cos^{2}(\frac{\pi}{2}t)F^{2}+\sin^{2}(\frac{\pi}{2})t&0\\
0&\cos^{2}(\frac{\pi}{2}t)F^{2}+\sin^{2}(\frac{\pi}{2})t\end{pmatrix}\geq\sin^{2}(\frac{\pi}{2}t).\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix},

since
\begin{pmatrix}\cos^{2}(\frac{\pi}{2}t)F^{2}&0\\
0&\cos^{2}(\frac{\pi}{2}t)F^{2}\end{pmatrix}
is positive. The result now follows since a positive element
a
in a unital
C^{*}
-algebra is invertible if and only if
a\geq\varepsilon.1
for some
\varepsilon>0
(here,
a=p(t)p(t)^{*}
, and
\varepsilon=\sin^{2}(\frac{\pi}{2}t)>0
). Thus,
p(t)
is invertible too.

Thus,
p
is a norm continuous path of Fredholm operators on
\mathcal{E}\oplus\mathcal{E}
, with
p(1)=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}
being invertible. The conclusion now follows from
Lemma

8.20
and
Lemma

8.19
.
∎

Lemma 8.25
.

K_{0}^{\operatorname{Fr}}
is a functor from the category of unital, graded
C^{*}
-algebras to abelian groups.

Proof.

This is a direct consequence of the functoriality of the internal tensor product construction: if
\varphi:A\rightarrow B
is a graded
*
-homomorphism and
H
a graded Hilbert
A
-module, then the induced graded unital
*
-homomorphism

\mathcal{B}_{A}(H)\xrightarrow{-\otimes_{\varphi}\operatorname{id}}\mathcal{B}_{B}(H\otimes_{\varphi}B)
restricts to a graded
*
-homomorphism

\mathcal{K}_{A}(H)\xrightarrow{-\otimes_{\varphi}\operatorname{id}}\mathcal{K}_{B}(H)
(Lemma 1.2.8 of
[
14
]
). This shows Fredholm cycles get mapped to Fredholm cycles. Concordant Fredholm cycles in
K_{0}^{\operatorname{Fr}}(A)
get mapped to concordant Fredholm cycles in
K_{0}^{\operatorname{Fr}}(B)
since the following diagram commutes

\hbox to97.4pt{\vbox to52.64pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 48.69786pt\lower-26.31947pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

The group structure is also preserved since the internal tensor product construction respects direct sums.
∎

8.4
Revisiting classical K-theory

In this subsection, we show that when
A
is a unital, ungraded
C^{*}
-algebra, then
K_{0}^{\operatorname{Fr}}(A)
is naturally isomorphic to
K_{0}(A)
, the well-studied projective module picture of
K
-theory (see
[
26
]
,
[
20
]
for the classical treatment). Although not completely necessary, in this subsection, we consider an ungraded picture of Fredholm cycles for the sake of convenience. We cast the definition in this setup.

In this subsection,
A
denotes a unital and trivally graded
C^{*}
-algebra.

Definition 8.26
.

A Fredholm family/cycle over
A
is a triple
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{F},F)
, where
\mathcal{E},\mathcal{F}
are countably generated Hilbert
A
-modules, and
F
is an Fredholm operator from
\mathcal{E}
to
\mathcal{F}
.

Definition 8.27
.

The following are some relevant definitions circling Fredholm families over
A
:

•

A Fredholm family
F
is called acyclic if
F
is invertible.

•

If
(\mathcal{E}_{0},\mathcal{F}_{0},F_{0})
and
(\mathcal{E}_{1},\mathcal{F}_{1},F_{1})
are Fredholm families, then
(\mathcal{E}_{0},\mathcal{F}_{0},F_{0})\oplus(\mathcal{E}_{1},\mathcal{F}_{1},F_{1}):=(\mathcal{E}_{0}\oplus\mathcal{E}_{1},\mathcal{F}_{0}\oplus\mathcal{F}_{1},F_{0}\oplus F_{1})
.

•

Two Fredholm cycles
(\mathcal{E}_{0},\mathcal{F}_{0},F_{0})
and
(\mathcal{E}_{1},\mathcal{F}_{1},F_{1})
are said to be isomorphic if there exists unitaries
U:\mathcal{E}_{1}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}_{0},V:\mathcal{F}_{0}\rightarrow F_{1}
such that
VF_{0}U=F_{1}
. We write
(\mathcal{E}_{0},F_{0})\cong_{U,V}(\mathcal{E}_{1},F_{1})
in this case. Although the unitaries
U,V
are a crucial bit of the data, we will oftentimes be suppressing them and simply write
(\mathcal{E}_{0},F_{0})\cong(\mathcal{E}_{1},F_{1})
.

•

Two Fredholm cycles
(\mathcal{E}_{0},\mathcal{F}_{0},F_{0})
and
(\mathcal{E}_{1},\mathcal{F}_{1},F_{1})
are said to be concordant (denoted by
(\mathcal{E}_{0},\mathcal{F}_{0},F_{0})\sim(\mathcal{E}_{1},\mathcal{F}_{1},F_{1})
) if there exists
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{F},F)
, a Fredholm family over
A\otimes C[0,1]
, and there exists unitaries
u_{0},v_{0},u_{1},v_{1}
such that
(\mathcal{E}\otimes_{\operatorname{ev}_{i}}A,\mathcal{F}\otimes_{\operatorname{ev}_{i}}A,\operatorname{ev}_{i}(F))\cong_{u_{i},v_{i}}(\mathcal{E}_{i},\mathcal{F}_{i},F_{i})
for
i=0,1
, where
\operatorname{ev}_{t}:A[0,1]\rightarrow A
is the homomorphism given by
\operatorname{ev}_{t}(f)=f(t)
. Note that in the notation we suppress the data about the unitary isomorphisms, although they are not to be ignored.

Lemma 8.28
.

When
A
is trivially graded, there is an obvious identification between the ungraded and graded versions of
K_{0}^{\operatorname{Fr}}(A)
.

Proof.

Associating the ungraded Fredholm cycle
(\mathcal{E}_{0},\mathcal{F}_{0},F_{0})
to the graded one given by
(\mathcal{E}_{0}\oplus\mathcal{F}_{0},\begin{pmatrix}0&F^{*}\\
F&0\end{pmatrix})
is the required identification.
∎

The main theorem about this model is the following:

Theorem 8.29
.

The natural map
\Phi:K_{0}(A)\rightarrow K_{0}^{\operatorname{Fr}}(A)
is an isomorphism. In fact, the two functors
K_{0}^{\operatorname{Fr}}(-)
and
K_{0}(-)
are naturally isomorphic when restricted to unital ungraded
C^{*}
algebras.

Recall that the natural map
\Phi:K_{0}(A)\rightarrow K_{0}^{\operatorname{Fr}}(A)
is given by
\Phi([\mathcal{E}]-[\mathcal{F}])=(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{F},0)
. The fact that this is a well-defined group homomorphism follows from the universal property of Grothendieck completion of an abelian monoid.

Before proving the theorem, we come up with an inverse map to
\Phi
. Infact, this inverse map will be a generalization of the notion of index of a Fredholm operator as defined in the last section:

Definition 8.30
.

Let
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{F},F)\in K_{0}^{\operatorname{Fr}}(A)
. We define the index of this Fredholm family as the index of F.

Proof that this is well defined:
We have to show that if
(\mathcal{E}_{0},\mathcal{F}_{0},F_{0})\sim(\mathcal{E}_{1},\mathcal{F}_{1},F_{1})
, then
\operatorname{ind}F_{0}=\operatorname{ind}F_{1}
. To this end, if the Fredholm
A[0,1]
-family
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{F},F)
realizes the concordance, then we claim that
\operatorname{ind}F_{0}=\operatorname{ind}F_{1}=(\operatorname{ind}F)\otimes_{\operatorname{ev}_{0}}A=(\operatorname{ind}F)\otimes_{\operatorname{ev}1}A
. To see the the equality between the last two quantities, note that
\operatorname{ev}_{0}=\operatorname{ev}_{1}\circ\alpha
, where
\alpha:C([0,1],A)\rightarrow C([0,1],A)
is the order reversing isomorphism, and use that the pushout construction is functorial. We next show that
\operatorname{ind}F_{0}=(\operatorname{ind}F)\otimes_{\operatorname{ev}_{0}}A
.

Choose a taming
(n,g)
of
{F}
, and set
\tilde{F}:=F+g
. First of all, if
(\operatorname{ev}_{0}(\mathcal{E}),\operatorname{ev}_{0}(\mathcal{F}),\operatorname{ev}_{0}(F))\cong_{u,v}(\mathcal{E}_{0},\mathcal{F}_{0},F)
, then the commutativity of the following diagram shows that
(n,v\circ\operatorname{ev}_{0}(g))
is a taming of
F_{0}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-11.18968pt}{-13.72363pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -15.48 -18.99)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

Denote by
g_{0}
, the operator
v\circ\operatorname{ev}_{0}(g)
. Thus, to show that
\operatorname{ind}F_{0}=(\operatorname{ind}F)\otimes_{\operatorname{ev}_{0}}A
, it suffices to show that
\ker(F_{0}+g_{0})\cong\ker(\tilde{F})\otimes A
.

Now, we have the short exact sequence of

(9)

Of course, here we crucially needed that
\tilde{F}
is surjective, whence its adjoint is injective. Moreover, surjectivity of
\tilde{F}
implies that
\operatorname{im}\tilde{F}^{*}
is a closed submodule of
\mathcal{E}
and
\ker\tilde{F}=\operatorname{im}{\tilde{F}{}^{*}}^{\perp}
(see Theorem 3.2 of
[
16
]
). Furthermore, as
\ker\tilde{F}
is given to be projective,
Eq.

9
is split exact. Whence, upon applying the functor
-\otimes_{\operatorname{ev}_{0}}A
, we still have the split exact sequence
