{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{F}_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}_{0}\oplus A^{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ker\tilde{F}\otimes_{\operatorname{ev}_{0}}A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{0}^{*}+g_{0}^{*}}

We also have the short exact sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{F}_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}_{0}\oplus A^{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{im}{(F_{0}+g_{0})^{*}}^{\perp}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(F_{0}+g_{0})^{*}}

since
(n,g_{0})
is a taming of
F_{0}
.
As
(F_{0}^{*}+g_{0}^{*})=(F_{0}+g_{0})^{*}
, and
\operatorname{im}{(F_{0}+g_{0})^{*}}^{\perp}=\ker(F_{0}+g_{0})
, we conclude that
\ker(F_{0}+g_{0})\cong\ker\tilde{F}\otimes_{\operatorname{ev}_{0}}A
, as required.

Lemma 8.31
.

\operatorname{ind}:K_{0}^{\operatorname{fr}}(A)\rightarrow K_{0}(A)
is a group homomorphism.

Proof.

Let
(\mathcal{E}_{0},\mathcal{F}_{0},\mathcal{F}_{0})
and
(\mathcal{E}_{1},\mathcal{F}_{1},F_{1})
be two elements of
K_{0}^{\operatorname{Fr}}(A)
. Let
(n_{0},g_{0})
and
(n_{1},g_{1})
be tamings of them respectively. Then, it immediately follows from basic properties of projective modules that
(n_{0}+n_{1},g_{0}+g_{1})
is a taming of
(\mathcal{E}_{0}\oplus\mathcal{E}_{1},\mathcal{F}_{0}\oplus\mathcal{F}_{1},F_{0}\oplus F_{1})
. The rest follows from
Lemma

8.16
.
∎

Proof (of
Theorem

8.29
).

We show that
\operatorname{ind}\circ\Phi=\operatorname{id}
, and
\operatorname{ind}
is injective.

For showing that
\operatorname{ind}\circ\Phi=\operatorname{id}
, it suffices to observe that
\operatorname{ind}\circ\Phi(\mathcal{E},0,0)=(\mathcal{E},0,0)
for any finitely generated projective
A
-module
\mathcal{E}
. This is clear from the well-definedness of the index map.

To show injectivity of
\operatorname{ind}:K_{0}^{\operatorname{Fr}}(A)\rightarrow K_{0}(A)
, let
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{F},F)
be a Fredholm cycle with
\operatorname{ind}F=0
. Choose a taming
(n,g)
of
F
. By basic Hilbert module theory, we have the decomposition
\mathcal{E}\oplus A^{n}=\ker(F+g)\oplus\ker(F+g)^{\perp}
, and the index of
F
being
0
yields that
\ker(F+g)
and
A^{n}
are stably isomorphic. Furthermore,
F+g:\ker(F+g)^{\perp}\rightarrow\mathcal{F}
is an isomorphism. Thus, there exists an
m\in\mathbb{N}
, and an isomorphism of the form
(F+g)\oplus U:\ker(F+g)^{\perp}\oplus(\ker(F+g)\oplus A^{m})\rightarrow\mathcal{F}\oplus(A^{n}\oplus A^{m})
. This shows that
F+g:\mathcal{E}\oplus A^{n}\oplus A^{m}\rightarrow\mathcal{F}\oplus A^{n}\oplus A^{m}
is a compact perturbation of an invertible, whence, so is
F\oplus 1_{n+m}:\mathcal{E}\oplus A^{n+m}\rightarrow\mathcal{F}\oplus A^{n+m}
. This shows that,
(\mathcal{E}\oplus A^{n+m},\mathcal{F}\oplus A^{n+m},F\oplus 1_{n+m})\sim(0,0,0)
. But since
(A^{n+m},A^{n+m},1_{n+m})=(0,0,0)
, we conclude that
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{F},F)\sim(0,0,0)
, as desired.
∎

8.5
The main theorem

We want to come up with a natural isomorphism between
K_{0}^{\operatorname{sp}}(A)
and
K_{0}^{\operatorname{Fr}}(A)
for unital,
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-graded (Real)
C^{*}
-algebras
A
. For technical reasons, we come up with the following substitutes of
\mathbb{K}(A)
and
K_{0}^{\operatorname{Fr}}(A)
:

•

We replace
\mathbb{K}(A)
by the space of all graded (Real)
*
-homomorphisms from
C_{0}(-1,1)\rightarrow\mathcal{K}(\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{A})
, where the domain is still equipped with the even-odd grading, and the Real structure is given by pointwise conjugation. We denote this new space by
\tilde{\mathbb{K}}(A)
, and its homotopy groups as
{\tilde{K}_{n}}^{\operatorname{sp}}(A)
for
n\geq 0
.
\tilde{\mathbb{K}}(A)
is homeomorphic to
\mathbb{K}(A)
in light of the odd, increasing homeomorphism from
\mathbb{R}
to
(-1,1)
given by
x\mapsto\frac{x}{\sqrt{x^{2}+1}}
, and inverse given by
y\mapsto\frac{y}{\sqrt{1-y^{2}}}
.

•

We replace
K_{0}^{\operatorname{Fr}}(A)
with its “essentially unitary counterpart”, wherein instead of considering odd, self-adjoint Fredholm operators, we only consider odd, self-adjoint operators
F
such that
\lVert F\rVert\leq 1
, and
F^{2}-1
is compact. We can thereby see that their concordance classes form a group, which we denote by
K_{0}^{\operatorname{eu}}(A)
.

That
K_{0}^{\operatorname{eu}}(A)
is naturally isomorphic to
K_{0}^{\operatorname{Fr}}(A)
requires some work, which we do now.

Proposition 8.32
.

The natural map
K_{0}^{\operatorname{eu}}(A)\rightarrow K_{0}^{\operatorname{Fr}}(A)
is a group isomorphism.

Proof.

Since every essentially unitary operator is Fredholm, the well definedness of the map is clear. We only need to check that it’s bijective.

We first deal with surjectivity. Let
(\mathcal{E},F)
be a Fredholm cycle. Then, we first claim that
F
is homotopic through a path of odd, self-adjoint Fredholm operators to an operator
G
such that
\lVert G\rVert\leq 1
, and
G^{2}-1
is compact. Indeed, as
F
is self-adjoint and Fredholm, choose some
c>0
such that
(-c,c)\cap\sigma_{\operatorname{ess}}(F)=\emptyset
. Consider the operator
h_{c}(F)
in
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{E})
, where

\displaystyle h_{c}:\mathbb{R}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\mathbb{R}

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle\mapsto\begin{cases}c\penalty\ \text{if}\penalty\ x\geq c\\
x\penalty\ \text{if}\penalty\ -c\leq x\leq c\\
-c\penalty\ \text{if}\penalty\ x\leq-c\\
\end{cases}

We have
h_{c}(F)^{2}-c^{2}
is a compact operator, and
\lVert h_{c}(F)\rVert\leq c
. Thus, taking
G=\frac{h_{c}(F)}{c}
works. To see that
G
is homotopic to
F
, note that
F=h_{\lVert F\rVert}(F)
, and the functions
h_{\lVert F\rVert}
and
h_{c}
are homotopic in
C_{b}(\mathbb{R})
, whence so are
F
and
h_{c}(F)
, and the latter is trivially homotopic to
\frac{h_{c}(F)}{c}
. The surjectivity assertion now follows from (a graded analogue of)
Lemma

8.19
.

Now, we move towards injectivity. Suppose
(\mathcal{E}_{0},F_{0})\sim^{\operatorname{Fr}}(\mathcal{E}_{1},F_{1})
. Let
(\mathcal{E},F)
be a concordance between them. Analogous to the argument for the sujectivity part, we choose some
c>0
such that
(-c,c)\cap\sigma_{\operatorname{ess}}(F)=\emptyset
. Then, we have
\frac{h_{c}(F)}{c}
and
F
are homotopic, with
(\mathcal{E},\frac{h_{c}(F)}{c})
being an “essentially unitary” cycle over
A[0,1]
. We claim that
(\mathcal{E}\hat{\otimes}_{\operatorname{ev}_{j}}A,\operatorname{ev}_{j}(\frac{h_{c}(F)}{c}))\sim^{\operatorname{eu}}(\mathcal{E}\hat{\otimes}_{\operatorname{ev}_{j}}A,\operatorname{ev}_{j}(F))
for
j=0,1
. We in fact show that
\operatorname{ev}_{j}(F)
and
\operatorname{ev}_{j}(\frac{h_{c}(F)}{c})
are homotopic through a path of odd, self-adjoint essentially unitary operators having norm less than or equal to one. First, note that
c\leq 1
, since
\operatorname{ev}_{j}(F)^{2}-1
is compact. Note that, by properties of the internal tensor product,
\operatorname{ev}_{j}(\frac{h_{c}(F)}{c})=\frac{h_{c}(\operatorname{ev}_{j}(F))}{c}
. As
\lVert\operatorname{ev}_{j}(F)\rVert\leq 1
, we have
\operatorname{ev}_{j}(F)=h_{1}(\operatorname{ev}_{j}(F))
. Then the path
t\mapsto\frac{h_{(c-1)t+1}(\operatorname{ev}_{j}(F))}{(c-1)t+1}
is the one we are after. Injectivity now follows from the transitivity of the “essentially unitary” concordance relation.

∎

Now, it suffices to come up with a natural map
\tilde{K_{0}}^{\operatorname{sp}}(A)\rightarrow K_{0}^{\operatorname{eu}}(A)
for every
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
graded (Real)
C^{*}
-algebra.

Let
\varphi\in\mathbb{K}(A)
. Recall that have the identification
\mathcal{K}(\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{A})\cong A\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{K}(\hat{l^{2}})
(see
[
2
]
). Set
\mathcal{H}_{\varphi}:=\overline{\varphi(\mathcal{S})(\mathcal{K}(\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{A}))}
.

Lemma 8.33
.

\varphi
restricts to a non-degenerate graded
*
-homomorphism
\varphi^{\operatorname{nd}}:C_{0}(-1,1)\rightarrow\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}_{\varphi})
.

Proof.

The definition of
\varphi^{\operatorname{nd}}
is simply by restriction; the point is to observe that it is still an
A
-compact operator. But this is clear from a graded analogue of
Lemma

8.4
.

The non-degeneracy of
\varphi^{\operatorname{nd}}
is clear: it suffices to show that every element of the form
\varphi(f)x,f\in C_{0}(-1,1),x\in\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{A}
can be expressed as
\varphi^{\operatorname{nd}}(f^{\prime})(x^{\prime}),f^{\prime}\in C_{0}(-1,1),x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{H}_{\varphi}
. Now, it is easy to see that every element of
C_{0}(-1,1)
can be written as product of two elements of
C_{0}(-1,1)
. Writing
f=g^{\prime}g^{\prime\prime}
, we can set
f^{\prime}=g^{\prime}
, and
x^{\prime}=\varphi(g^{\prime\prime})(x)

∎

The reason we care about non-degeneracy at this juncture is due to the following:

Proposition 8.34
.

Let
A,B,C
be
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-graded
C^{*}
-algebras, such that
A
is an ideal in
B
. Let
E
be a
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-graded Hilbert
C
-module. Suppose that
\varphi:A\rightarrow\mathcal{B}(E)
is a nondegenerate graded
*
-homomorphism. Then,
\varphi
extends uniquely to a graded
*
-homomorphism
\tilde{\varphi}:B\rightarrow\mathcal{B}(E)
. Moreover, when
B
is unital, the extension
\tilde{\varphi}
is unital.

Proof.

Essentially
[
16
]
Proposition 2.1. That
\tilde{\varphi}
is graded, and the unitality assertion can be seen from its proof and the uniqueness of
\tilde{\varphi}
.
∎

Thus, we have a commutative diagram as follows:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C_{0}(-1,1)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{B}(\mathcal{H}_{\varphi}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C[-1,+1]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{\operatorname{nd}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{inc}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\varphi}}

Moreover,
\tilde{\varphi}
is unital from
Proposition

8.34
.

Corollary 8.35
.

Let
\iota_{*}:\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}_{\varphi})\rightarrow\mathcal{K}(\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{A})
be the map as described in
Lemma

8.5
associated to the (not necessarily adjointable) isometry
\iota:\mathcal{H}_{\varphi}\hookrightarrow\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{A}
. Then,
\iota_{*}\circ\varphi^{\operatorname{nd}}=\varphi:\mathcal{S}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}(\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{A})
.

Proof.

It is a direct consequence of
Theorem

8.6
.
∎

Lemma 8.36
.

\tilde{\varphi}(x)
is an odd, self-adjoint operator such that
\lVert\tilde{\varphi}(x)\rVert\leq 1
, and
\tilde{\varphi}(x)^{2}-1
is compact. Moreover,
\varphi^{\operatorname{nd}}
is the functional calculus of
\tilde{\varphi}(x)
.

Proof.

The first part of the lemma follows immediately since the function
x\mapsto x
on
C[-1,1]
has all those properties.

To see that
\varphi^{\operatorname{nd}}=\Phi_{\tilde{\varphi}(x)}:C_{0}(-1,1)\rightarrow\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}_{\varphi})
, it suffices to check it on all polynomials in
C_{0}(-1,1)
which vanish at
+1
and
-1
(as its dense in
C_{0}(-1,1)
by Stone-Weierstrass). Now, for any polynomial
p
,
\Phi_{\tilde{\varphi}(x)}(p)=p(\tilde{\varphi}(x))=\tilde{\varphi}(p)
. When
p\in C_{0}(-1,1)
,
\tilde{\varphi}(p)=\varphi^{\operatorname{nd}}(p)
, and the claim follows.
∎

Corollary 8.37

(Definition of our natural map)
.

The assignment

\varphi\mapsto({\mathcal{H}_{\varphi}},\tilde{\varphi}(x))
is a natural set map from
\tilde{\mathbb{K}}(A)\rightarrow K_{0}^{\operatorname{eu}}(A)
, which descends to a well-defined natural monoid homomorphism

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{A}:

\displaystyle\tilde{K}_{0}^{\operatorname{sp}}(A)\rightarrow K_{0}^{\operatorname{eu}}(A)

\displaystyle[\varphi]\mapsto({\mathcal{H}_{\varphi}},\tilde{\varphi}(x))

Proof.

In light of the preceding discussions, all we now only need to show the following:

1.

Homotopic elements of
\tilde{K}_{0}^{\operatorname{sp}}(A)
are concordant. This can be seen as follows: if
\varphi\sim\psi
, there exists a graded
*
-homomorphism
\Phi:C_{0}(-1,1)\rightarrow\operatorname{Map}([0,1],\mathcal{K}(\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{A}))\cong\mathcal{K}(\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{A[0,1]})
such that
\Phi_{0}=\varphi
,
\Phi_{1}=\psi
. We first claim that the following diagram commutes (
j=0,1
):

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C_{0}(-1,1)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{K}(\hat{\mathcal{H}_{A[0,1]}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}_{\Phi})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{K}(\hat{\mathcal{H}_{A}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}_{\varphi_{j}})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{ev}_{j}(-)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\mid_{\mathcal{H}_{\Phi}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\mid_{\mathcal{H}_{\varphi_{j}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{ev}_{j}(-)}

The commutativity of the left triangle is by definition. For the square, in light of the functoriality of
\operatorname{ev}_{j}(-)
, it suffices to note that
\operatorname{ev}_{j}(\mathcal{H}_{\Phi})=\mathcal{H}_{\varphi_{j}}
. But this follows since every algebraic generator
\Phi(f)x
(
f\in C_{0}(-1,1),x\in\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{A[0,1]}
) of
\mathcal{H}_{\Phi}
is mapped to
\varphi_{j}(f)(\operatorname{ev}_{j}(x))
, and every algebraic generator
\varphi_{j}(f)y
(
f\in C_{0}(-1,1),y\in\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{A}
) of
\mathcal{H}_{\varphi_{j}}
is of the form
\varphi_{j}(f)(\operatorname{ev}_{j}(y^{\prime}))
for some
y^{\prime}\in\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{A[0,1]}
.
This shows that
\operatorname{ev}_{j}(\mathcal{H}_{\Phi})
is a dense subset of
\mathcal{H}_{\varphi_{j}}
. The conclusion follows from the definition of the pushout construction.

Thus, we have
\operatorname{ev}_{j}(-)\circ\Phi^{\operatorname{nd}}=\varphi^{\operatorname{nd}}
. The rest follows from the universality of the extension in
Proposition

8.34
.

2.

\mathcal{F}_{A}
is a monoid homomorphism. This follows directly from the definitions of the addition on both sides, and as unitarily equivalent Fredholm cycles are concordant.

∎

We next proceed to show that
\mathcal{F}_{A}
is surjective and injective. First, we deal with surjectivity.

We need a preparatory lemma:

Lemma 8.38
.

Let
(E,F)\in K_{0}^{\operatorname{eu}}(A)
be an essentially unitary cycle. Then, it can be represented as
(E^{\prime},G)
, where
E^{\prime}\subseteq E
, and
\overline{\Phi_{G}(C_{0}(-1,1))E^{\prime}}=E^{\prime}
, where
\Phi_{G}
denotes the functional calculus of
G
.

Proof.

First, note that
{\Phi_{F}(C_{0}(-1,1))}\subseteq\mathcal{K}_{A}(E)
. Set
E^{\prime}:=\overline{\Phi_{F}(C_{0}(-1,1))E}
. Then,
F(E^{\prime})\subseteq E^{\prime}
, as
f\in C_{0}(-1,1)\implies xf\in C_{0}(-1,1)
. Thus,
G:=F\mid_{E^{\prime}}
is an element of
\mathcal{B}(E^{\prime})
. Moreover, as
F^{2}-1\in\mathcal{K}(E)
and
\operatorname{Im}(F^{2}-1)\subseteq E^{\prime}
,
Theorem

8.6
yields that
(F^{2}-1)\mid_{E^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{K}(E^{\prime})
, whence
G
is an odd, self-adjoint essentially unitary operator on
E^{\prime}
.

We now show that
\overline{\Phi_{G}(C_{0}(-1,1))E^{\prime}}=E^{\prime}
, i.e., the functional calculus of
G
is non-degenerate. To that end, it suffices to observe that
\Phi_{G}=\Phi_{F}^{\operatorname{nd}}:C_{0}(-1,1)\rightarrow\mathcal{K}(E^{\prime})
. To see this, note that,
\Phi_{F}(p)=p(F)
for any polynomial
p
, and in particular, for polynomials which vanish at
+1
and
-1
. For any such polynomial
p
, on the one hand, we have
\Phi_{F}^{\operatorname{nd}}(p)=p(F)\mid_{E^{\prime}}=p(F\mid_{E^{\prime}})
by
Lemma

8.33
. On the other hand, for all such polynomials
p
,
\penalty\ \Phi_{G}(p)=p(G)=p(F\mid_{E^{\prime}})
. Thus,
\Phi_{G}=\Phi_{F}
on the dense subspace of
C_{0}(-1,1)
comprising of polynomials on
[-1,1]
which vanish at the end-points, and we are done.

We finally claim that
(E,F)\sim(E^{\prime},G)
. We build a concordance as follows: Set
\mathcal{H}=\{e:[0,1]\rightarrow E\penalty\ \text{continuous}\penalty\ \mid e(0)\in E^{\prime}\}
, and view it as a graded Hilbert submodule of the graded Hilbert
A[0,1]
-module
\operatorname{Map}([0,1],E)
. Define

\displaystyle\tilde{F}

\displaystyle:\mathcal{H}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}

\displaystyle(\tilde{F}e)(t)

\displaystyle:=F(e(t))

Then
\tilde{F}
is self-adjoint, hence an element of
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})
. Moreover,
\tilde{F}^{2}-1
is compact by (a graded analogue of)
Proposition

8.3
and
Theorem

8.6
, and so
[\mathcal{H},\tilde{F}]
is a concordance. It is now clear that
(\mathcal{H}\hat{\otimes}_{\operatorname{ev}_{0}}A,\operatorname{ev}_{0}(\tilde{F}))\cong(E,F)
,
(\mathcal{H}\hat{\otimes}_{\operatorname{ev}_{1}}A,\operatorname{ev}_{1}(\tilde{F}))\cong(E^{\prime},G)
.
∎

Remark 25
.

Note that, from the above lemma, since
\Phi_{G}=\Phi_{F}^{\operatorname{nd}}
, we have
F\mid_{E^{\prime}}=G=\Phi_{G}(x)=\tilde{\Phi_{F}}(x)

Corollary 8.39
.

\mathcal{F}_{A}
is surjective.

Proof.

Let
(E,F)\in K_{0}^{\operatorname{eu}}(A)
be an essentially unitary cycle. By Kasparov stabilization, we can take
E=\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{A}
without loss of generality. Then, the functional calculus of
F
is a graded
*
-homomorphism
\Phi_{F}:C_{0}(-1,1)\rightarrow\mathcal{K}(\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{A})
. Then,
\mathcal{F}_{A}(\Phi_{F})=(\mathcal{H}_{\Phi_{F}},\tilde{\Phi_{F}}(x))
, and
Lemma

8.38
and
Remark

25
show that
(\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{A},F)\sim(\mathcal{H}_{\Phi_{F}},\tilde{\Phi_{F}}(x))
. This shows surjectivity of
\mathcal{F}_{A}
.
∎

We now focus on the injectivity of
\mathcal{F}_{A}
. We now proceed to show injectivity.

Proposition 8.40
.

\mathcal{F}_{A}
is injective.

Proof.

Let
\varphi_{0}
and
\varphi_{1}
be elements of
\tilde{\mathbb{K}}(A)
such that
\mathcal{F}_{A}(\varphi_{0})=\mathcal{F}_{A}(\varphi_{1})
. This means
[\mathcal{H}_{\varphi_{0}},\tilde{\varphi_{0}}(x)]\sim[\mathcal{H}_{\varphi_{1}},\tilde{\varphi_{1}}(x)]
. Suppose
[\mathcal{E},F]
is the concordance. The functional calculus of
F
is map
\Phi:C_{0}(-1,1)\rightarrow\mathcal{K}_{A[0,1]}(\mathcal{E})
. By Kasparov stabilization, we choose an adjointable isometry
\iota:E\rightarrow\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{A}
.

We now make a series of claims:

Claim
.

Applying the functor
-\otimes_{ev_{j}}A
(
j=0,1
) to the sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C_{0}(-1,1)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{K}_{A[0,1]}(\mathcal{E})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{K}_{A[0,1]}(\hat{\mathcal{H}_{A[0,1]}})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Ad(\iota)}

we get
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C_{0}(-1,1)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{K}_{A}(\operatorname{ev}_{j}\mathcal{E})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{K}_{A}(\hat{\mathcal{H}_{A}})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{ev}_{j}(\Phi)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Ad(\operatorname{ev}_{j}(\iota))}

Proof.

Lemma 1.2.8 of
[
14
]
shows that the map
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C_{0}(-1,1)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{K}_{A}(\operatorname{ev}_{j}\mathcal{E})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{ev}_{j}(\Phi)}
is well-defined (that is, we indeed land in the compact operators). The rest follows from the functoriality of the pushout construction.
∎

Claim
.

The
*
-homomorphisms
\operatorname{Ad(ev}_{0}(\iota))\circ\operatorname{ev}_{0}(\Phi)
and
\operatorname{Ad(ev}_{1}(\iota))\circ\operatorname{ev}_{1}(\Phi)
are homotopic.

Proof.

We have that
\operatorname{Ad}(\iota)\circ\Phi:\mathcal{S}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}_{A[0,1]}(\hat{\mathcal{H}_{A[0,1]}})\cong\operatorname{Map}([0,1],\mathcal{K}_{A}(\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{A}))
is a homotopy between
(\operatorname{Ad}(\iota)\circ\Phi)\mid_{0}
and
(\operatorname{Ad}(\iota)\circ\Phi)\mid_{1}
. The rest follows since under the canonical isomorphism
\mathcal{K}(\hat{\mathcal{H}_{A[0,1]}})\cong\operatorname{Map}([0,1],\mathcal{K}_{A}(\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{A}))
, the functor
-\otimes_{\operatorname{ev}_{j}}A
corresponds to the functor evaluation at time
j
.
∎

Claim
.

In the setup of the last claim,
\operatorname{ev}_{j}(\Phi)
is the functional calculus of
\operatorname{ev}_{j}(F)
.

Proof.

We have the following commutative diagram, where c.f.c. stands for the continuous functional calculus:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C_{0}(-1,1)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C(\sigma(F))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{E})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C(\sigma(\operatorname{ev}_{j}(F)))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{B}(\operatorname{ev}_{j}(\mathcal{E}))}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{res}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{c.f.c\penalty\ of}F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{c.f.c\penalty\ of\penalty\ ev}_{j}(F)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{ev}_{j*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\mid_{\sigma(F)}}

Indeed, the top row composite is
\Phi
, and the square commutes because the continuous functional calculus is uniquely defined upon knowing it for the constant function
1
, and the identity function.
∎

Claim
.

The following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C_{0}(-1,1)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{K}_{A}(\operatorname{ev}_{j}\mathcal{E})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{K}_{A}(\mathcal{H}_{\varphi_{j}})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{ev}_{j}(\Phi)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{j}^{\operatorname{nd}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Ad}(u_{j})}

Proof.

Obvious, in light of the above claim and since
\varphi_{j}^{\operatorname{nd}}
is the functional calculus of
\tilde{\varphi_{j}}(x)
by
Lemma

8.36
.
∎

Summarizing, we now know the following:

there exists adjointable even isometries
\kappa_{j}:\mathcal{H}_{\varphi_{j}}\rightarrow\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{A}
such that
\operatorname{Ad}(\kappa_{0})\circ\varphi_{0}^{\operatorname{nd}}\sim\operatorname{Ad}(\kappa_{1})\circ\varphi_{1}^{\operatorname{nd}}

Now,
Corollary

8.35
shows that
{\operatorname{inc}_{j}}_{*}\circ\varphi_{j}^{\operatorname{nd}}\sim\varphi_{j}
, and we are now done in light of
Lemma

8.8
.
∎

Summarizing the work done in this section, we now have:

Theorem 8.41
.

Let
A
be a
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-graded (Real)
C^{*}
-algebra. Then, there is a natural isomorphism between
K_{0}^{\operatorname{sp}}(A)
and
K_{0}^{\operatorname{Fr}}(A)
. In particular,
K_{0}^{\operatorname{sp}}(A)
is an abelian group.

Theorem 8.42
.

Let
A
be a unital, trivially graded (Real)
C^{*}
-algebra. Then, there is a natural isomorphism between
K_{0}^{\operatorname{sp}}(A)
and
K_{0}(A)
.

Corollary 8.43
.

When
A
is unital and ungraded, under the isomorphism of
Theorem

8.42
, the graded
*
-homomorphism

\displaystyle\theta:\mathcal{S}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\mathcal{K}(\hat{l^{2}})

\displaystyle f

\displaystyle\mapsto\begin{pmatrix}f(0)P_{0}&0\\
0&f(0)P_{1}\end{pmatrix},

where
P_{0},P_{1}
are compact projections, gets mapped to the class
[P_{0}]-[P_{1}]\in K_{0}(A)
.

Proof.

This is just a matter of checking the fate of
\theta
under the isomorphisms

K_{0}^{\operatorname{sp}}(A)\rightarrow\tilde{K_{0}}^{\operatorname{sp}}(A)\rightarrow K_{0}^{\operatorname{eu}}(A)\rightarrow K_{0}^{\operatorname{Fr}}(A)\rightarrow K_{0}(A)

While viewing
\theta
as a
*
-homomorphism
C_{0}(-1,1)\rightarrow\mathcal{K}(\hat{l^{2}})
, we note that
\mathcal{H}_{\theta}=(P_{0}\oplus P_{1})\hat{l^{2}}
. The uniqueness assertion in
Proposition

8.34
) immediately shows that

\displaystyle\tilde{\theta}:C[-1,1]

\displaystyle\rightarrow\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}_{\theta})

\displaystyle f

\displaystyle\mapsto\begin{pmatrix}f(0)P_{0}&0\\
0&f(0)P_{1}\end{pmatrix},

Thus,
\tilde{\theta}(x)=0
, and so the class in
K_{0}^{\operatorname{Fr}}(A)
corresponding to
\theta
is
(\mathcal{H}_{\theta},0)
. The rest follows from the definition of the index of a Fredholm cycle.
∎

Thus, any projection onto an even one-dimensional subspace yields an element of
K_{0}^{\operatorname{sp}}(\mathbf{C})
, which we denote by
1
in light of
Theorem

8.42
. We need a technical lemma concerning this for the next section:

Lemma 8.44
.

With
1\in K_{0}^{\operatorname{sp}}(\mathbf{C})
as above, if

A
is any graded
C^{*}
-algebra and if
x\in K_{0}^{\operatorname{sp}}(A)
, then under the
isomorphism
\mathbf{C}\hat{\otimes}A\cong A
the class
1\times x

corresponds to
x
.

Proof.

Let
\theta:w\mapsto\langle w,x\rangle x
be our rank-one projection, where
x
is a homogeneous element in
\hat{l^{2}}
. Note that we have an isometry

\displaystyle\iota:\hat{l^{2}}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\hat{l^{2}}\hat{\otimes}\hat{l^{2}}

\displaystyle e_{i}

\displaystyle\mapsto x\hat{\otimes}e_{i}

Then, the induced map
\iota_{*}:\mathcal{K}(\hat{l^{2}})\rightarrow\mathcal{K}(\hat{l^{2}})\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{K}(\hat{l^{2}})
is given by
\iota_{*}(T)=\theta\hat{\otimes}T
for homogeneous operators
T
. Now,
\iota
is homotopic to an even unitary
u:\hat{l^{2}}\rightarrow\hat{l^{2}}\hat{\otimes}\hat{l^{2}}
, so that
u^{*}
is an identification between
\hat{l^{2}}\hat{\otimes}\hat{l^{2}}
and
\hat{l^{2}}
. From
Definition

7.8
, we are now done by checking that for the generators
u
and
v
in
\mathcal{S}
, the composite

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{S}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{S}\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{S}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{K}(\hat{l^{2}})\hat{\otimes}A\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{K}(\hat{l^{2}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{K}(\hat{l^{2}})\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{K}(\hat{l^{2}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{K}(\hat{l^{2}})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\triangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\hat{\otimes}x}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\hat{\otimes}\iota^{*}_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{flip}A\penalty\ \&\penalty\ \mathcal{K}(\hat{l^{2}})}

identifies the class of
1\times x
with
x
.
∎

9
Bott Periodicity

In this section, we state and prove the Bott periodicty theorem in the spectral model. As we have already deduced natural isomorphisms between the spectral and projection pictures of K-theory in the unital, ungraded case, we will make no distinctions between these models henceforth. Most results here are inspired from
[
13
]
and
[
4
]
.

We will be making extensive use of Clifford algebras to that end. This is because, in both the complex and Real cases, the well known periodicity stems out of the periodicity of the Clifford algebras.

9.1
Clifford algebras

In this section, we discuss Clifford algebras. As usual, we develop everything in the Real world. We will consider Clifford algebras in a variety of generalities, as follows:

Definition 9.1
.

Let
E
be an
n
-dimensional
\mathbb{R}
-vector space and
Q
a quadratic
form on
E
. The Clifford algebra
\operatorname{Cliff}(E,Q)
is the
solution of the universal problem that asks for an
\mathbb{R}
-algebra
C

and a linear map
j:E\to C
with the following properties: If
A

is an associative
\mathbb{R}
-algebra with unit
1_{A}
and if
f:E\to A
is
linear with
f(v)^{2}=Q(v)1_{A}
for all
v\in E
, then there is a
unique algebra homomorphism
\tilde{f}:C\to A
with
\tilde{f}\circ j=f
.

Remark 26
.

The map
j:E\to\operatorname{Cliff}(E,Q)
will be denoted by

i_{E}
. The special case
Q=0
yields the exterior algebra

\Lambda^{*}E
. For general
Q
we obtain
\operatorname{Cliff}(E,Q)

as the quotient of the tensor algebra

\mathcal{T}(E)=\mathbb{R}\oplus\bigoplus_{k\geq 0}\underbrace{(E\otimes\ldots\otimes E)}_{k\text{ times}}

by the ideal which is generated by elements of the form
v\otimes v-Q(v)1_{T(E)}
,
v\in E
.

Definition 9.2
.

Let
V
be a finite dimensional Euclidean vector space, that is, a finite dimensional real vector space together with a positive-definite inner product. We define

\operatorname{Cliff}(V):=\operatorname{Cliff(V,{\lVert.\rVert^{2}}_{V}})

Now, we consider the situation where
V
also carries a non-trivial involution.

Definition 9.3
.

A finite dimensional pseudo-Euclidean vector space
(V,\kappa)
is a finite dimensional Euclidean vector space equipped with an self-adjoint involution
\kappa
.

Some elementary algebra shows that the following definition makes sense:

Definition 9.4
.

Let
(V,\kappa)
be a finite dimensional pseudo-Euclidean vector space. Then, we can equip
\operatorname{Cliff}(V)
with an involution; by extending
\kappa
multiplicatively on
\operatorname{Cliff}(V)
. We denote
\operatorname{Cliff}(V)
together with this extended involution by
\operatorname{Cliff}(V,\kappa)
.

Proposition 9.5
.

Let
(V,\kappa)
be a finite dimensional pseudo-Euclidean vector space. Then,
\operatorname{Cliff}(V,\kappa)
can be canonically given the structure of a
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-graded real
C^{*}
-algebra.

Proof.

The result is well known, we don’t give all the details here; the details can be found in
[
23
]
and
[
17
]
. We just mention the grading on
\operatorname{Cliff}(V,\kappa)
, and mention a graded injective algebra homomomorphism
c:\operatorname{Cliff}(V,\kappa)\rightarrow\operatorname{End}(\Lambda^{*}V)
, the real
C^{*}
-algebra of bounded operators on the graded real Hilbert space
\Lambda^{*}V
. The norm and adjoint operation on
\operatorname{Cliff}(V,\kappa)
is then defined to be such that this algebra homomorphism is an isomorphism of
C^{*}
-algebras.

We first discuss the grading on
\operatorname{Cliff}(V)
. Note that, by the universal property of Clifford algebras, the map
v\mapsto-v
on
V
extends to an involution preserving automorphism
\alpha
of
(\operatorname{Cliff}(V,\kappa))
such that
\alpha^{2}=1
. We declare
\alpha
to be the grading operator on
\operatorname{Cliff}(V,\kappa)
.

We next give the definition of

c:\operatorname{Cliff}(V,\kappa)\rightarrow\operatorname{End}(\Lambda^{*}V)

For each
v\in V
,
there is a homomorphism

\operatorname{ext}_{v}:\Lambda^{*}V\to\Lambda^{*+1}(V),\qquad\omega\mapsto v\wedge\omega

with
\operatorname{ext}_{v}^{2}=0
.

If
L\in V^{*}
, there also is a unique homomorphism

\operatorname{ins}_{L}:\Lambda^{*}V\to\Lambda^{*-1}(V)
such
that

\operatorname{ins}_{L}=L:V=\Lambda^{1}(V)\to\Lambda^{0}(V)=\mathbb{K}

and

\operatorname{ins}_{L}(\omega\wedge\eta)=\operatorname{ins}_{L}(\omega)\wedge\eta+(-1)^{k}\omega\wedge\operatorname{ins}_{L}(\eta)\qquad\forall\omega\in\Lambda^{k}(V),\eta\in\Lambda^{l}(V)

We have
\operatorname{ins}_{L}^{2}=0
and

\operatorname{ext}_{v}\operatorname{ins}_{L}+\operatorname{ins}_{L}\operatorname{ext}_{v}=L(v).

Now, as
V
is an euclidean vector space, then
\Lambda^{*}V

has an inner product and
\operatorname{ext}^{*}_{v}=\operatorname{ins}_{\langle v,-\rangle}
.

Now define a map

\gamma:V\oplus V\to\operatorname{End}(\Lambda^{*}E),\qquad(v,w)\mapsto\operatorname{ext}_{v+w}+\operatorname{ins}_{\langle v-w,-\rangle}.

Then a direct calculation shows that

\gamma(v,w)^{2}=\lVert v\rVert^{2}-\lVert w\rVert^{2}.

By the universal property of the Clifford algebras, we can continue to an algebra homomorphism

\gamma:\operatorname{Cliff}(V\oplus V,\lVert.\rVert^{2}\oplus(-\lVert.\rVert^{2}))\to\operatorname{End}(\Lambda^{*}V)

to a graded algebra homomorphism (in fact, an isomorphism). Since
i:(V,\lVert.\rVert)\rightarrow(V\oplus V,\lVert.\rVert\oplus(-\lVert.\rVert)),v\mapsto(v,0)
is an isometry, there also is an
algebra homomorphism
c:=\gamma\circ\operatorname{Cliff}(i):\operatorname{Cliff}(V,\lVert.\rVert)\to\operatorname{End}(\Lambda^{*}V)
, which we mention is the one we are after.

∎

We remark that the grading on Clifford algebras actually makes its study easier; as the following lemma shows (see Theorem 1.2.3 of
[
23
]
):

Lemma 9.6
.

Let
(V_{1},\kappa_{1})
and
(V_{2},\kappa_{2})
be finite dimensional pseudo-Euclidean vector spaces. Then the map

\displaystyle f:V_{1}\oplus V_{2}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\operatorname{Cliff}(V_{1},\kappa_{1})\hat{\otimes}\operatorname{Cliff}(V_{2},\kappa_{2})

\displaystyle v_{1}\oplus v_{2}

\displaystyle\mapsto j_{V_{1}}(v_{1})\hat{\otimes}1+1\hat{\otimes}j_{V_{2}}(v_{2})

induces an isomorphism

\operatorname{Cliff}(V_{1}\oplus V_{2},\kappa_{1}\oplus\kappa_{2})\rightarrow\operatorname{Cliff}(V_{1},\kappa_{1})\hat{\otimes}\operatorname{Cliff}(V_{2},\kappa_{2})

We now turn our attention to the complexified Clifford algebras.

Corollary 9.7
.

Let
(V,\kappa)
be a finite dimensional pseudo-Euclidean vector space. Then,
\operatorname{Cliff}^{\mathbb{C}}(V,\kappa):=\operatorname{Cliff}(V,\kappa)\hat{\otimes}_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbf{C}
is a
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-graded Real
C^{*}
-algebra with Real structure given by
\kappa\hat{\otimes}\bar{(.)}
.

For the remainder of this section, we will make the notation a bit more concise by not mentioning the involution
\kappa
.

Definition 9.8
.

Let
V
be a finite dimensional pseudo-Euclidean vector space. Denote by
\mathcal{C}(V)
the
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-graded, Real
C^{*}
-algebra
C_{0}(V,\operatorname{Cliff}^{\mathbb{C}}(V))
, with pointwise grading, and Real structure by combining the Real structures on
V
and
\operatorname{Cliff}^{\mathbb{C}}(V)
as in
Example

2.4
.

Lemma 9.9
.

Let
V
,
W
be involutive finite dimensional Euclidean vector spaces. The map
f_{1}\hat{\otimes}f_{2}\mapsto f
, where
f(v+w)=f_{1}(v)f_{2}(w)
determines an isomorphism

\operatorname{Cliff}^{\mathbb{C}}(V)\hat{\otimes}\operatorname{Cliff}^{\mathbb{C}}(W)\rightarrow\operatorname{Cliff}^{\mathbb{C}}(V\oplus W)

of graded, Real
C^{*}
-algebras.

Proof.

This follows easily from (the complexified version of)
Lemma

9.6
and the isomorphism
C_{0}(V)\hat{\otimes}C_{0}(W)\cong C_{0}(V\oplus W)
.
∎

Definition 9.10
.

Let
V
be a pseudo-Euclidean vector space. Denote by
P:V\rightarrow\operatorname{Cliff}^{\mathbb{C}}(V)
the composite
V\rightarrow\operatorname{Cliff}(V)\rightarrow\operatorname{Cliff}^{\mathbb{C}}(V)
, where the first map is given by
v\mapsto v
, and the second map is
-\hat{\otimes}1
.

Definition 9.11
.

The Bott element
b\in K_{0}^{\operatorname{sp}}(\mathcal{C}(V))
is the
K
-theory class of the
*
-homomorphism
\beta:\mathcal{S}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}(V)
defined by
\beta:f\rightarrow f(P)
.
\beta
is called the Bott map.

The following lemma is needed to show that the Bott map indeed is a class in spectral K-theory.

Lemma 9.12
.

The Bott map commutes with the grading and the Real structure.

Proof.

We first note that the set of all elements
f\in\mathcal{S}
for which
\beta(\varepsilon_{\mathcal{S}}(f))=\varepsilon_{\mathcal{C}(V)}(\beta(f))
is a
C^{*}
-subalgebra of
\mathcal{S}
. Thus, by the Stone-Weierstrass theorem, it suffices to check the condition on the functions
e^{-x^{2}}
and
xe^{-x^{2}}
. But for these, it is obvious in light of their power series expansions. Thus,
\beta
commutes with the grading.

To see that
\beta
commutes with the Real structure, by another Stone-Weierstrass argument, we boil down to investigating it only on the function
f:=\frac{1}{x+i}
. We have to show that
\overline{f}v=\kappa(f(\kappa{v}))\penalty\ \forall\penalty\ v\in V
(here, we think about extending
\kappa

\mathbb{R}
-linearly). The left hand side evaluates to
\frac{1}{v\otimes 1-1\otimes i}
. The right-hand side evaluates to
\kappa(\frac{1}{\kappa v\otimes 1+1\otimes i})=\frac{1}{v\otimes 1-1\otimes i}
, as desired.

∎

9.2
Bott Periodicity - the statement and the formal part

Having given the relevant background on Clifford algebras, we are now in a position to state the Bott periodicity theorem.

Theorem 9.13
.

For every graded
C^{*}
-algebra
A
, and every finite-dimensional pseudo Euclidean vector space
V
, the Bott map

\beta:K_{0}(A)\rightarrow K_{0}(A\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{C}(V)),

defined by
\beta(x)=x\times b
, is an isomorphism of abelian groups.

In the remainder of this subsection, we discuss a trick, originally due to Michael Atiyah, which substantially reduces the workload needed to prove
Theorem

9.13
.

Definition 9.14
.

Let us say that a graded
C^{*}
-algebra
B
has the rotation property
if the automorphism
b_{1}\widehat{\otimes}b_{2}\mapsto(-1)^{\partial b_{1}\partial b_{2}}b_{2}\widehat{\otimes}b_{1}
which
interchanges the two factors in the tensor product
B\widehat{\otimes}B
is homotopic to a tensor product
*
-homomorphism

1\otimes\iota:B\widehat{\otimes}B\to B\widehat{\otimes}B
.

Lemma 9.15
.

Let
(V,\kappa)
be a finite dimensional pseudo-Euclidean vector space. The
C^{*}
-algebra
\mathcal{C}(V)
has the rotation property.

Proof.

Under the isomorphism

\mathcal{C}(V)\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{C}(V)\cong\mathcal{C}(V\oplus V),

the flip isomorphism corresponds to the
*
-automorphism of
\mathcal{C}(V\oplus V)
associated to the map
\tau_{**}
which exchanges the two copies of
V
in the direct sum
V\oplus V
. We claim that
\tau
is homotopic to the map
\xi:V\oplus V\rightarrow V\oplus V,\xi(v_{1},v_{2})=(v_{1},-v_{2})
, through a path of isometric isomorphisms of
V\oplus V
which preserve the involution
\kappa
. This follows by observing that the standard homotopy
\begin{pmatrix}\sin(\frac{\pi}{2}t)&\cos(\frac{\pi}{2}t)\\
\cos(\frac{\pi}{2}t)&-\sin(\frac{\pi}{2}t)\end{pmatrix}
clearly commutes with the involution
\begin{pmatrix}\kappa&0\\
0&\kappa\end{pmatrix}
.
∎

The reason we care about the rotation property is because of the following theorem:

Theorem 9.16
.

Let
B
be a graded
C^{*}
-algebra with the rotation property, and
\mathcal{H}
some graded Real Hilbert space whose even and odd parts are both infinite dimensional.
Suppose there exists a class
b\in K_{0}(B)
and an asymptotic morphism

\alpha:\mathcal{S}\widehat{\otimes}B\to\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H})

with the property that the induced
K
-theory homomorphism
\alpha_{*}:K_{0}(B)\to K_{0}(\mathbf{C})
maps
b
to
1
. Then for every

C^{*}
-algebra
A
the maps,

\alpha_{*}:K_{0}(A\widehat{\otimes}B)\to K_{0}(A)\qquad\text{and}\qquad\beta_{*}:K_{0}(A)\to K_{0}(A\widehat{\otimes}B)

induced by
\alpha
and by multiplication by the
K
-theory class
b

are inverse to one another.

Remark 27
.

Note that in light of
Lemma

8.44
, and since
K_{0}(\mathbf{C})\cong\mathbb{Z}
from classical K-theory, the assumption on
\alpha
is readily seen to be equivalent to saying that the
\alpha\beta:K_{0}(\mathbf{C})\rightarrow K_{0}(\mathbf{C})
is the identity.

Proof.

From
Corollary

7.17
, the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K(C)\otimes K(A\hat{\otimes}B)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K(C\hat{\otimes}A\hat{\otimes}B)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K(C)\otimes K(A)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K(C\hat{\otimes}A)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K\text{-theory product}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\otimes\alpha_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K\text{-theory product}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{*}}

We express this property by saying that
\alpha
is multiplicative, i.e.
\alpha_{*}(x\times y)=x\times\alpha_{*}(y)
. It follows directly from this multiplicativity property of
\alpha
that
\alpha
is a left inverse to
\beta
:

\alpha_{*}(\beta_{*}(x))=\alpha_{*}(x\times b)=x\times\alpha_{*}(b)=x\times 1=x.

It remains to show that
\alpha
is also a right-inverse to
\beta
. To that end, it suffices to show that
\alpha
has a left inverse. To that end, we consider the isomorphisms
\sigma:A\hat{\otimes}B\rightarrow B\hat{\otimes}A
given by flipping, and
\tau:B\hat{\otimes}A\hat{\otimes}B\rightarrow B\hat{\otimes}A\hat{\otimes}B
given by flipping the first and last factors in the tensor products. Note that the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K_{0}(B)\otimes K_{0}(A\hat{\otimes}B)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K_{0}(B\hat{\otimes}A\hat{\otimes}B)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K_{0}(A\hat{\otimes}B)\otimes K_{0}(B)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K_{0}(A\hat{\otimes}B\hat{\otimes}B)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K_{0}(B\hat{\otimes}A)\otimes K_{0}(B)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K_{0}(B\hat{\otimes}A\hat{\otimes}B)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma_{*}\otimes 1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K-\text{theory product}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K-\text{theory product}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{12_{*}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K-\text{theory product}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\text{flip}\penalty\ A\hat{\otimes}B\penalty\ \text{and}\penalty\ B)_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x\otimes y\mapsto y\otimes x}

Indeed, the top rectangle commutes because of
Proposition

7.10

(ii)
, and the bottom one commutes because of
Remark

20
. This yields that,

\sigma_{*}(y)\times z=\tau_{*}(z\times y),\penalty\ \forall\penalty\ y\in K_{0}(A\hat{\otimes}B),z\in K_{0}(B).

As
B
has the rotation property,
\tau
is homotopic to
\iota\hat{\otimes}1\hat{\otimes}1
, where
\iota
is as in
Definition

9.14
.Thus, setting
z=b
above, we obtain:

\sigma_{*}(y)\times b=\tau_{*}(b\times y)=(\iota\hat{\otimes}1\hat{\otimes}1)_{*}(b\times y)=\iota_{*}(b)\times y.

Applying
\alpha_{*}
, we deduce that:

\sigma_{*}(y)=\alpha_{*}(\sigma_{*}(y)\times b)=\alpha_{*}(\iota_{*}(b)\times y)=\iota_{*}(b)\times\alpha_{*}(y)

where for the first and last equalities, we used the multiplicativity of
\alpha
. Applying the flip isomorphism
\sigma
to the above equation, we obtain

y=\alpha_{*}(y)\times\iota_{*}(b).

Thus, multiplication by
\iota_{*}(b)
is a left-inverse to
\alpha
, as desired.
∎

9.3
Inverse to the Bott map

To prove
Theorem

9.13
, it suffices to prove the following theorem in light of
Theorem

9.16
and
Lemma

9.15
:

Theorem 9.17
.

Given any pseudo Euclidean vector space
V
, there exists a Real graded Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}(V)
, and asymtotic morphism
\alpha:\mathcal{S}\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{C}(V)\dashrightarrow\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}(V))
for which the induced homomorphism
\alpha:K_{0}(\mathcal{C}(V))\rightarrow K_{0}(\mathbf{C})
maps the Bott element
b\in K_{0}(\mathcal{C}(V))
to
1\in K_{0}(\mathbf{C})
.

We first come up with the Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}(V)
.

Definition 9.18
.

Let
V
be a finite-dimensional Euclidean vector space. Let us provide the
finite-dimensional linear space underlying the algebra

\operatorname{Cliff}^{\mathbb{C}}(V)
with the Hilbert space structure for which the
monomials
e_{i_{1}}\cdots e_{i_{p}}
(associated to an orthonormal basis of

V
) are orthonormal. The Hilbert space structure so obtained is
independent of the choice of
e_{1},\ldots,e_{n}
12
12

12

strictly speaking, we look at the elements
e_{i}\otimes 1
, but we will refer to
x\otimes 1
in
\operatorname{Cliff}^{\mathbb{C}}(V)
as simply
x
from now on.
. Denote by

\mathcal{H}(V)
the infinite-dimensional complex Hilbert space of
square-integrable
\operatorname{Cliff}^{\mathbb{C}}(V)
-valued functions on
V
, Thus:

\mathcal{H}(V)=L^{2}(V,\operatorname{Cliff}^{\mathbb{C}}(V)).

The Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}(V)
is a graded Hilbert space, with grading
inherited from
\operatorname{Cliff}^{\mathbb{C}}(V)
, and a Real structure combining the ones on
V
and
\operatorname{Cliff}^{\mathbb{C}}(V)
.

To prove this theorem, we need to develop some more machinery:

Definition 9.19
.

Let
V
be a finite-dimensional Euclidean vector space and let
e,f\in V
. Define linear operators on the finite-dimensional graded Hilbert space
underlying
\operatorname{Cliff}^{\mathbb{C}}(V)
by the formulas

\displaystyle e(x)

\displaystyle=e\cdot x

\displaystyle\hat{f}(x)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{\partial x}x\cdot f.

Lemma 9.20
.

The operator
e:\operatorname{Cliff}^{\mathbb{C}}(V)\rightarrow\operatorname{Cliff}^{\mathbb{C}}(V)
is self-adjoint, while the operator
\hat{f}:\operatorname{Cliff}^{\mathbb{C}}(V)\rightarrow\operatorname{Cliff}^{\mathbb{C}}(V)
is skew adjoint. Furthermore,
e
and
\hat{f}
are Real operators.

Proof.

For the proof, it helps to have the point of view on Clifford algebras as used in the proof of
Proposition

9.5
.

Indeed, we have the graded
*
-algebra homomorphism:

\displaystyle c:\operatorname{Cliff}^{\mathbb{C}}(V)

\displaystyle\rightarrow\operatorname{End}(\Lambda^{*}V)

\displaystyle v

\displaystyle\mapsto\operatorname{ext}_{v}+\operatorname{ext}_{v^{*}}.

Then,
\gamma:\operatorname{Cliff}^{\mathbb{C}}(V)\rightarrow\Lambda^{*}V
,
\gamma(v)=c(v).1
is a linear isomorphism. We define the positive normed functional

T:\operatorname{Cliff}^{\mathbb{C}}(V)\rightarrow\mathbb{C},T(x)=\frac{1}{2^{n}}(\operatorname{Tr}(c(x))).

Under these identifications, it is easy to see that the scalar product on
\operatorname{Cliff}^{\mathbb{C}}(V)
is given by
\langle x,y\rangle=T(x^{*}y)
.
As
\operatorname{End}(\Lambda^{*}V)
is inner graded, the properties of trace show that
T(x)=T(\alpha(x))
.
Now, we compute

\langle ex,y\rangle=T((ex)^{*}y)=T(x^{*}e^{*}y){\mathrel{\to}^{e=e^{*}}}T(x^{*}ey)=\langle x,ey\rangle.

and

\displaystyle\langle\hat{f}x,y\rangle

\displaystyle=T((\alpha(x).f)^{*}y)

\displaystyle=T(f^{*}\alpha(x)^{*}y)

\displaystyle=T(\alpha(x)^{*}yf)\penalty\ (\text{since}\penalty\ T(abc)=T(bca),f=f^{*})

\displaystyle=T(\alpha(\alpha(x)^{*})\alpha(y)\alpha(f))\penalty\ (\text{since}\penalty\ T(x)=T(\alpha(x)))

\displaystyle=T(x^{*}\alpha(y)\alpha(f))

\displaystyle=-T(x^{*}\alpha(y)f)\penalty\ (\text{since}\penalty\ \alpha(f)=-f)

\displaystyle=-\langle x,\hat{f}y\rangle

The Reality of
e
and
\hat{f}
follows immediately from the definition.
∎

Definition 9.21
.

Let
V
be a finite-dimensional Euclidean vector space. Denote by

\mathfrak{s}(V)
the dense subspace of
\mathcal{H}(V)
comprised of
Schwartz-class
\operatorname{Cliff}^{\mathbb{C}}(V)
-valued functions:

\mathfrak{s}(V,\operatorname{Cliff}^{\mathbb{C}}(V))=\text{Schwartz-class
}\operatorname{Cliff}^{\mathbb{C}}(V)\text{-valued functions.}

The Dirac operator of
V
is the unbounded operator
D
on

\mathcal{H}(V)
, with domain
\mathfrak{s}(V,\operatorname{Cliff}^{\mathbb{C}}(V))
, defined by

(Df)(v)=\sum_{1}^{n}\hat{e}_{i}\left(\frac{\partial f}{\partial x_{i}}(v)\right),

where
e_{1},\ldots,e_{n}
is an orthonormal basis of
V
and
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
are the corresponding coordinates on
V
.

Definition 9.22
.

Let
V
be a finite dimensional pseudo Euclidean vector space. The Clifford operator is the Real unbounded operator on
\mathcal{H}(V)
, with domain
\mathfrak{s}(V,\operatorname{Cliff}^{\mathbb{C}}(V))
, which is given by the formula

(Cf)(v)=vf(v)

Lemma 9.23
.

The Dirac operator is Real.

Proof.

We can factor the Dirac operator as

\mathfrak{s}(V,\operatorname{Cliff}^{\mathbb{C}}(V))\xrightarrow{\nabla}\mathfrak{s}(V,V\hat{\otimes}\operatorname{Cliff}^{\mathbb{C}}(V))\xrightarrow{C}\mathfrak{s}(V,\operatorname{Cliff}^{\mathbb{C}}(V))

where
\nabla(f)=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\hat{e_{j}}\hat{\otimes}\partial_{j}f
. It now suffices to show that both
C
and
\nabla
are Real operators.

Reality of
C
: It suffices to observe that for an element
x\mapsto v\hat{\otimes}f(x)\in\mathfrak{s}(V,\operatorname{Cliff}^{\mathbb{C}}(V))
, where
v\in V
is fixed, the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x\mapsto v\hat{\otimes}f(x)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x\mapsto vf(x)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x\mapsto\overline{\kappa(v)\hat{\otimes}\kappa f(\kappa x)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x\mapsto\overline{\kappa(v)\kappa f(\kappa(x))}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
Real form
Real form
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}

Reality of
\nabla
: It’s another diagram chase, but a bit more subtle:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\sum_{j=1}^{n}\hat{e_{j}}\hat{\otimes}\partial_{j}f}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x\mapsto\overline{\kappa f(\kappa x)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x\mapsto\sum_{j=1}^{n}\overline{\kappa\hat{e_{j}}\hat{\otimes}\kappa(Df(\kappa x).e_{j})}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x\mapsto\sum_{j=1}^{n}\overline{\hat{e_{j}}\hat{\otimes}\kappa(D(f)(\kappa x).(\kappa e_{j}))}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x\mapsto\sum_{j=1}^{n}\overline{\hat{e_{j}}\hat{\otimes}\frac{d}{dt}\mid_{t=0}(\kappa f(\kappa(x+te_{j})))}}}
Real structure
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nabla}
Real structure
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nabla}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=(\text{since}\penalty\ \kappa\penalty\ \text{is linear; and chain rule})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=(\text{as}\penalty\ \kappa(e_{j})=\pm 1)}

∎

Since the individual
\hat{e}_{i}
are skew-adjoint and since they commute
with the partial derivatives we see that
D
is formally self-adjoint on

\mathfrak{s}(V)
. The following lemma is well known in the theory of differential operators:

Lemma 9.24
.

Let
V
be a finite-dimensional pseudo-Euclidean vector space. The Dirac operator on
V
is closed and essentially self-adjoint. If
f\in\mathcal{S}
, if
h\in\mathcal{C}(V)
and if
M_{h}
is the operator of pointwise multiplication by
h
on the Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}(V)
, then the product
f(D)M_{h}
is a compact operator on
\mathcal{H}(V)
.

Proof.

D
is an essentially self-adjoint operator follows from well known theory of the Dirac operator (
[
27
]
Proposition 8.1.8). Thus, it makes sense to talk about the operator
f(D)
, since there is a functional calculus for self-adjoint operators on a Hilbert space. (
[
27
]
Theorem D.1.7).

We next show that
f(D)M_{h}
is a compact operator. First, take
f(x)=(x\pm i)^{-1}
, and
h\in C_{c}^{\infty}(V,\operatorname{Cliff(V)})
. Some well known characterization of essential self-adjointness yields that
(D\pm i):\mathfrak{s}(V)\rightarrow\mathcal{H}(V)
has dense range. Thus, Rellich lemma and Garding’s inequality (see
[
27
]
) shows that
M_{h}(D\pm i)^{-1}
is a compact operator on
\mathcal{H}(V)
. Thus, by taking adjoints, we get
(D\pm i)^{-1}M_{h}
is compact. We can now see that
(D\pm i)^{-1}M_{h}
is compact for all
h\in\mathcal{C}(V)
, since
C_{c}^{\infty}(V,\operatorname{Cliff(V)})
is dense in
\mathcal{C}(V)
. We are now done by a direct application of the Stone-Weierstrass theorem, as the set of
f\in\mathcal{S}
for which
f(D)M_{h}
is compact, for all
h
, is an ideal in
\mathcal{S}
.
∎

Remark 28
.

From the proof of
Lemma

9.24
, it also follows that
f(t^{-1}D)M_{h_{t}}
is compact where
t\in[1,\infty)
, and
h_{t}(v):=h(t^{-1}v).

Lemma 9.25
.

In the notation of
Lemma

9.24
, for every
f\in\mathcal{S}
and
h\in\mathcal{C}(V)
, we have

\lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}\lVert[f(t^{-1}D),M_{h_{t}}]\rVert=0,

where
[.,.]
denotes the graded commutator.

Proof.

As in the proof of
Lemma

9.24
, a Stone-Weierstrass argument reduces to proving the lemma for the functions
x\mapsto\frac{1}{x^{2}+1}
and
x\mapsto\frac{x}{x^{2}+1}
, and
h
is smooth and compactly supported. We show the computation for
f=\frac{1}{x^{2}+1}
.

\displaystyle\left[f(t^{-1}D),M_{h_{t}}\right]

\displaystyle=\left[(t^{-2}D^{2}+I)^{-1},M_{h_{t}}\right]

\displaystyle=(t^{-2}D^{2}+I)^{-1}M_{h_{t}}\pm M_{h_{t}}(t^{-2}D^{2}+I)^{-1}

\displaystyle=(t^{-2}D^{2}+I)^{-1}M_{h_{t}}(t^{-2}D^{2}+I)(t^{-2}D^{2}+I)^{-1}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\pm(t^{-2}D^{2}+I)^{-1}(t^{-2}D^{2}+I)M_{h_{t}}(t^{-2}D^{2}+I)^{-1}

\displaystyle=(t^{-2}D^{2}+I)^{-1}\left[M_{h_{t}},(t^{-2}D^{2}+I)\right](t^{-2}D+I)^{-1}

\displaystyle=(t^{-2}D^{2}+I)^{-1}\left[M_{h_{t}},t^{-2}D^{2}\right](t^{-2}D+I)^{-1}

\displaystyle=t^{-2}(t^{-2}D+I)^{-1}\left[M_{h_{t}},D^{2}\right](t^{-2}D^{2}+I)^{-1}.

which has norm bounded by
t^{-2}\lVert[M_{h_{t}},D^{2}]\rVert
, and the conclusion follows from here.
∎

Proposition 9.26
.

Upto equivalence, there is a unique asymptotic morphism

\alpha_{t}:\mathcal{S}\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{C}(V)\rightarrow\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}(V))

for which, on elementary tensors,

\alpha_{t}(f\hat{\otimes}h)=f(t^{-1}D)M_{h_{t}}.

Proof.

For
t\in[1,\infty)
, we can define a linear map
\alpha_{t}:\mathcal{S}\hat{\odot}\mathcal{C}(V)\rightarrow\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}(V))
by the formula

\alpha_{t}(f\hat{\otimes}h)=f(t^{-1}D)M_{h_{t}}.

Lemma

9.25
shows that
\alpha_{t}
defines a homomorphism from
\mathcal{S}\hat{\odot}\mathcal{C}(V)
into
\mathfrak{A}(\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}(V)))
. By the universal property of the tensor product, this extends to a
*
-homomorphism defined on
\mathcal{S}\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{C}(V)
.
Remark

28
shows that
f(t^{-1}D)M_{h_{t}}
is compact. So, our
*
-homomorphism actually maps
\mathcal{S}\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{C}(V)
into the subalgebra
\mathfrak{A}(\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}(V)))
, as required.
∎

9.4
The Harmonic Oscillator and the calculations

The
\alpha
constructed in the previous subsection is the asymptotic morphism sought after in
Theorem

9.17
. The only part remaining is thus to show that
\alpha_{*}(b)=1
. We proceed to show this now.

Lemma 9.27
.

Let
V
be a finite dimensional pseudo Euclidean vector space. If
\mathcal{C}(V)
is represented on the Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}(V)
by pointwise multiplication operators then the composition

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{S}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}(V)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{B}(\,\mathcal{H}(V))}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}

maps
f\in\mathcal{S}
to
f(C)\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}(V))
.

Proof.

We do a direct calculation: for
g\in\mathcal{H}(V),v\in V
, we have

\displaystyle(M\beta(f))(g)(v)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle M_{\beta(f)}(g)(v)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\beta(f)(v).g(v)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle f(P)(v).g(v)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle f(v).g(v)

which can be checked to be equal to
f(C)(g)(v)
as well.
∎

We shall compute the composition
\alpha_{*}(b)
by analyzing the following operator:

Definition 9.28
.

Let
V
be a finite-dimensional Euclidean vector space. Define an unbounded operator
B
on
\mathcal{H}(V)
, with domain
\mathfrak{s}(V)
, by the formula

(Bf)(v)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}e_{i}(f(v))+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\widehat{e}_{i}\left(\frac{\partial f}{\partial x_{i}}(v)\right).

Thus,
B=C+D
, where
C
is the Clifford operator and
D
is the Dirac operator.

Remark 29
.

Note that
B=C+D
, where
C
is the Clifford operator and
D
is the Dirac operator. Furthermore, its straightforward to see that
B
maps the Schwartz space
\mathfrak{s}(V)
to itself, whence the operator
H=B^{2}
is defined on
\mathfrak{s}(V)
.

Example 9.29
.

Suppose
V=\mathbb{R}
. Then

B=\begin{pmatrix}0&x-d/dx\\
x+d/dx&0\end{pmatrix},

upon identifying
\mathcal{H}(V)
with
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})\oplus L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
.

Observe that the operator
B
maps the Schwartz space

\mathfrak{s}(V,\operatorname{Cliff}^{\mathbb{C}}(V))
into itself. So the operator
H=B^{2}
is defined
on
\mathfrak{s}(V)
.

Definition 9.30
.

Let
V
be a finite-dimensional Euclidean vector space. Define the number operator on
\operatorname{Cliff}(V)
by

N=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\hat{e_{i}}e_{i},

where
e_{i}
’s form an orthonormal basis of
V
.

Lemma 9.31
.

N
maps the monomial
e_{i_{1}}...e_{i_{p}}
(where
i_{1}<i_{2}<...<i_{p}
) to
(2p-n)e_{i_{1}}...e_{i_{p}}
.

Proof.

We calculate

\displaystyle N(e_{i_{1}}...e_{i_{p}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\hat{e_{i}}e_{i}(e_{i_{1}}...e_{i_{p}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\hat{e_{i}}(e_{i}e_{i_{1}}...e_{i_{p}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}(-1)^{p+1}(e_{i}e_{i_{1}}...e_{i_{p}})e_{i}

\displaystyle=(2p-n)e_{i_{1}}...e_{i_{p}},

as can be readily seen from the Clifford relations.
∎

The following lemma will be useful in calculations:

Lemma 9.32
.

B^{2}=C^{2}+D^{2}+N
, and
N
commutes with
C^{2}
and
D^{2}
.

Proof.

The proof is a purely algebraic calculation with partial derivatives, and is done in detail in Proposition 3.2.13 of
[
4
]
, so we skip the proof here. That
N
commutes with
C^{2}
and
D^{2}
is also obvious from the definitions.
∎

Proposition 9.33
.

Let
V
be a finite dimensional Euclidean vector space of dimension
n
. Then,

1.

the eigenvalues of
B^{2}
are
2m\penalty\ (m=0,1,...)
, and each eigenvalue occurs with finite multiplicity.

2.

B^{2}
has an orthonormal basis of eigenfunctions within
\mathfrak{s}(V)
.

3.

the eigenvalue
0
occurs precisely once, and the corresponding eigenfunction is
e^{-\frac{1}{2}\lVert v\rVert^{2}}.1

Proof.

We first consider the case when
V=\mathbb{R}
. In this case, a direct computation shows that

B^{2}=\begin{pmatrix}x^{2}-\frac{d^{2}}{dx^{2}}-1&0\\
0&x^{2}-\frac{d^{2}}{dx^{2}}+1\end{pmatrix}.

Thus, it suffices to show the following:

Claim
.

There is an orthonormal basis of eigenvectors for the operator

H=x^{2}-\frac{d^{2}}{dx^{2}}.

defined on the Schwarz subspace of
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
, whose eigenvalues are all odd positive integers, and each eigenvalue has multiplicity one.

Proof.

Set
L=x+\frac{d}{dx}
,
R=x-\frac{d}{dx}
. Also, set
f_{1}(x)=e^{-\frac{1}{2}x^{2}}
. A direct calculation shows that

(RL)(f)(x)=x^{2}f(x)-f^{\prime\prime}(x)-f(x),(LR)(f)(x)=x^{2}f(x)-f^{\prime\prime}(x)+f(x),

implying that
H=RL+I=LR-I
. Also, its direct to check that
Lf_{1}=0
, whence
H(f_{1})=f_{1}
, implying
f_{1}
is in the
1
-eigenspace of
B^{2}
. A direct calculation shows that
HR=RH+2R
, and so by induction,
HR^{n}=R^{n}H+2nR^{n}
. Thus, by setting
f_{n+1}:=R^{n}f_{1}
, we get
H(f_{n+1})=(2n+1)f_{n+1}
. Also, note that
f_{n+1}
is a non-zero polynomial of degree
n
times
f_{1}
. Thus,
\operatorname{span}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\{f_{n}\}=\operatorname{span}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\{x^{n}e^{\frac{-x^{2}}{2}}\}
, and the latter is known to be dense in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
(
[
19
]
). Also, the functions
f_{n}
are orthogonal, being eigenfunctions corresponding to distinct eigenvalues of a symmetric operator. So, we conclude that
\{\frac{f_{n}}{{\lVert f_{n}\rVert}_{2}}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is an orthonormal basis of eigenfunctions of
B^{2}
, the eigenvalues of
H
are exactly the odd positive integers, each having multiplicity one.
∎

The general case follows by noting that
B^{2}
is the tensor product of the restrictions of
B^{2}
onto the span of each of the orthonormal basis vectors of
V
.

∎

Corollary 9.34
.

Let
V
be a finite dimensional Euclidean vector space. Then,

1.

B is essentially self-adjoint

2.

The eigenvalues of
B
are
\pm\sqrt{2m}\penalty\ (m=0,1,...)
, and the eigenspace associated to each is finite dimensional.

3.

B
has an orthonormal basis of eigenfunctions within
\mathfrak{s}(V)
, and has compact resolvent.

4.

The kernel of
B
is one-dimensional and is generated by the function
e^{-\frac{1}{2}\lVert v\rVert^{2}}

Proof.

1.

Note that
B
is formally self-adjoint operator, by being the sum of the (essentially) self-adjoint operators
C
and
D
. That
B
is essentially self adjoint now follows from Theorem 2.2 of
[
24
]
.

2.

Note that, if
v
is an eigenfunction of
B^{2}
with eigenvalue
\lambda
, then

B^{2}(Bv)=B(B^{2}v)=B(\lambda v)=\lambda Bv,

implying
B
leaves the eigenspaces corresponding to every eigenvalue of
B^{2}
invariant. By
Proposition

9.33
, these eigenspaces are finite dimensional, whence
B
, viewed as an operator on the eigenspace corresponding to
2m
, has an eigenvalue by elementary linear algebra which has to be a square root of
2m
(
m\geq 0
). As
B
is odd, the eigenvalues are symmetric about the origin, and the conclusion follows.

3.

That
B
has an orthonormal basis consisting of eigenfunction now follows from the corresponding fact about
B^{2}
. That
B
has compact resolvents also follows as the absolute value of the eigenvalues of
B
tend to infinity.

4.

This follows immediately from the corresponding result about
B^{2}
, and the fact that
B
leaves the eigenspaces corresponding to every eigenvalue of
B^{2}
invariant.

∎

Our next goal is to show:

Theorem 9.35
.

The composition of
\alpha_{t}
and
\beta
is given by:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{S}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{S}\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{S}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{S}\hat{\otimes}C_{0}(V,\operatorname{Cliff}(V))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}(V))}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}\hat{\otimes}\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{t}}

is asymptotically equivalent to the asymptotic morphism
\gamma_{t}:\mathcal{S}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{K}(H)
defined by

\gamma_{t}(f)=f(t^{-1}B),

for all
t\geq 1
.

For the proof, we need some knowledge of some well known results from unbounded operator theory:

Proposition 9.36
.

[Mehler’s formula]
For the Clifford operator
C
and the Dirac operator
D
defined
above we have the following identities for
s>0

e^{-s(C^{2}+D^{2})}=e^{-\frac{1}{2}s_{1}C^{2}}e^{-s_{2}D^{2}}e^{-\frac{1}{2}s_{1}C^{2}}\quad\text{and}\quad e^{-s(C^{2}+D^{2})}=e^{-\frac{1}{2}s_{1}D^{2}}e^{-s_{2}C^{2}}e^{-\frac{1}{2}s_{1}D^{2}}

with

s_{1}=\frac{\cosh(2s)-1}{\sinh(2s)}\qquad\text{and}\qquad s_{2}=\frac{\sinh(2s)}{2}

Proof.

See
[
27
]
Theorem D.3.6 and Corollary D.3.7 for example.
∎

We also note the asymptotic conditions:

Lemma 9.37
.

For an unbounded self-adjoint operator
X
we have the following:

\lim_{t\to\infty}\lVert{e^{-\frac{1}{2}\tau_{1}X^{2}}-e^{-\frac{1}{2}t^{-2}X^{2}}}\rVert=0

\lim_{t\to\infty}\lVert{e^{-\frac{1}{2}\tau_{2}X^{2}}-e^{-\frac{1}{2}t^{-2}X^{2}}}\rVert=0

\lim_{t\to\infty}\lVert{t^{-1}Xe^{-\frac{1}{2}\tau_{1}X^{2}}-t^{-1}Xe^{-\frac{1}{2}t^{-2}X^{2}}}\rVert=0

\lim_{t\to\infty}\lVert{t^{-1}Xe^{-\frac{1}{2}\tau_{2}X^{2}}-t^{-1}Xe^{-\frac{1}{2}t^{-2}X^{2}}}\rVert=0

where

\tau_{1}=\frac{\cosh(2t^{-2})-1}{\sinh(2t^{-2})}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\tau_{2}=\frac{\sinh(2t^{-2})}{2}

Proof.

In light of the spectral theorem, it suffices to prove these when we replace
X
by a real variable
x
, and the operator norm is replaced by the supremum norm on
C_{0}(\mathbb{R})
. Then, all the limits become an easy exercise in calculus.
∎

With the relevant background mentioned, we are ready to proceed. We need another lemma, whose proof is similar to
Lemma

9.25
.

Lemma 9.38
.

Let
f,g\in C_{0}(\mathbb{R})
, then

\lim_{t\to\infty}\lVert{[f(t^{-1}C),g(t^{-1}D)]}\rVert=0

Lemma 9.39
.

For operators
C
and
D
defined before, we have

\displaystyle e^{-t^{-2}B^{2}}

\displaystyle\sim_{\text{asy}}e^{-t^{-2}C^{2}}e^{-t^{-2}D^{2}}

\displaystyle t^{-1}Be^{-t^{-2}B^{2}}

\displaystyle\sim_{\text{asy}}t^{-1}(C+D)e^{-t^{-2}C^{2}}e^{-t^{-2}D^{2}}

Proof.

From
Lemma

9.32
, we conclude that

e^{-t^{-2}B^{2}}=e^{-{t^{-2}}(C^{2}+D^{2})}e^{-t^{-2}N},

and therefore,
Proposition

9.36
implies that

e^{-t^{-2}B^{2}}=e^{-\frac{1}{2}\tau_{1}C^{2}}e^{-\tau_{2}D^{2}}e^{-\frac{1}{2}\tau_{1}C^{2}}e^{-t^{-2}N},

where
\tau_{1}
and
\tau_{2}
are as in
Lemma

9.37
. It follows from
Lemma

9.37
that

e^{-t^{-2}B^{2}}\sim e^{-\frac{1}{2}t^{-2}C^{2}}e^{-t^{-2}D^{2}}e^{-\frac{1}{2}t^{-2}C^{2}}e^{-t^{-2}N},

whence
Lemma

9.38
shows that

e^{-t^{-2}B^{2}}\sim e^{-t^{-2}C^{2}}e^{-t^{-2}D^{2}},

since the operators
e^{-t^{-2}N}
converge in norm to the identity operator.

The other proof is similar.
∎

Remark 30
.

The statements of the last lemma still hold good if we swap
C
and
D
.

We now prove
Theorem

9.35
; it is also covered in
[
4
]
, and given here for convenience.

Proof of
Theorem

9.35
.

Since
\mathcal{S}
is generated by
u
and
v
, it suffices to check that

\alpha(\operatorname{id}\hat{\otimes}\beta)(\Delta(f))\sim_{\text{asy}}\gamma_{t}(f),

for
f=u
and
f=v
.

For
f=u
,

\displaystyle\alpha(\operatorname{id}\hat{\otimes}\beta)(\Delta(u))

\displaystyle=\alpha(\operatorname{id}\hat{\otimes}\beta)(u\hat{\otimes}u)

\displaystyle=\alpha(u\hat{\otimes}\beta(u))

\displaystyle=\alpha(u\hat{\otimes}u(C))

\displaystyle=u(t^{-1}D)M_{u(C)_{t}}

\displaystyle=u(t^{-1}D)u(t^{-1}C)

\displaystyle=e^{-t^{-2}D^{2}}e^{-t^{-2}C^{2}}

and

\gamma_{t}(u)=u(t^{-1}B)=e^{-t^{-2}B^{2}},

and these are both asymptotically equivalent by
Lemma

9.39
.

For
f=v
,

\displaystyle\alpha(\operatorname{id}\hat{\otimes}\beta)(\Delta(u))

\displaystyle=\alpha(\operatorname{id}\hat{\otimes}\beta)(u\hat{\otimes}v+v\hat{\otimes}u)

\displaystyle=\alpha(u\hat{\otimes}\beta(v)+v\hat{\otimes}\beta(u))

\displaystyle=\alpha(u\hat{\otimes}v(C)+v\hat{\otimes}u(C))

\displaystyle\sim_{\text{asy}}\alpha(u\hat{\otimes}v(C))+\alpha(v\hat{\otimes}u(C))

\displaystyle=u(t^{-1}D)M_{v(C)_{t}}+v(t^{-1}D)M_{u(C)_{t}}

\displaystyle=u(t^{-1}D)v(t^{-1}C)+v(t^{-1}D)u(t^{-1}C)

\displaystyle=e^{-t^{-2}D^{2}}t^{-1}Ce^{-t^{-2}C^{2}}+t^{-1}De^{-t^{-2}D^{2}}e^{-t^{-2}C^{2}}

\displaystyle=t^{-1}(C+D)e^{-t^{-2}C^{2}}e^{-t^{-2}D^{2}}

and

\gamma_{t}(v)=v(t^{-1}B)=t^{-1}Be^{-t^{-2}B^{2}}

Then by
Lemma

9.39
these are asymptotically equivalent.
Hence the composition of
\alpha
and
\beta
is asymptotically
equivalent to
\gamma_{t}
.
∎

We finally are able to prove
Theorem

9.13
.

Corollary 9.40
.

The composition
\alpha_{*}\beta_{*}
of the induced homomorphisms

\beta_{*}:K_{0}(\mathbf{C})\to K_{0}(C_{0}(V,\operatorname{Cliff}(V)))

and

\alpha_{*}:K_{0}(C_{0}(V,\operatorname{Cliff}(V)))\to K_{0}(\mathbf{C})

is the identity homomorphism.

Proof.

By
Theorem

9.35
, the composition of
\alpha_{*}\beta_{*}
is
equivalent to the asymptotic morphism
\gamma
given by

\gamma_{t}(f)=f(t^{-1}B)
. Since
f
is in
\mathcal{S}
, each

\gamma_{t}
is a
*
-homomorphism, and the asymptotic morphism
\gamma
is homotopic to the

*
-homomorphism mapping
f
to
f(B)
. (
Proposition

7.16
(iii)). Now, in light of
Corollary

8.43
, it suffices to define
a homotopy between the functional calculus of
B
,
\Phi_{B}:\mathcal{S}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}(V))
and the map
\theta:\mathcal{S}\to\mathcal{K}(\mathcal{H}(V))
defined by
\theta(f)=f(0)p
where
p

is a rank 1 projection (by Corollary 3.2.15) onto the kernel of

B
. But this we have already done in the proof of
Lemma

2.15
.
∎

9.5
The Periodicity Results

Finally, we show the well known
2
-fold and
8
-fold periodicity of complex and Real
K
-theory. The advantage of working with Clifford algebras now comes handy; these periodicity results follow from well-known periodicity of Clifford algebras (see Theorem 1.2.6 of
[
23
]
.)
Before, proceeding, we set up some notation.

Definition 9.41
.

Let
\mathbb{R}^{p,q}
denote the pseudo-Euclidean vector space
\mathbb{R}^{p+q}
, with the involution
\kappa_{p,q}
given by
\kappa_{p,q}(x_{1},x_{2},...,x_{p+q})=(-x_{1},-x_{2},...,-x_{p},x_{p+1},x_{p+2},...,x_{p+q})
. Set
Cl_{p,q}:=\operatorname{Cliff}(\mathbb{R}_{p,q},\kappa_{p,q})
, and
\mathbf{C}l_{p,q}:=\operatorname{Cliff}^{\mathbb{C}}(\mathbb{R}_{p,q},\kappa_{p,q})
.

Proposition 9.42
.

Let
A
be a
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-graded
C^{*}
-algebra. Then, for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, there is a natural isomorphism between
K_{0}(A\hat{\otimes}\mathbf{C}l_{n,0})
and
K_{n}(A)
.

Proof.

We have the following series of isomorphisms, with each one being natural:

\displaystyle K_{0}(A\hat{\otimes}\mathbf{C}l_{n,0})

\displaystyle\cong K_{0}(A\hat{\otimes}\mathbf{C}l_{n,0}\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{C}(\mathbf{C}l_{0,n}))\penalty\ \text{( \lx@cref{creftype~refnum}{thm: BP}})

\displaystyle\cong K_{0}(A\hat{\otimes}\mathbf{C}l_{n,0}\hat{\otimes}C_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\hat{\otimes}\mathbf{C}l_{0,n})\penalty\ \text{(since $\kappa_{0,n}=1$)}

\displaystyle\cong K_{0}(A\hat{\otimes}C_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\hat{\otimes}\mathbf{C}l_{n,0}\hat{\otimes}\mathbf{C}l_{0,n})\penalty\ \text{(flip isomorphism)}

\displaystyle\cong K_{0}(A\hat{\otimes}C_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\hat{\otimes}\mathbf{C}l_{n,n})\penalty\ \text{(\lx@cref{creftype~refnum}{lem: tensor product Cliff})}

\displaystyle\cong K_{0}(A\hat{\otimes}C_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\hat{\otimes}M_{2^{n}}(\mathbf{C}))

\displaystyle\cong K_{0}(A\hat{\otimes}C_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))\penalty\ \text{(K-theory stability)}

\displaystyle\cong K_{n}(A)\penalty\ \text{(\lx@cref{creftype~refnum}{higher K-group})}.

∎

Corollary 9.43
.

Let
A
be a
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-graded
C^{*}
-algebra. If
A
is complex, then there are natural isomorphisms
K_{n}(A)\cong K_{n+2}(A)
, and if
A
is Real, then there are natural isomorphisms
K_{n}(A)\cong K_{n+8}(A)
.

Proof.

This follows from the periodicity properties of Real and complex Clifford algebras, and K-theory stability. Indeed, we have
\mathbf{C}l_{8,0}\cong M_{16}(\mathbf{C})
as Real
C^{*}
-algebras. In the complex case, we have
\mathbf{C}l_{2,0}\cong M_{2}(\mathbf{C})
as complex
C^{*}
-algebras.
∎
